A complete classification of the zero-dimensional homogeneous spaces under determinacy Thanks: The author acknowledges the support of the FWF grants P 35655-N and P 35588-N
Andrea Medini

Abstract
. All spaces are assumed to be separable and metrizable. We give a complete classification of the zero-dimensional homogeneous spaces, under the Axiom of Determinacy. This classification is expressed in terms of topological complexity (in the sense of Wadge theory) and Baire category. In the same spirit, we also give a complete classification of the filters on ω \omega up to homeomorphism. As byproducts, we obtain purely topological characterizations of the semifilters and filters on ω \omega . The Borel versions of these results are in almost all cases due to Fons van Engelen. Along the way, we obtain Wadge-theoretic results of independent interest, especially regarding closure properties.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 54H05, 03E15, 03E60.

Chapter 1
Introduction

Throughout this article, unless we specify otherwise, we will be working in the theory
\mathsf{ZF}+\mathsf{DC}
, that is, the usual axioms of Zermelo-Fraenkel (without the Axiom of Choice) plus the principle of Dependent Choices (see Section
2.2
for more details). By
space
we will mean separable metrizable topological space, unless we specify otherwise. In this chapter, we will give an overview of our results and some of the history that led up to them.

1.1.
History

We begin with the notion that motivates the research contained in this monograph. Intuitively, a space is homogeneous if all of its points “look alike.”

Definition 1.1
.

A space
X
is
homogeneous
if for every
(x,y)\in X\times X
there exists a homeomorphism
h:X\longrightarrow X
such that
h(x)=y
.

Homogeneity is a classical notion in topology, which has been studied in depth (see the survey
[
AvM
]
). Every topological group is homogeneous. However, even among the zero-dimensional Borel spaces, the converse implication does not hold (see Corollary
2.18
and the proof of Proposition
10.10
).

Definition 1.2
.

Given a space
Z
, we will say that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is a
Wadge class
in
Z
if there exists
A\subseteq Z
such that

\mathbf{\Gamma}=\{f^{-1}[A]:f:Z\longrightarrow Z\text{ is a continuous function}\}.

The systematic study of these classes, founded by William Wadge in his doctoral thesis
[
Wa1
]
(see also
[
Wa2
]
), is known as Wadge theory, and it has become a classical topic in descriptive set theory (see
[
Ke
, Section 21.E]
). Under the Axiom of Determinacy, the collection of all Wadge classes on a zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
, ordered by inclusion, constitutes a well-behaved hierarchy that is similar to, but much finer than the well-known Borel hierarchy (and not limited to sets of low complexity). Furthermore, as we will discuss in Section
9.1
, when
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is a good Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
(see Definition
7.8
), it will make sense to talk about spaces of
exact complexity

\mathbf{\Gamma}
. This concept will feature prominently in the statements (and proofs) of our main results.

In his remarkable doctoral thesis
[
vE3
]
(see also
[
vE1
]
and
[
vE2
]
), Fons van Engelen pioneered the application of Wadge theory to topology by giving a complete classification of the zero-dimensional homogeneous Borel spaces. This classification can be split into three cases. The trivial case is that of the locally compact spaces, since the only zero-dimensional homogeneous ones are the non-empty discrete spaces,
2^{\omega}
, and
\omega\times 2^{\omega}
(see Proposition
2.14
). The next case is that of the low complexity spaces (more precisely, of complexity below
\Delta(\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}))
) that are not locally compact. The simplest examples of this kind are
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathbb{Q}\times 2^{\omega}
. This case can be viewed as a unified generalization of a series of classical results that characterize certain “famous” spaces (these results are neatly collected in
[
vE1
, Theorem 2.4]
). The final case is that of the high complexity spaces (more precisely, of complexity above
\Delta(\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}))
). It is only in this case that Wadge theory becomes relevant. In fact, the crucial consequence of the high complexity assumption is that it ultimately enabled van Engelen (as it will enable us) to apply a theorem of Steel, and the proof of this implication follows a deeply Wadge-theoretic path.

1.2.
Our results

The aim of this article is to extend van Engelen’s classification beyond the Borel realm, under suitable determinacy assumptions. Naturally, our focus will be on the high complexity case, as the low complexity cases have already been exhaustively treated elsewhere (see
[
vE1
]
or
[
vE3
, Chapters 2 and 3]
). In particular, under the Axiom of Determinacy, our results will yield a classification of
all
zero-dimensional homogeneous spaces. Not surprisingly, this classification can be split into a uniqueness result and an existence result, outlined as follows (see Theorem
9.18
for a more precise formulation), where we assume that all spaces are zero-dimensional and
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is a
good
Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
. This assumption does not result in any loss of generality, since every zero-dimensional homogeneous space of high enough complexity has exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
for some good Wadge class
\mathbf{\Gamma}
in
\omega^{\omega}
(see Theorem
9.7
). We also remark that every homogeneous space is either meager or Baire (see Proposition
2.21
). The uniqueness theorem was essentially proved in
[
CMM1
]
(see Section
9.3
for more details).

We hope that the reader will not be too irritated by the fact that we have not yet defined most of the relevant notions, as it would not be feasible to do that here. Even so, we believe that stating our main results early will be useful in guiding the reader through this article. We will only say that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})
and
t(\mathbf{\Gamma})
respectively denote the
level
and
type
of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. We also remark that at the end of this monograph we give a list of symbols and terminology, which indicates the sections in which they are defined.

The uniqueness theorem

Up to homeomorphism, there exist at most two homogeneous spaces of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
: at most one of them is Baire, and at most one of them is meager.

The existence theorem

\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the

\mathbf{\Gamma}
does not have the

separation property

separation property

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq 2

There exist both Baire and meager

or

homogeneous spaces

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=1
and
t(\mathbf{\Gamma})=3

of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}

There exist only Baire

There exist only meager

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=1
and
t(\mathbf{\Gamma})\in\{1,2\}

homogeneous spaces

homogeneous spaces

of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}

of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}

It will naturally follow from our proof of the above classification result that every zero-dimensional homogeneous space (with the trivial exception of locally compact spaces) is homeomorphic to a semifilter on
\omega
(see Theorem
9.19
). Furthermore, building on the above classification, we will be able to determine precisely for which good Wadge classes
\mathbf{\Gamma}
in
\omega^{\omega}
there exists a filter on
\omega
of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
(see Theorem
10.4
). This will allow us to obtain a purely topological characterization of the filters on
\omega
(see Theorem
10.5
). The Borel versions of all the results concerning filters were obtained by van Engelen in
[
vE5
]
.

Along the way, we will obtain several Wadge-theoretic results of independent interest, culminating with the closure properties of Section
7.5
. Even in the case of “folklore” or otherwise known results, we believe that the statements given here will improve on the existing literature in two ways. First, we will generally state our results for uncountable zero-dimensional Polish spaces instead of just
\omega^{\omega}
. Second, we will give level-by-level statements with respect to the determinacy assumptions (see Section
2.2
). As noteworthy examples, consider Theorem
5.8
and the results of Section
6.3
.

In many cases, the generalization of a Borel result under suitable determinacy assumptions is trivial to achieve (simply play the same game, while the determinacy assumptions will guarantee that the proof applies to a wider class of payoff sets). The reason why this is not the case in the present context is that van Engelen’s results are fundamentally tied to Louveau’s analysis of the Borel Wadge classes from
[
Lo1
]
, and extending this very fine analysis to all Wadge classes does not seem to be feasible.
1
1

1

Partial results in this direction have been obtained in
[
Fo
]
, but they are limited to very low levels of the projective hierarchy.
Luckily, the coarser analysis given in
[
Lo2
]
(as developed and generalized in
[
CMM2
]
) provides an adequate foundation for our purposes. As a most welcome side-effect, our results will not only generalize van Engelen’s, but often have simpler proofs and simpler statements.

The exposition is self-contained, except for the proofs of fundamental Wadge-theoretic results (for which we refer to
[
CMM2
]
), for results pertaining to the low complexity case (for which we refer to
[
vE3
]
,
[
vE5
]
and
[
Me4
]
), and for classical results (for which we refer to
[
En
]
,
[
Je
]
and
[
Ke
]
).

1.3.
Organization of the paper

In Chapter
2
, we will establish our general terminology and notation, and collect several basic results concerning determinacy, the Baire property, filters, semifilters, and homogeneity properties. In Chapter
3
, we will give a concise introduction to Wadge theory. As we mentioned above, we will heavily reference
[
CMM2
]
, although we have tried to make our presentation accessible to readers who are not familiar with this article.

In Chapter
4
, we will discuss Hausdorff operations and universal sets, mostly as a tool to construct new Wadge classes (see Corollary
4.16
). The intended application is to separated unions (see
6.6
). As a bonus, we will show how Hausdorff operations can clarify the notions of level and expansion introduced in Chapter
3
.

In Chapter
5
, we will discuss the separation property and show that exactly one Wadge class in every non-selfdual pair possesses this property (see Theorem
5.8
). In Chapter
6
, we will extend Louveau’s notion of type beyond the Borel context, introduce separated unions, then use them to characterize the classes of small type (see Theorem
6.11
). These results will be applied in Chapter
7
, in order to obtain certain closure properties. For our purposes, the most important results of this kind are those regarding good Wadge classes (see Corollary
7.13
) and those that link the level, the type and the separation property with closure under separated unions (see Theorems
7.19
and
7.20
).

In Chapter
8
, we will give a complete proof of a theorem due to Steel (see Theorem
8.9
) which will ultimately provide the homeomorphisms needed to obtain our classification results. An interesting feature of this chapter is that is does not require any determinacy assumptions.

In Chapter
9
, we will employ the above results to finally obtain the classification of the zero-dimensional homogeneous spaces. In particular, the closure properties of good Wadge classes will allow us to apply Steel’s Theorem
8.9
, in order to obtain the uniqueness part of the classification (see Theorem
9.9
). On the other hand, the results on closure under separated unions will allow us to construct the desired homogeneous spaces (see Corollaries
9.14
and
9.15
). This chapter begins with a theory of complexity for zero-dimensional spaces, which will enable us to give natural-sounding statements of our classification results (in fact, we have already done so above). This seems to be another worthwhile improvement with respect to van Engelen’s presentation.

In Chapter
10
, we will obtain a classification of the filters on
\omega
up to homeomorphism (see Theorem
10.4
). For this purpose, we will employ the notion of closure under squares and prove a further existence result (see Theorem
10.3
). Finally, we will discuss the relation between filters and topological groups, including a conjecture that would yield a complete classification of the zero-dimensional topological groups up to homeomorphism (see Conjecture
10.8
).

We conclude this section with a diagram that highlights the most significant logical dependencies among the main topics of this article.

Chapter 2
Preliminaries

In this chapter, we will establish much of the terminology and notation to be employed in the remainder of this monograph (with the exception of Wadge theory). We will give basic facts concerning determinacy, the Baire property, filters, semifilters and homogeneity. Furthermore, we will discuss how these notions interact with each other (see for example the diagram in Section
2.4
).

2.1.
Terminology and notation

Our reference for basic set theory will be
[
Je
]
. Let
\Omega
be a set. We will denote by
\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
the collection consisting of all subsets of
\Omega
. When
\Omega
is a countably infinite set, we will identify every element of
\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
with its characteristic function, so that every subset of
\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
will inherit the subspace topology from
2^{\Omega}
. We will denote by
\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)
the collection of all finite subsets of
\Omega
, and by
\mathsf{Cof}(\Omega)
the collection of all cofinite subsets of
\Omega
.

Given
s\in\Omega^{m}
and
t\in\Omega^{n}
, where
m<\omega
and
n\leq\omega
, we will denote by
s^{\frown}t\in\Omega^{m+n}
the
concatenation
of
s
and
t
, that is the function defined by setting

(s^{\frown}t)(i)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}s(i)&\text{if }i<m,\\
t(i-m)&\text{if }m\leq i<m+n,\end{array}\right.

for
i<m+n
. We will often write
i^{\frown}t
or
s^{\frown}i
instead of
(i)^{\frown}t
or
s^{\frown}(i)
respectively. We will use the notation
\Omega^{<\omega}=\bigcup_{n<\omega}\Omega^{n}
and
\Omega^{\leq\omega}=\bigcup_{n\leq\omega}\Omega^{n}
. Given
s\in\Omega^{<\omega}
, we will use the notation
\mathsf{N}_{s}=\{z\in\Omega^{\omega}:s\subseteq z\}
.

Next, assume that
\sigma:\Omega^{<\omega}\longrightarrow\Omega^{<\omega}
is given. Recall that
\sigma
is
monotone
if
\sigma(s)\subseteq\sigma(t)
whenever
s,t\in\Omega^{<\omega}
and
s\subseteq t
. We will say that
\sigma
is
eventually growing
if
|\sigma(z\upharpoonright n)|\to\infty
for every
z\in\Omega^{\omega}
. When
\sigma
is monotone and eventually growing, it makes sense to define
f_{\sigma}:\Omega^{\omega}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\omega}
by setting

f_{\sigma}(z)=\bigcup\{\sigma(z\upharpoonright n):n\in\omega\}

for
z\in\Omega^{\omega}
. For a proof of the following simple fact, see
[
Ke
, Proposition 2.6]
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
\Omega
be a discrete space, and let
f:\Omega^{\omega}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\omega}
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

f
is continuous,

•

f=f_{\sigma}
for some monotone and eventually growing
\sigma:\Omega^{<\omega}\longrightarrow\Omega^{<\omega}
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow Y
. Given
S\subseteq X
, we will use the notation
f[S]=\{f(x):x\in S\}
. Given
S\subseteq Y
, we will use the notation
f^{-1}[S]=\{x\in X:f(x)\in S\}
. We will use the notation
\mathsf{dom}(f)=X
for the
domain
of
f
, and
\mathsf{ran}(f)=f[X]
for the
range
of
f
. When
X=Y
and
f(x)=x
for every
x\in X
, we will use the notation
\mathsf{id}_{X}=f
for the
identity
on
X
. Recall that
f
is a
retraction
if
f
is continuous,
Y\subseteq X
and
f\upharpoonright Y=\mathsf{id}_{Y}
.

Our reference for topology will be
[
En
]
. A subset of a space is
clopen
if it is closed and open. A
base
for a space
X
is a collection
\mathcal{B}
consisting of non-empty open subsets of
X
such that for every
x\in X
and every non-empty open neighborhood
U
of
x
there exists
V\in\mathcal{B}
such that
x\in V\subseteq U
. A space is
zero-dimensional
if it is non-empty and it has a base consisting of clopen sets.
1
1

1

The empty space has dimension
-1
(see
[
En
, Section 7.1]
).
A subset
S
of a space
X
is
codense
if
X\setminus S
is dense. A subset
S
of a space
X
is
meager
if there exist closed nowhere dense subsets
C_{n}
of
X
for
n\in\omega
such that
S\subseteq\bigcup_{n\in\omega}C_{n}
. A subset
S
of a space
X
is
comeager
if
X\setminus S
is meager. A space is
meager
if it is a meager subset of itself. A space
X
is
Baire
if every non-empty open subset of
X
is non-meager. Let
X
and
Y
be spaces. We will write
X\approx Y
to mean that
X
and
Y
are homeomorphic. An
embedding
is a function
j:X\longrightarrow Y
such that
j:X\longrightarrow j[X]
is a homeomorphism. We will say that an embedding is
dense
if it has dense range. A function
f:X\longrightarrow Y
is
closed
if
f[C]
is a closed subset of
Y
whenever
C
is a closed subset of
X
. Similarly define an
open
function. The straightforward proof of the following result is left to the reader.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
Z
and
W
be spaces, and let
f:Z\longrightarrow W
be a closed function. Assume that
X\subseteq Z
and
Y\subseteq W
are such that
f[X]\subseteq Y
and
f[Z\setminus X]\subseteq W\setminus Y
. Then
f\upharpoonright X:X\longrightarrow Y
is closed.

Let
X
be a metric space, and let
\mathsf{d}
denote the metric on
X
. Given
x\in X
and
\varepsilon>0
, we will use the notation
\mathsf{B}(x,\varepsilon)=\{z\in X:\mathsf{d}(z,x)<\varepsilon\}
for the
open ball
of radius
\varepsilon
around
x
. Given
S\subseteq X
, we will use the notation
\mathsf{diam}(S)=\mathsf{sup}\{\mathsf{d}(x,y):x,y\in S\}
for the
diameter
of
S
. Given
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)
, we will use the notation
\mathsf{mesh}(\mathcal{S})=\mathsf{sup}\{\mathsf{diam}(S):S\in\mathcal{S}\}
for the
mesh
of
\mathcal{S}
. A metric on a space is
compatible
if it induces the topology of the space.

Our reference for descriptive set theory will be
[
Ke
]
. Given a space
Z
, we will denote by
\mathsf{Borel}(Z)
the collection of all Borel subsets of
Z
, and by
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
,
\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1}(Z)
and
\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(Z)
respectively the collections of all open, closed and clopen subsets of
Z
. A space
Z
is a
Borel space
if there exists a Polish space
W
and an embedding
j:Z\longrightarrow W
such that
j[Z]\in\mathsf{Borel}(W)
. In particular, every Polish space is a Borel space. By proceeding as in the proof of
[
MZ1
, Proposition 4.2]
, it is easy to show that a space
Z
is Borel iff
j[Z]\in\mathsf{Borel}(W)
for every Polish space
W
and every embedding
j:Z\longrightarrow W
. Given
1\leq\xi<\omega_{1}
and spaces
Z
and
W
, a function
f:Z\longrightarrow W
is
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}
-measurable
if
f^{-1}[U]\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(Z)
for every
U\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(W)
. A function
f:Z\longrightarrow W
is
Borel
if
f^{-1}[U]\in\mathsf{Borel}(Z)
for every
U\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(W)
.

The classes defined below constitute the so-called
difference hierarchy
(or
small Borel sets
). For a detailed treatment, see
[
Ke
, Section 22.E]
or
[
vE3
, Chapter 3]
. Here, we will only mention that the classes
\mathsf{D}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(Z))
are among the simplest concrete examples of Wadge classes (see Section
3.1
).

Definition 2.3

(Kuratowski)
.

Let
Z
be a space, let
1\leq\eta<\omega_{1}
, and let
1\leq\xi<\omega_{1}
. Given a sequence of sets
(A_{\mu}:\mu<\eta)
, define

\mathsf{D}_{\eta}(A_{\mu}:\mu<\eta)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\bigcup\{A_{\mu}\setminus\bigcup_{\zeta<\mu}A_{\zeta}:\mu<\eta\text{ and }\mu\text{ is odd}\}&\text{if }\eta\text{ is even,}\\
\bigcup\{A_{\mu}\setminus\bigcup_{\zeta<\mu}A_{\zeta}:\mu<\eta\text{ and }\mu\text{ is even}\}&\text{if }\eta\text{ is odd.}\end{array}\right.

Define
\mathsf{D}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(Z))
by declaring
A\in\mathsf{D}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(Z))
if there exist
A_{\mu}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(Z)
for
\mu<\eta
such that
A=\mathsf{D}_{\eta}(A_{\mu}:\mu<\eta)
.

We conclude this section by stating a classical result that will serve as a fundamental technical tool in Chapter
7
(see
[
Ke
, Theorem 22.16]
).
2
2

2

The conclusion of this theorem could be stated more succinctly by saying that
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
has the
generalized reduction property
(see
[
Ke
, Definition 22.14]
).

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
X
be a zero-dimensional space, and let
1\leq\xi<\omega_{1}
. If
U_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
for
n\in\omega
then there exist
U^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
for
n\in\omega
such that the following conditions are satisfied:

•

U^{\prime}_{n}\subseteq U_{n}
for each
n
,

•

U^{\prime}_{m}\cap U^{\prime}_{n}=\varnothing
whenever
m\neq n
,

•

\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U^{\prime}_{n}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U_{n}
.

2.2.
Determinacy and nice topological pointclasses

For more extensive introductions to the topic of games, we refer the reader to
[
Ke
, Section 20]
or
[
Je
, Chapter 33]
. Here, we only want to give the definitions of strategy and determinacy. Given a set
\Omega
and
S\subseteq\Omega^{\omega}
, a
play
of the
game

\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{G}(\Omega,S)
is described by the diagram

I

x_{0}

x_{1}

\cdots

II

y_{0}

y_{1}

\cdots
,

where
x_{n},y_{n}\in\Omega
for
n\in\omega
. We will say that Player I
wins
this play of
\mathsf{G}
if
(x_{0},y_{0},x_{1},y_{1},\ldots)\in S
. Player II
wins
if Player I does not win. The set
S
is called the
payoff set
.

A
strategy
for Player I on
\Omega
is a monotone
\sigma:\Omega^{<\omega}\longrightarrow\Omega^{<\omega}
such that
|\sigma(s)|=|s|+1
for every
s\in\Omega^{<\omega}
. We will say that a strategy
\sigma
for Player I on
\Omega
is
winning
in
\mathsf{G}
if setting
x=f_{\sigma}(y)
yields a play in which Player I wins for every
y\in\Omega^{\omega}
, where
x=(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots)
and
y=(y_{0},y_{1},\ldots)
. Similarly, a
strategy
for Player II on
\Omega
is a monotone
\tau:\Omega^{<\omega}\longrightarrow\Omega^{<\omega}
such that
|\tau(s)|=|s|
for all
s\in\Omega^{<\omega}
, and
\tau
is
winning
in
\mathsf{G}
if setting
y=f_{\tau}(x)
yields a play in which Player II wins for every
x\in\Omega^{\omega}
. We will say that the game
\mathsf{G}
(or simply the set
S
) is
determined
if (exactly) one of the players has a winning strategy. For the purposes of this article, only the case in which
\Omega
is countable will be relevant. Given
\mathbf{\Sigma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega^{\omega})
, we will write
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
to mean that every element of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is determined. The assumption
\mathsf{Det}(\mathcal{P}(\omega^{\omega}))
is known as the
Axiom of Determinacy
(
\mathsf{AD}
). The assumption
\mathsf{Det}(\bigcup_{1\leq n<\omega}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}(\omega^{\omega}))
is known as the axiom of
Projective Determinacy
(
\mathsf{PD}
).

We will use
\mathsf{AC}
to denote the Axiom of Choice, and
\mathsf{AC}_{\omega}
to denote the Countable Axiom of Choice. Recall that the principle of
Dependent Choices
(
\mathsf{DC}
) states that if
R
is a binary relation on a non-empty set
\Omega
such that for every
x\in\Omega
there exists
y\in\Omega
such that
(y,x)\in R
, then there exists a sequence
(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots)\in\Omega^{\omega}
such that
(x_{n+1},x_{n})\in R
for every
n\in\omega
. This principle is what is needed to carry out recursive constructions of length
\omega
. Furthermore, it is easy to see that the implications
\mathsf{AC}\rightarrow\mathsf{DC}\rightarrow\mathsf{AC}_{\omega}
hold (see
[
Je
, Exercise 5.7]
).

As the determinacy assumptions get stronger, the set-theoretic universe becomes more regular. It follows that
\mathsf{AC}
is incompatible with
\mathsf{AD}
, since
\mathsf{AC}
enables one to construct pathological sets (see for example
[
Je
, Lemma 33.1]
). For this reason, as we mentioned at the beginning of the article, we will be working in
\mathsf{ZF}+\mathsf{DC}
.
3
3

3

As eloquently put by Kanamori, while comparing
\mathsf{DC}
to a weaker axiom, “it soon became
de rigueur
to assume the stronger
\mathsf{DC}
in much of the investigation of
\mathsf{AD}
” (see
[
Ka
, page 378]
).
It is a famous theorem of Martin that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathsf{Borel}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds in
\mathsf{ZF}+\mathsf{AC}_{\omega}
(this was originally proved in
[
Ma1
]
, but see also
[
Ma2
, Remark (2) on page 307]
). However, Harrington showed that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(\omega^{\omega}))
has large cardinal strength (see
[
Har
]
). For the consistency of
\mathsf{ZF}+\mathsf{DC}+\mathsf{AD}
, see
[
Ne
]
and
[
Ka
, Proposition 11.13]
.

Most results that use determinacy assumptions follow a consistent pattern: they will hold for Borel sets without any additional assumptions (by Martin’s theorem), for projective sets under
\mathsf{PD}
, and for all sets under
\mathsf{AD}
. The following is
[
CMM2
, Definition 3.1]
, and it provides a convenient expositional tool to simultaneously state the various versions of our results.
4
4

4

Notice that the term “function” in the following definition is an abuse of terminology, as each topological pointclass is a proper class. Therefore, every theorem in this paper that mentions these pointclasses is strictly speaking an infinite scheme (one theorem for each topological pointclass).
This is what we mean when we speak about “level-by-level” results. Given a set
Z
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
, we will denote by
\mathsf{b}\mathbf{\Sigma}
the smallest boolean subalgebra of
\mathcal{P}(Z)
that contains
\mathbf{\Sigma}
.

Definition 2.5

(Carroy, Medini, Müller)
.

A function
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is a
topological pointclass
if it satisfies the following requirements:

•

The domain of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is the class of all spaces,

•

\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
for every space
Z
,

•

If
f:Z\longrightarrow W
is continuous and
B\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(W)
then
f^{-1}[B]\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
.

We will say that a topological pointclass
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is
nice
if it satisfies the following additional requirements:

•

\mathsf{Borel}(Z)\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
for every space
Z
,

•

\mathsf{b}\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)=\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
for every space
Z
,

•

If
f:Z\longrightarrow W
is a Borel function and
B\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(W)
then
f^{-1}[B]\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
,

•

For every space
Z
, if
j[Z]\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(W)
for some Borel space
W
and embedding
j:Z\longrightarrow W
, then
i[Z]\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(W)
for every Borel space
W
and embedding
i:Z\longrightarrow W
.

For the purposes of this paper, the following are the intended examples of nice topological pointclasses.
5
5

5

For a more detailed discussion, see
[
CMM2
, Section 3]
.
Recall that
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}(Z)
for
1\leq n<\omega
can be defined for an arbitrary space
Z
by declaring
A\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}(Z)
if there exists a Polish space
W
and an embedding
j:Z\longrightarrow W
such that
j[A]=\widetilde{A}\cap j[Z]
for some
\widetilde{A}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}(Z)
.

•

\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)=\mathsf{Borel}(Z)
for every space
Z
,

•

\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)=\mathsf{b}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}(Z)
for every space
Z
, where
1\leq n<\omega
,

•

\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)=\bigcup_{1\leq n<\omega}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}(Z)
for every space
Z
,

•

\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)=\mathcal{P}(Z)
for every space
Z
.

We conclude this section by giving precise formulations of well-known results concerning the Baire property under suitable determinacy assumption. We remark that Corollary
2.8
will be needed in the proof of Theorem
9.9
. Recall that a subset
A
of a space
Z
has the
Baire property
if there exists an open subset
U
of
Z
such that
A\Delta U
is a meager subset of
Z
.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Then every element of
\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
has the Baire property in
\omega^{\omega}
.

Proof.

Set
\Omega=\omega^{<\omega}\setminus\{\varnothing\}
. Given
s_{n}\in\Omega
for
n\in\omega
, where each
s_{n}\in\omega^{k_{n}+1}
for suitable
k_{n}\in\omega
, define

\varphi(s_{0},s_{1},\ldots)=(s_{0}(0),\ldots,s_{0}(k_{0}),s_{1}(0)\ldots,s_{1}(k_{1}),\ldots),

and observe that
\varphi:\Omega^{\omega}\longrightarrow\omega^{\omega}
is continuous. In order to prove that
A
has the Baire property in
\omega^{\omega}
, by
[
Ke
, Exercises 8.35 and 8.36]
, it will be enough to show that
\mathsf{G}(\Omega,\varphi^{-1}[(\omega^{\omega}\setminus A)\cup U])
is determined for every open subset
U
of
\omega^{\omega}
, where
\mathsf{G}
is the game introduced at the beginning of this section. This is straightforward to check, using the fact that
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is a nice topological pointclass and our determinacy assumptions.
∎

Corollary 2.7
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be a Polish space. Then every element of
\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
has the Baire property in
Z
.

Proof.

The desired result is trivial if
Z
is empty, so assume that
Z
is non-empty. Then, by
[
Ke
, Exercise 7.14]
, we can fix an open continuous surjection
f:\omega^{\omega}\longrightarrow Z
. Pick
A\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
. Notice that
f^{-1}[A]\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
because
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is a topological pointclass, hence
f^{-1}[A]
has the Baire property in
\omega^{\omega}
by Theorem
2.6
. This means that
f^{-1}[A]\,\Delta\,U
is meager in
\omega^{\omega}
for some open subset
U
of
\omega^{\omega}
. Since

A\,\Delta\,f[U]=f[f^{-1}[A]]\,\Delta\,f[U]\subseteq f[f^{-1}[A]\,\Delta\,U],

it will be enough to show that the right-hand side is meager in
Z
. This follows from the fact that open continuous surjections map meager sets to meager sets.
∎

Corollary 2.8
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be a Polish space, and let
X\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
be a dense Baire subspace of
Z
. Then
X
is comeager in
Z
.

Proof.

First, observe that
X
has the Baire property in
Z
by Corollary
2.7
. This means that there exist
G\in\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{2}(Z)
and a meager subset
M
of
Z
such that
X=G\cup M
(see
[
Ke
, Proposition 8.23]
). Notice that
M
is also meager in
X
by density. Since
X
is a Baire space, it follows that
G
is dense in
X
, hence in
Z
.
∎

2.3.
Filters and semifilters

Throughout this section, we will assume that
\Omega
is a given countably infinite set. We will say that
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
is
closed under finite modifications
if
|x\Delta y|<\omega
and
x\in\mathcal{X}
implies
y\in\mathcal{X}
for all
x,y\subseteq\Omega
. A collection
\mathcal{X}
is
closed under finite intersections
(respectively
closed under finite unions
) if
x\cap y\in\mathcal{X}
(respectively
x\cup y\in\mathcal{X}
) for all
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
. A collection
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
is
upward-closed
(respectively
downward-closed
) if
y\supseteq x\in\mathcal{X}
(respectively
y\subseteq x\in\mathcal{X}
) implies
y\in\mathcal{X}
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
.

Definition 2.9
.

A
semifilter
on
\Omega
is a collection
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
that satisfies the following conditions:

•

\varnothing\notin\mathcal{S}
and
\Omega\in\mathcal{S}
,

•

\mathcal{S}
is closed under finite modifications,

•

\mathcal{S}
is upward-closed.

A
filter
on
\Omega
is a semifilter
\mathcal{F}
on
\Omega
that satisfies the following additional condition:

•

\mathcal{F}
is closed under finite intersections.

A
semiideal
on
\Omega
is a collection
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
that satisfies the following conditions:

•

\varnothing\in\mathcal{R}
and
\Omega\notin\mathcal{R}
,

•

\mathcal{R}
is closed under finite modifications,

•

\mathcal{R}
is downward-closed.

An
ideal
on
\Omega
is a semiideal
\mathcal{I}
on
\Omega
that satisfies the following additional condition:

•

\mathcal{I}
is closed under finite unions.

When the set
\Omega
is not mentioned, we will assume that
\Omega=\omega
.

A collection
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
has the
finite intersection property
(respectively the
finite union property
) if
\bigcap F\notin\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)
(respectively
\bigcup F\notin\mathsf{Cof}(\Omega)
) whenever
F\subseteq\mathcal{X}
is finite and non-empty. One can easily check that a collection
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
has the finite intersection property (respectively the finite union property) iff it can be extended to a filter (respectively an ideal) on
\Omega
.

Next, we will point out how semifilters (respectively filters) are indistinguishable from semiideals (respectively ideals) from the topological point of view. To be more precise, given
F\subseteq\omega
,
x\in 2^{\omega}
and
n\in\omega
, define

\left.\begin{array}[]{lcl}&&h_{F}(x)(n)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1-x(n)&\textrm{if }n\in F,\\
x(n)&\textrm{if }n\in\omega\setminus F.\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

It is clear that each
h_{F}:2^{\omega}\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
is a homeomorphism. Throughout this article, we will denote by
c=h_{\omega}
the complement homeomorphism. Given any
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
, it is trivial to check that
\mathcal{X}
is a semifilter (respectively a semiideal) iff
c[\mathcal{X}]
is a semiideal (respectively a semifilter). Similarly, one sees that
\mathcal{X}
is a filter (respectively an ideal) iff
c[\mathcal{X}]
is an ideal (respectively a filter). Since
\mathcal{X}\approx c[\mathcal{X}]
, this means that every result about the topology of semifilters (respectively filters) immediately translates to a result about semiideals (respectively ideals), and viceversa.

As an application of this principle, one can see that every filter is a topological group. In fact, it is straightforward to check that every ideal is a topological subgroup of
2^{\omega}
with the operation of coordinatewise addition modulo
2
, and any space that is homeomorphic to a topological group is a topological group. In particular, every filter is homogeneous. As Corollary
2.18
will show, this is true of semifilters as well, but the proof is considerably more involved.

Another notable topological property of filters is given by the following result from
[
MZ2
]
, which will be useful in Section
10.2
.

Theorem 2.10

(Medini, Zdomskyy)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a filter. Then
\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{F}\approx\mathcal{F}
.

We conclude this section with some well-known facts about the Baire category of the above combinatorial objects under suitable determinacy assumptions.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathcal{X}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(2^{\omega})
be closed under finite modifications. Then
\mathcal{X}
is either meager in
2^{\omega}
or comeager in
2^{\omega}
.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathcal{X}
is non-meager. Then, since
\mathcal{X}
has the Baire property in
2^{\omega}
by Corollary
2.7
, there exist
n\in\omega
and
s\in 2^{n}
such that
\mathcal{X}\cap\mathsf{N}_{s}
is comeager in
\mathsf{N}_{s}
(see
[
Ke
, Proposition 8.26]
). Notice that

2^{\omega}\setminus\mathcal{X}=\bigcup\{h_{F}[\mathsf{N}_{s}\setminus\mathcal{X}]:F\subseteq n\}

because
\mathcal{X}
is closed under finite modifications. Since the right-hand side is a finite union of meager subsets of
2^{\omega}
, it follows that
\mathcal{X}
is comeager, as desired.
∎

Corollary 2.12
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathcal{F}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(2^{\omega})
be a filter. Then
\mathcal{F}
is meager in
2^{\omega}
.

Proof.

Assume, in order to get a contradiction, that
\mathcal{F}
is non-meager in
2^{\omega}
. Then
\mathcal{F}
is comeager in
2^{\omega}
by Proposition
2.11
. Since
c
is a homeomorphism and
2^{\omega}
is a non-meager space, it follows that there exists
x\in\mathcal{F}
such that
\omega\setminus x=c(x)\in\mathcal{F}
, which contradicts the definition of filter.
∎

2.4.
Homogeneity

We have already introduced homogeneity in Definition
1.1
. The following notion is both a useful technical tool and an independent object of study (see the introduction to
[
Me2
, Chapter 3]
for a brief survey of this topic).

Definition 2.13
.

A zero-dimensional space
X
is
strongly homogeneous
(or
h-homogeneous
) if every non-empty clopen subspace of
X
is homeomorphic to
X
.

Familiar examples of strongly homogeneous spaces are given by the rationals
\mathbb{Q}
, the Cantor set
2^{\omega}
, and Baire space
\omega^{\omega}
. This can be seen by using their characterizations (see
[
Ke
, Exercise 7.12, Theorem 7.4 and Theorem 7.7]
respectively).

As an unpleasant example, consider the subspace

X=(\mathbb{R}\times\{0\})\cup\{(q_{n},2^{-n}):n\in\omega\}

of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathbb{Q}=\{q_{n}:n\in\omega\}
is an enumeration. Using
[
Me1
, Theorem 18]
, one can show that every non-empty clopen subspace of
X^{\omega}
is homeomorphic to
X^{\omega}
. On the other hand, the space
X^{\omega}
is not homogeneous because it is zero-dimensional at some points but not at others. So, if we dropped the zero-dimensionality requirement from Definition
2.13
, there would be strongly homogeneous spaces that are not homogeneous, which is highly undesirable.

In fact, even when confined to the zero-dimensional realm, the relationship between homogeneity and strong homogeneity remains rather delicate. It is a folklore result that every strongly homogeneous space is homogeneous (see Corollary
2.16
). Furthermore, the discrete spaces of size at least two and
\omega\times 2^{\omega}
are obviously homogeneous but not strongly homogeneous. However, as Proposition
2.14
and Theorem
9.19
will show, it is consistent that these are the only zero-dimensional counterexamples (this was first proved in
[
CMM1
]
).

The following simple proposition justifies referring to the locally compact case of the classification as the trivial case.

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
X
be a zero-dimensional locally compact space. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

X
is homogeneous ,

•

X
is discrete,
X\approx 2^{\omega}
, or
X\approx\omega\times 2^{\omega}
.

Proof.

Clearly, all the spaces mentioned above are homogeneous. In order to prove the other implication, assume that
X
is homogeneous but not discrete. Notice that
X
must be crowded by homogeneity. Since
X
is locally compact and zero-dimensional, it is possible to partition
X
into compact clopen sets. Using
[
Ke
, Theorem 7.4]
, it follows that
X\approx 2^{\omega}
(if the partition is finite) or
X\approx\omega\times 2^{\omega}
(if the partition is infinite).
∎

The following ingenious result is an important tool for constructing homogeneous spaces, and it first appeared as
[
vM2
, Lemma 2.1]
.
6
6

6

This result was later generalized by Medvedev (see
[
Mv
, Theorem 3]
). In fact, the proof given here is a simplified version of Medvedev’s proof.
Corollary
2.17
is essentially the same as
[
MvMZ
, Lemma 2]
. Given a space
X
and
x\in X
, recall that a
local base
for
X
at
x
is a collection
\mathcal{B}
consisting of open neighborhoods of
x
such that for every open neighborhood
U
of
x
there exists
V\in\mathcal{B}
such that
V\subseteq U
.

Theorem 2.15

(van Mill)
.

Let
X
be a space, let
x,y\in X
, let
U_{0}\supseteq U_{1}\supseteq\cdots
be clopen subsets of
X
such that
\{U_{n}:n\in\omega\}
is a local base for
X
at
x
, and let
V_{0}\supseteq V_{1}\supseteq\cdots
be clopen subsets of
X
such that
\{V_{n}:n\in\omega\}
is a local base for
X
at
y
. Assume that
U_{n}\approx V_{n}
for each
n
. Then there exists a homeomorphism
h:X\longrightarrow X
such that
h(x)=y
.

Proof.

Assume without loss of generality that
U_{0}\cap V_{0}=\varnothing
. Fix homeomorphisms
f_{n}:U_{n}\longrightarrow V_{n}
for
n\in\omega
. If
f_{n}(x)=y
for some
n
, then it would be clear how to construct the desired homeomorphism. So assume that
f_{n}(x)\neq y
for each
n
. This makes it possible to also assume that
f_{n}[U_{n+1}]\cap V_{n+1}=\varnothing
for each
n
. We will construct homeomorphisms
h_{n}:U_{n}\setminus U_{n+1}\longrightarrow V_{n}\setminus V_{n+1}
for
n\in\omega
. In the end, set

\left.\begin{array}[]{lcl}&&h(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}z&\textrm{if }z\in X\setminus(U_{0}\cup V_{0}),\\
h_{n}(z)&\textrm{if }U_{n}\setminus U_{n+1},\\
h_{n}^{-1}(z)&\textrm{if }V_{n}\setminus V_{n+1},\\
y&\textrm{if }z=x,\\
x&\textrm{if }z=y.\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

for
z\in X
. It is straightforward to check that
h
will be as desired.

So pick
n\in\omega
. Recall that
f_{n}[U_{n+1}]\cap V_{n+1}=\varnothing
, hence
U_{n+1}\cap f_{n}^{-1}[V_{n+1}]=\varnothing
. Observe that
f_{n}[U_{n+1}\cup f_{n}^{-1}[V_{n+1}]]=f_{n}[U_{n+1}]\cup V_{n+1}
, therefore

f_{n}[U_{n}\setminus(U_{n+1}\cup f_{n}^{-1}[V_{n+1}])]=V_{n}\setminus(f_{n}[U_{n+1}]\cup V_{n+1}).

Furthermore, since

f_{n}^{-1}[V_{n+1}]\approx V_{n+1}\approx U_{n+1}\approx f_{n}[U_{n+1}],

it is possible to fix a homeomorphism
g_{n}:f_{n}^{-1}[V_{n+1}]\longrightarrow f_{n}[U_{n+1}]
. Finally, given
z\in U_{n}\setminus U_{n+1}
, define

\left.\begin{array}[]{lcl}&&h_{n}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f_{n}(z)&\textrm{if }z\in U_{n}\setminus(U_{n+1}\cup f_{n}^{-1}[V_{n+1}]),\\
g_{n}(z)&\textrm{if }z\in f_{n}^{-1}[V_{n+1}].\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

It is clear that
h_{n}:U_{n}\setminus U_{n+1}\longrightarrow V_{n}\setminus V_{n+1}
is a homeomorphism, as desired.
∎

Corollary 2.16
.

Every zero-dimensional strongly homogeneous space is homogeneous.

Corollary 2.17
.

Every subspace of
2^{\omega}
that is closed under finite modifications is homogeneous.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a subspace of
2^{\omega}
that is closed under finite modifications. Pick
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
. We will show that there exists a homeomorphism
h:X\longrightarrow X
such that
h(x)=y
. Set
U_{n}=\mathsf{N}_{x\upharpoonright n}\cap\mathcal{X}
and
V_{n}=\mathsf{N}_{y\upharpoonright n}\cap\mathcal{X}
for
n\in\omega
. By Theorem
2.15
, it will be enough to show that
U_{n}\approx V_{n}
for each
n
.

So pick
n\in\omega
, and set
F=\{i\in n:x(i)\neq y(i)\}
. Consider the homeomorphism
h_{F}
defined in Section
2.3
, and observe that
h_{F}[\mathsf{N}_{x\upharpoonright n}]=\mathsf{N}_{y\upharpoonright n}
. Using the fact that
\mathcal{X}
is closed under finite modifications, one sees that
h_{F}[\mathcal{X}]=\mathcal{X}
. It follows that
h_{F}[U_{n}]=V_{n}
, hence
U_{n}\approx V_{n}
.
∎

Corollary 2.18
.

Every semifilter is homogeneous.

At this point, it seems fitting to mention the following proposition and question (they first appeared as
[
Me4
, Proposition 13.6 and Question 13.5]
respectively).

Proposition 2.19
.

Every filter is strongly homogeneous.

Question 2.20
.

Is every semifilter strongly homogeneous?

The following diagram illustrates the connections between the homogeneity-type properties and the combinatorial properties that we have discussed so far. With the possible exception of the implication mentioned by Question
2.20
, the implications depicted below (and their obvious consequences) are the only ones that are provable in
\mathsf{ZF}+\mathsf{DC}
alone. The desired counterexamples are given by Proposition
10.10
, by the proof of
[
CMM1
, Theorem 1.2]
(which was essentially obtained by van Douwen in
[
vD
]
), and by
[
Me3
, Proposition 8.3]
.
7
7

7

A few clarifications are in order, so that trivial counterexamples will be avoided. First, we are restricting the attention to zero-dimensional spaces that are not locally compact. Second, by filter (respectively semifilter), we mean homeomorphic to a filter (respectively homeomorphic to a semifilter). Third, regarding the relationship between filters and topological groups, we refer the reader to Section
10.4
for a more nuanced discussion.

\textstyle{\mathrm{Filter}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Topological\text{ }group}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Semifilter}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Strongly\text{ }homogeneous}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Homogeneous}}

Next, we prove an intuitive dichotomy regarding homogeneous spaces, which can be assumed to be folklore (but see
[
FZ
, Lemma 3.1]
for a more general result).

Proposition 2.21
.

If
X
is a homogeneous space then
X
is either meager or Baire.

Proof.

Assume that
X
is a homogeneous space that is not Baire. Fix a non-empty meager open subset
U
of
X
. Notice that

\mathcal{U}=\{h[U]:h:X\longrightarrow X\text{ is a homeomorphism}\}

is an open cover of
X
by homogeneity, and that
\mathcal{U}
consists of meager subsets of
X
. By considering a countable subcover of
\mathcal{U}
, one sees that
X
is meager.
∎

We conclude this section with a technical result that will be needed in the proof of Corollary
9.10
. It is a slight variation on
[
Te
, Theorem 2.4]
. Recall that a
\pi
-base

\mathcal{B}
for a space is a collection
\mathcal{B}
consisting of non-empty open sets such that for every non-empty open set
U
there exists
V\in\mathcal{B}
such that
V\subseteq U
.

Lemma 2.22

(Terada)
.

Let
X
be a zero-dimensional space. Assume that
X
has a
\pi
-base
\mathcal{B}
consisting of clopen sets such that
U\approx X
for every
U\in\mathcal{B}
. Then
X
is strongly homogeneous.

Proof.

It is easy to realize that if
X
has an isolated point then
|X|=1
, so assume that
X
is crowded. If
X
is compact then
X\approx 2^{\omega}
by
[
Ke
, Theorem 7.4]
, so assume that
X
is non-compact. Fix an infinite partition of
X
consisting of clopen sets, and injectively enumerate it as
\{X_{n}:n\in\omega\}
.

Pick a non-empty clopen subspace
U
of
X
, and notice that
U
contains a clopen subspace that is homeomorphic to
X
. In particular, we can fix an infinite partition of
U
consisting of clopen sets, and injectively enumerate it as
\{U_{n}:n\in\omega\}
. Start by setting
Y_{0}=\varnothing
, then use our assumptions on
\mathcal{B}
to recursively construct clopen sets
V_{n}\subseteq U_{n}
and
Y_{n+1}\subseteq X_{n+1}
for
n\in\omega
such that
V_{n}\approx X_{n}\setminus Y_{n}
and
Y_{n+1}\approx U_{n}\setminus V_{n}
for each
n
. Finally, fix homeomorphisms
f_{n}:X_{n}\setminus Y_{n}\longrightarrow V_{n}
and
g_{n}:Y_{n+1}\longrightarrow U_{n}\setminus V_{n}
for
n\in\omega
, and set
h=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(f_{n}\cup g_{n})
. It is straightforward to check that
h:X\longrightarrow U
is a homeomorphism, as desired.
∎

Chapter 3
Wadge theory: fundamental notions and results

The purpose of this chapter is to establish the Wadge-theoretic preliminaries for the remainder of this monograph, mostly referring to
[
CMM2
]
for the proofs. The two most fundamental results are Wadge’s Lemma
3.3
and the fact that the Wadge hierarchy is well-founded (see Theorem
3.4
). The method of relativization allows one to apply Wadge theory not just in
\omega^{\omega}
(which is usually the only ambient space considered in the literature), but more generally in zero-dimensional Polish (or even Borel) spaces. We will also discuss the notions of level and expansion, which are best understood together (thanks to the Expansion Theorem
3.25
). Finally, we will discuss separated differences, which can be used to give a concrete description of the non-selfdual Wadge classes of level
0
. The operation of separated differences will be a crucial technical tool for both the uniqueness and existence results, because of the role that it plays in obtaining the closure properties of Sections
7.2
and
7.5
.

3.1.
The basics of Wadge theory

We begin with the most fundamental definitions and results of Wadge theory. For the proofs, see
[
CMM2
, Section 4]
. Assume that a set
Z
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
are given. We will use the notation
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}=\{Z\setminus A:A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}\}
for the
dual
of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
, and say that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is
selfdual
if
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
. Also set
\Delta(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\mathbf{\Gamma}\cap\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
.

Definition 3.1

(Wadge)
.

Let
Z
be a space, and let
A,B\subseteq Z
. We will write
A\leq B
if there exists a continuous function
f:Z\longrightarrow Z
such that
A=f^{-1}[B]
. In this case, we will say that
A
is
Wadge-reducible
to
B
, and that
f

witnesses
the reduction.
1
1

1

Wadge-reduction is usually denoted by
\leq_{\mathsf{W}}
, which allows to distinguish it from other types of reduction (such as Lipschitz-reduction). Since we will not consider any other type of reduction in this article, we decided to simplify the notation.
We will write
A<B
if
A\leq B
and
B\not\leq A
. We will say that
A
is
selfdual
if
A\leq Z\setminus A
. We will use the notation

A\!\!\downarrow\,=\{B\subseteq Z:B\leq A\}.

We will say that
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
is
continuously closed
if
C\!\!\downarrow\,\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
for every
C\subseteq Z
.

We have already introduced Wadge classes in Definition
1.2
. Notice that, using the notation of Definition
3.1
, the Wadge classes in a space
Z
are precisely the sets of the form
A\!\!\downarrow\,
for some
A\subseteq Z
. Also observe that a subset
A
of a space
Z
is selfdual iff
A\!\!\downarrow\,
is selfdual.

Definition 3.2
.

Given a space
Z
, define

\mathsf{NSD}(Z)=\{\mathbf{\Gamma}:\mathbf{\Gamma}\textrm{ is a non-selfdual Wadge class in }Z\}.

Also set
\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)=\{\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}(Z):\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)\}
whenever
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is a topological pointclass.

Lemma 3.3

(Wadge)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be a zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
A,B\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
. Then either
A\leq B
or
Z\setminus B\leq A
.

Theorem 3.4

(Martin, Monk)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be a zero-dimensional Polish space. Then the relation
\leq
on
\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
is well-founded.

The following helpful criterion is a straightforward application of Lemma
3.3
. For example, it can be used to show that each
\mathsf{D}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(Z))
is a Wadge class whenever
Z
is an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space (since all these classes are non-selfdual by
[
Ke
, Exercise 22.26]
, or by the methods of Chapter
4
).

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be a zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
. If
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is continuously closed and non-selfdual then
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is a Wadge class.

The following important result gives the analysis of the selfdual sets (see
[
CMM2
, Corollary 5.5]
). In pratice, this result is used to reduce the problem at hand to the non-selfdual case.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be a zero-dimensional Polish space, let
A\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
be selfdual, and let
U\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(Z)
. Then there exist pairwise disjoint
U_{n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(U)
and non-selfdual
A_{n}<A
in
Z
for
n\in\omega
such that
\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U_{n}=U
and
\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(A_{n}\cap U_{n})=A\cap U
.

Next, we state an elementary result, which shows that clopen sets are “neutral sets” with respect to Wadge-reduction (the straightforward proof is left to the reader). In Chapter
7
, we will discuss much more sophisticated closure properties.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
Z
be a space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}
be a Wadge class in
Z
.

•

Assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}\neq\{Z\}
. Then
A\cap U\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
whenever
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
U\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(Z)
.

•

Assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}\neq\{\varnothing\}
. Then
A\cup U\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
whenever
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
U\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(Z)
.

We conclude this section with the complete analysis of the non-selfdual Wadge classes below
\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{2}
(see
[
CMM2
, Theorem 11.2]
).

Theorem 3.8
.

Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(Z)
be such that
\mathbf{\Gamma}\neq\{\varnothing\}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}\neq\{Z\}
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}(Z)
,

•

There exists
1\leq\eta<\omega_{1}
such that
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{D}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))
or
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\widecheck{\mathsf{D}}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))
.

3.2.
Relativization

When one tries to give a systematic exposition of Wadge theory, it soon becomes apparent that it would be very useful to be able to say when
A
and
B
belong to “the same” Wadge class, even when
A\subseteq Z
and
B\subseteq W
for distinct ambient spaces
Z
and
W
. In the non-selfdual case, this problem can be solved by using Wadge classes in
\omega^{\omega}
to parametrize Wadge classes in arbitrary zero-dimensional Polish spaces. This is essentially due to Louveau and Saint Raymond (see
[
LSR2
, Theorem 4.2]
), but we will follow the more systematic treatment given in
[
CMM2
]
. In particular, the following definition and three results establish the foundations of this method (see
[
CMM2
, Section 6]
for the proofs).

Definition 3.9

(Louveau, Saint Raymond)
.

Let
Z
be a space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega^{\omega})
. We will use the notation

\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)=\{A\subseteq Z:f^{-1}[A]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}\text{ for every continuous }f:\omega^{\omega}\longrightarrow Z\}.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be a zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
. Then there exists a unique
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
such that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)=\mathbf{\Lambda}
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
Z
and
W
be spaces, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega^{\omega})
.

•

If
f:Z\longrightarrow W
is continuous and
B\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
then
f^{-1}[B]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
.

•

If
h:Z\longrightarrow W
is a homeomorphism then
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
iff
h[A]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
.

•

\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)}=\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}(Z)
.

•

If
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is continuously closed then
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\omega^{\omega})=\mathbf{\Gamma}
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
and
W
be zero-dimensional Borel spaces such that
W\subseteq Z
, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Then

\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)=\{A\cap W:A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)\}.

Until now, we have never assumed the uncountability of the ambient spaces. As the following three results show, the situation gets particularly pleasant when this assumption is satisfied (see
[
CMM2
, Section 7 and Lemma 20.2]
for the proofs).

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space. Then

\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)=\{\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z):\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})\}.

Theorem 3.14
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
and
W
be uncountable zero-dimensional Borel spaces, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Then

\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)\subseteq\mathbf{\Lambda}(Z)\text{ iff }\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)\subseteq\mathbf{\Lambda}(W).

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be a zero-dimensional Borel space, let
W
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Assume that
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
and
B\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
. Then there exists a continuous
f:Z\longrightarrow W
such that
A=f^{-1}[B]
.

3.3.
Level

We begin with some preliminaries concerning the following useful notion.
2
2

2

According to Wadge, this notion had already been identified by “the classical descriptive set theorists” (see
[
Wa2
, page 187]
).
Partitioned unions will also play a major role in Chapter
6
.

Definition 3.16
.

Let
Z
be a space, let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. Define
\mathsf{PU}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
to be the collection of all sets of the form

\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(A_{n}\cap V_{n}),

where each
A_{n}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
, the
V_{n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}_{1+\xi}^{0}(Z)
are such that
V_{m}\cap V_{n}=\varnothing
whenever
m\neq n
, and
\bigcup_{n\in\omega}V_{n}=Z
. A set in this form is called a
partitioned union
of sets in
\mathbf{\Gamma}
.

The following proposition, whose straightforward proof is left to the reader, collects the most basic facts about partitioned unions.

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
Z
be a space, let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. Then

•

If
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is continuously closed then
\mathsf{PU}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
is continuously closed,

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathsf{PU}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Gamma})\subseteq\mathsf{PU}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
whenever
\eta\leq\xi
,

•

\mathsf{PU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\mathbf{\Gamma}
whenever
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is a Wadge class in
Z
,

•

\widecheck{\mathsf{PU}}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\mathsf{PU}_{\xi}(\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
,

•

\mathsf{PU}_{\xi}(\mathsf{PU}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Gamma}))=\mathsf{PU}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
.

The following notion was essentially introduced in
[
Lo1
]
. Our approach will be closer to that of
[
LSR1
]
and
[
Lo2
]
, as developed in
[
CMM2
]
.

Definition 3.18

(Louveau and Saint Raymond)
.

Let
Z
be a space, let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. Define

•

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq\xi
if
\mathsf{PU}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\mathbf{\Gamma}
,

•

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\xi
if
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq\xi
and
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\not\geq\xi+1
,

•

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\omega_{1}
if
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq\eta
for every
\eta<\omega_{1}
.

We refer to
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})
as the
level
of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
.

As immediate consequences of Proposition
3.17
, observe that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq 0
for every Wadge class
\mathbf{\Gamma}
, and that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\ell(\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
for an arbitrary
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. The following statements provide concrete examples, where
Z
is an uncountable Polish space (the last two can be proved using
[
Ke
, Theorem 22.4 and Proposition 37.1]
respectively):

•

\ell(\{\varnothing\})=\ell(\{Z\})=\omega_{1}
,

•

\ell(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z))=\ell(\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z))=\xi
whenever
\xi<\omega_{1}
,

•

\ell(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}(Z))=\ell(\mathbf{\Pi}^{1}_{n}(Z))=\omega_{1}
whenever
1\leq n<\omega
.

The following result (whose straightforward proof is left to the reader) gives the first hint that classes of higher level tend to have better closure properties.

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
Z
be a space, let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
be such that
\varnothing\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. Assume that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq\xi
. Then
A\cap V\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
whenever
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
V\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z)
.

Next, we will show that the level is well-behaved with respect to relativization (see
[
CMM2
, Corollary 16.2]
).

Theorem 3.20
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
and
W
be uncountable zero-dimensional Polish spaces, let
\xi<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Then

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z))\geq\xi\text{ iff }\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}(W))\geq\xi.

At this point, it is not clear whether every non-selfdual Wadge class
\mathbf{\Gamma}
even
has
a level.
3
3

3

For example, it is conceivable that
\mathsf{PU}_{n}(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\mathbf{\Gamma}
for all
n<\omega
, while
\mathsf{PU}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma})\neq\mathbf{\Gamma}
.
That this is actually the case is the content of the next theorem (see
[
CMM2
, Corollary 17.2]
), whose proof heavily relies on methods from
[
Lo2
]
.

Theorem 3.21

(Carroy, Medini, Müller)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be a zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
. Then there exists
\xi\leq\omega_{1}
such that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\xi
.

We conclude this section with a technical result concerning classes of level
0
. Lemma
3.22
first appeared as
[
CMM1
, Lemma 14.3]
, and it will be useful in the proof of Theorem
9.7
. By setting
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
and
A=Z\setminus\{z\}
, where
z\in Z
is non-isolated, one sees that the assumption that
A
is codense in
Z
cannot be dropped.

Lemma 3.22

(Carroy, Medini, Müller)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
, and let
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Assume that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=0
and that
A
is codense in
Z
. Then there exists a non-empty
U\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(Z)
and
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
such that
\mathbf{\Lambda}\subsetneq\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
A\cap U\in\mathbf{\Lambda}
.

Proof.

Using the assumption that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=0
, Lemma
3.3
and Proposition
3.17
, it is a simple exercise to show that
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\subseteq\mathsf{PU}_{1}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
. This means that we can fix
A_{n}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
U_{n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
such that
\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U_{n}=Z
, the
U_{n}
are pairwise disjoint, and
Z\setminus A=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(A_{n}\cap U_{n})
.

By the Baire category theorem, one can find a non-empty
U\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(Z)
such that
U\subseteq U_{n}
for some
n
. Next, using Lemma
3.7
, one sees that
U\setminus A=A_{n}\cap U\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
, hence
Z\setminus(A\cap U)=(U\setminus A)\cup(Z\setminus U)\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Therefore, we have
A\cap U\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
Z\setminus(A\cap U)\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
, which easily implies that
\mathbf{\Lambda}=(A\cap U)\!\!\downarrow\,
has the desired properties if
A\cap U
is non-selfdual.

To conclude the proof, assume that
A\cap U
is selfdual. By Theorem
3.6
, we can fix non-selfdual
B_{n}<A
and
V_{n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
such that
\bigcup_{n\in\omega}V_{n}=U
, the
V_{n}
are pairwise disjoint, and
A\cap U=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(B_{n}\cap V_{n})
. Since
A
is codense, there must be
n\in\omega
such that
V_{n}\neq\varnothing
and
B_{n}\neq Z
. In particular, it is possible to apply Lemma
3.7
, which yields
A\cap V_{n}=B_{n}\cap V_{n}\leq B_{n}
. Setting
\mathbf{\Lambda}=B_{n}\!\!\downarrow\,
then gives the desired result.
∎

3.4.
Expansions

The following notion can be traced back to
[
Wa1
, Chapter IV]
, and it is inspired by work of Kuratowski (see
[
Wa2
, Sections 20 and 21]
). Once again, our approach will be closer to that of
[
Lo2
]
, as developed in
[
CMM2
]
.

Definition 3.23
.

Let
Z
be a space, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. Given
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
, define

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}=\{f^{-1}[A]:A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}\text{ and }f:Z\longrightarrow Z\text{ is $\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}$-measurable}\}.

We will refer to
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}
as an
expansion
of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
.

The following proposition (whose straightforward proof is left to the reader) collects some basic facts about expansions.

Proposition 3.24
.

Let
Z
be a space, let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
.

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}
is continuously closed.

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\eta)}\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}
whenever
\eta\leq\xi
.

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(0)}=\mathbf{\Gamma}
whenever
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is continuously closed.

•

\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}}=\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{(\xi)}
.

The next result, known as the Expansion Theorem, clarifies the crucial connection between level and expansions. It is an immediate consequence of
[
CMM2
, Theorems 16.1 and 22.2]
.

Theorem 3.25
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
and
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq\xi
,

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\xi)}
for some
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
.

Corollary 3.26
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional space, let
\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. Then

\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Lambda}\text{ iff }\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}\subseteq\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\xi)}.

Proof.

The left-to-right implication is clear by the definition of expansion. In order to prove the other implication, assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}\nsubseteq\mathbf{\Lambda}
. Then
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Lambda}}\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
by Lemma
3.3
, which implies
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{(\xi)}\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}
. So, if we had
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}\subseteq\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\xi)}
, it would follow that
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\xi)}
is self-dual, contradicting Theorem
3.25
. Therefore
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}\nsubseteq\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\xi)}
, as desired.
∎

The following result shows that expansions “add up” when they are composed (see
[
CMM2
, Theorem 18.2]
). As a corollary, we will be able to determine the exact level of an expansion.

Theorem 3.27
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\xi,\eta<\omega_{1}
. Then

(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\eta)})^{(\xi)}=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi+\eta)}

whenever
\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\eta)}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
.

Corollary 3.28
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, let
\xi<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
. Assume that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\eta<\omega_{1}
. Then

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)})=\xi+\eta.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.25
, we can fix
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
such that
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\eta)}=\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Notice that
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\xi+\eta)}=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}
by Theorem
3.27
, hence
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)})\geq\xi+\eta
by Theorem
3.25
. Now assume, in order to get a contradiction, that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)})\geq\xi+\eta+1
. By Theorem
3.25
, we can fix
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
such that
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\xi+\eta+1)}=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}
. Notice that

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}=\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\xi+\eta+1)}=(\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\eta+1)})^{(\xi)},

where the second equality holds by Theorem
3.27
. It follows from Corollary
3.26
that
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\eta+1)}
, which contradicts the fact that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\ngeq\eta+1
by Theorem
3.25
.
∎

We conclude this section with three technical results. Lemmas
3.29
and
3.30
show that expansions interact in the expected way with the machinery of relativization (see
[
CMM2
, Lemmas 14.1 and 14.2]
respectively). Lemma
3.31
is a variation on the theme of Kuratowski’s Transfer Theorem (see
[
CMM2
, Section 12]
), and it can be proved like
[
CMM2
, Lemma 14.3]
.

Lemma 3.29
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
and
W
be zero-dimensional Polish spaces, let
\xi<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. If
f:Z\longrightarrow W
is
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}
-measurable then
f^{-1}[A]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}(Z)
for every
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
.

Lemma 3.30
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, let
\xi<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Then
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}(Z)=\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)^{(\xi)}
.

Lemma 3.31
.

Let
Z
be a zero-dimensional Polish space, let
\xi<\omega_{1}
, let
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega^{\omega})
for
n\in\omega
, and let
\mathcal{A}_{n}\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}(Z)^{(\xi)}
for
n\in\omega
be countable. Then there exist a zero-dimensional Polish space
W
and a
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}
-measurable bijection
f:Z\longrightarrow W
such that
f[A]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}(W)
for every
n\in\omega
and
A\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
.

3.5.
Separated differences

The following notion was essentially introduced in
[
Lo1
]
, but (as in
[
CMM2
]
) we will follow the simplified approach given in
[
Lo2
]
.

Definition 3.32

(Louveau)
.

Let
Z
be a space, let
1\leq\eta<\omega_{1}
, and let
U_{\mu,n},A_{\mu,n},A^{\ast}\subseteq Z
for
\mu<\eta
and
n\in\omega
. Define

(3.1)

\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}((U_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),(A_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),A^{\ast})=\\
=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu<\eta\\
n\in\omega\end{subarray}}\left(A_{\mu,n}\cap U_{\mu,n}\setminus\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu^{\prime}<\mu\\
m\in\omega\end{subarray}}U_{\mu^{\prime},m}\right)\cup\left(A^{\ast}\setminus\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu<\eta\\
n\in\omega\end{subarray}}U_{\mu,n}\right).

Given
\mathbf{\Delta},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
, define
\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})
as the collection of all sets in the above form, where each
U_{\mu,n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
and
U_{\mu,m}\cap U_{\mu,n}=\varnothing
whenever
m\neq n
, each
A_{\mu,n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}
, and
A^{\ast}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
. Sets in this form are known as
separated differences
.

Next, we state two simple but useful results (see
[
CMM2
, Lemmas 19.2 and 19.3]
respectively). We remark that Lemma
3.34
gives the simplest examples of Wadge classes that can be obtained as separated differences.

Lemma 3.33
.

Let
Z
be a space, let
1\leq\eta<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Delta},\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
. Then

\widecheck{\mathsf{SD}}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathbf{\Gamma})=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\widecheck{\mathbf{\Delta}},\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}).

Lemma 3.34
.

Let
Z
be a space, let
1\leq\eta<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Delta}=\{\varnothing\}\cup\{Z\}
. Then

\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{\varnothing\})=\mathsf{D}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))\text{ and }\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{Z\})=\widecheck{\mathsf{D}}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)).

Separated differences are important because they make it possible to give concrete descriptions of the non-selfdual Wadge classes of level
0
. This is made precise by the following result, which is essentially due to Louveau (it can be easily derived using
[
CMM2
, Proposition 19.4 and Theorem 22.2]
). See also the first paragraph of Section
6.2
to clarify the meaning of
\mathbf{\Delta}
.

Theorem 3.35
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}(Z)
and
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=0
,

•

There exist
1\leq\eta<\omega_{1}
,
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\in\mathsf{NSD}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}\in\mathsf{NSD}(Z)
such that each
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n})\geq 1
,
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}\subseteq\mathbf{\Delta}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})
, where
\mathbf{\Delta}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n})
.

Chapter 4
Wadge theory: Hausdorff operations and universal sets

In this chapter, we will discuss the classical notions of Hausdorff operation and universal set. Ultimately, these notions will allow us to construct new non-selfdual Wadge classes by combinining given ones in a suitable way (see Corollary
4.16
). This will be applied to separated unions in Theorem
6.6
. Furthermore, the results on universal sets will find a direct application in Lemma
5.4
. Along the way, we will discuss the auxiliary notion of relativizable pointclass. Finally, we will show how to interpret level and expansions in the context of Hausdorff operations.

4.1.
Hausdorff operations

While the following notion is certainly not the focus of this article, it is technically very useful, since it captures the standard way to produce non-selfdual Wadge classes. In fact, in the next section, we will show that classes with a universal set must be non-selfdual, and Hausdorff operations are an appropriate tool to manipulate universal sets. The exposition in this section is based on
[
CMM2
, Sections 8 and 9]
, and all results are straightforward to prove except Theorem
4.7
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
, and let
Z
be a set. Define

\mathcal{H}_{D}(A_{0},A_{1},\ldots)=\{x\in Z:\{n\in\omega:x\in A_{n}\}\in D\}

whenever
A_{0},A_{1},\ldots\subseteq Z
. Functions of this form are called
Hausdorff operations
(or
\omega
-ary Boolean operations
).

Notice that the set
D
in the above definition simultaneously yields a Hausdorff operation for every set
Z
. In case it will be important to specify the ambient set, we will use the notation
\mathcal{H}_{D}^{Z}
.

A basic fact about Hausdorff operations, expressed by the following proposition, is that they include all operations obtained by combinining unions, intersections and complements.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
I
be a non-empty set, and let
D_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
for
i\in I
. Fix an ambient set
Z
and
A_{0},A_{1},\ldots\subseteq Z
.

•

\mathcal{H}_{S_{n}}(A_{0},A_{1},\ldots)=A_{n}
for all
n\in\omega
, where
S_{n}=\{A\subseteq\omega:n\in A\}
.

•

\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathcal{H}_{D_{i}}(A_{0},A_{1},\ldots)=\mathcal{H}_{D}(A_{0},A_{1},\ldots)
, where
D=\bigcap_{i\in I}D_{i}
.

•

\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{H}_{D_{i}}(A_{0},A_{1},\ldots)=\mathcal{H}_{D}(A_{0},A_{1},\ldots)
, where
D=\bigcup_{i\in I}D_{i}
.

•

Z\setminus\mathcal{H}_{D}(A_{0},A_{1},\ldots)=\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{P}(\omega)\setminus D}(A_{0},A_{1},\ldots)
for all
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
.

Furthermore, the composition of Hausdorff operations still yields a Hausdorff operation, as described by the following proposition.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
Z
be a set, let
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
and
E_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
for
n\in\omega
. Fix a bijection
\sigma:\omega\times\omega\longrightarrow\omega
. Then there exists
F\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
such that

\mathcal{H}_{D}(B_{0},B_{1},\ldots)=\mathcal{H}_{F}(A_{0},A_{1},\ldots)

for all
A_{0},A_{1},\ldots\subseteq Z
, where
B_{n}=\mathcal{H}_{E_{n}}(A_{\sigma(n,0)},A_{\sigma(n,1)},\ldots)
for
n\in\omega
.

As an application, one can combine Propositions
4.2
and
4.3
to prove by induction that each
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(Z)
is a Hausdorff class for every space
Z
. Furthermore, the same will be true for the classes
\mathsf{D}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(Z))
.

The following result shows that Hausdorff operations are well-behaved with respect to inverse images and subsets.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
, let
Z
and
W
be sets, let
A_{0},A_{1},\ldots\subseteq Z
, and let
B_{0},B_{1},\ldots\subseteq W
.

•

f^{-1}[\mathcal{H}_{D}(B_{0},B_{1},\ldots)]=\mathcal{H}_{D}(f^{-1}[B_{0}],f^{-1}[B_{1}],\ldots)
for all
f:Z\longrightarrow W
.

•

W\cap\mathcal{H}_{D}^{Z}(A_{0},A_{1},\ldots)=\mathcal{H}_{D}^{W}(A_{0}\cap W,A_{1}\cap W,\ldots)
whenever
W\subseteq Z
.

In the present context, the significance of the following definition lies in the fact that, under suitable determinacy assumptions, it gives an alternative description of non-selfdual Wadges classes (see Theorem
4.7
).

Definition 4.5
.

Let
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
. Define

\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(Z)=\{\mathcal{H}_{D}(A_{0},A_{1},\ldots):A_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)\text{ for }n\in\omega\}

for every space
Z
. We will refer to collections in this form as
Hausdorff classes
.

The following result is the analogue of relativization for Hausdorff classes, and it can be proved using Lemma
4.4
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
, and let
Z
and
W
be spaces.

•

If
f:Z\longrightarrow W
is continuous and
B\in\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(W)
then
f^{-1}[B]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(Z)
.

•

Assume that
W\subseteq Z
. Then
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(W)=\{A\cap W:A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(Z)\}
.

For a proof of the following result, see
[
CMM2
, Theorem 22.2]
. The case
Z=\omega^{\omega}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathcal{P}
is originally due to Van Wesep (see
[
VW1
, Theorem 5.3.1]
).

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
,

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(Z)
for some
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
.

4.2.
Relativizable pointclasses and universal sets

In Section
2.2
, we have seen that nice topological pointclasses provide a suitable framework to formulate determinacy assumptions. Next, we will give another application of topological pointclasses, by showing that the following notion provides a suitable framework to formulate and prove results concerning universal sets.

Definition 4.8
.

We will say that a function
\mathbf{\Pi}
is a
relativizable pointclass
if the following conditions hold:

•

\mathbf{\Pi}
is a topological pointclass,

•

\mathbf{\Pi}(W)=\{A\cap W:A\in\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)\}
whenever
Z
and
W
are zero-dimensional Borel spaces such that
W\subseteq Z
.

Given
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
, using Lemma
4.6
, one sees that setting
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(Z)
for every space
Z
yields a relativizable pointclass. However, in the present context, the most important example is given by the following definition. Notice that the assignment described by Definition
4.9
(under suitable determinacy assumptions) gives a relativizable pointclass by Lemmas
3.11
and
3.12
. In fact, the reason for the restriction “zero-dimensional Borel” in Definitions
4.8
and
4.9
is that Lemma
3.12
only applies to such spaces.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}(\omega^{\omega})
. We will say that a relativizable pointclass
\mathbf{\Pi}

induced
by
\mathbf{\Gamma}
if
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)=\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
for every zero-dimensional Borel space
Z
.

The following is a classical notion (see for example
[
Ke
, Definition 22.2]
).

Definition 4.10
.

Let
\mathbf{\Pi}
