be a topological pointclass, and let
W
and
Z
be spaces. We will say that
S
is a
W
-universal set
for
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
if the following conditions hold:

•

S\in\mathbf{\Pi}(W\times Z)
,

•

\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)=\{S_{y}:y\in W\}
,

where we denote by
S_{y}=\{x\in Z:(y,x)\in S\}
the
vertical section
above
y\in W
.

Next, we will prove three results that are essentially the same as
[
CMM2
, Proposition 10.2, Corollary 10.3, and Lemma 10.4]
, except that we will formulate them in the more general framework described above. We remark that Proposition
4.11
could be viewed as a warm-up for Theorem
4.15
.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
, and let
Z
be a space. Then there exists a
2^{\omega}
-universal set for
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(Z)
.

Proof.

By
[
Ke
, Theorem 22.3]
, we can fix a
2^{\omega}
-universal set
U
for
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
. Let
h:2^{\omega}\longrightarrow(2^{\omega})^{\omega}
be a homeomorphism, and let
\pi_{n}:(2^{\omega})^{\omega}\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
denote the projection on the
n
-th coordinate for
n\in\omega
. Set
(y)_{n}=\pi_{n}(h(y))
for
y\in 2^{\omega}
and
n\in\omega
. Pick a space
Z
. By
[
Ke
, Theorem 22.3]
, we can fix a
2^{\omega}
-universal set
U
for
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
. Define

U_{n}=\{(y,x)\in 2^{\omega}\times Z:((y)_{n},x)\in U\}

for
n\in\omega
, and observe that each
U_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(2^{\omega}\times Z)
.

Set
S=\mathcal{H}_{D}(U_{0},U_{1},\ldots)
. In order to conclude the proof, we will show that
S
is a
2^{\omega}
-universal set for
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(Z)
. Using Lemma
4.6
, it is easy to see that
S\in\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(2^{\omega}\times Z)
and
S_{y}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(Z)
for every
y\in 2^{\omega}
. Pick
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(Z)
. Let
A_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
be such that
A=\mathcal{H}_{D}(A_{0},A_{1},\ldots)
. Since
U
is
2^{\omega}
-universal, we can fix
y_{n}\in 2^{\omega}
for
n\in\omega
such that
U_{y_{n}}=A_{n}
for each
n
. Let
y\in 2^{\omega}
be defined by requiring that
(y)_{n}=y_{n}
for each
n
. It is straightforward to verify that
S_{z}=A
.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\mathbf{\Pi}
be a relativizable topological pointclass, and let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Borel space. If there exists a
2^{\omega}
-universal set for
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
then there exists a
Z
-universal set for
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
.

Proof.

Let
S\in\mathbf{\Pi}(2^{\omega}\times Z)
be a
2^{\omega}
-universal set for
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
. Using
[
Ke
, Theorem 13.6]
, we can fix an embedding
j:2^{\omega}\longrightarrow Z
. Set
W=j[2^{\omega}]
. Notice that
(j\times\mathsf{id}_{Z})[U]\in\mathbf{\Pi}(W\times Z)
because
\mathbf{\Pi}
is a topological pointclass. Since
\mathbf{\Pi}
is relativizable, there exists
T\in\mathbf{\Pi}(Z\times Z)
such that
T\cap(W\times Z)=(j\times\mathsf{id}_{Z})[S]
. It is straightforward to check that
T
is a
Z
-universal set for
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
.
∎

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
\mathbf{\Pi}
be a topological pointclass, and let
Z
be a space. If there exists a
Z
-universal set for
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
then
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
is non-selfdual.

Proof.

Fix a
Z
-universal set
S
for
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
. Assume, in order to get a contradiction, that
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
is selfdual. Define
f:Z\longrightarrow Z\times Z
by setting
f(z)=(z,z)
for
z\in Z
, and observe that
f
is continuous. It follows that
f^{-1}[S]\in\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
, hence
Z\setminus f^{-1}[S]\in\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
. Therefore, since
S
is
Z
-universal, we can fix
z\in Z
such that
S_{z}=Z\setminus f^{-1}[S]
. It is straightforward to check that
z\in S_{z}
iff
z\notin S_{z}
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Finally, we are in a position to characterize the induced relativizable pointclasses, under suitable determinacy assumptions. Notice that Theorem
4.15
can be viewed as an improved version of Theorem
4.7
.

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Pi}
be a relativizable pointclass such that
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
for every zero-dimensional Borel space
Z
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\mathbf{\Pi}
is induced by some
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
,

(2)

There exists
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
such that
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(Z)
for every zero-dimensional Borel space
Z
,

(3)

There exists
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
such that
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(Z)
for some uncountable zero-dimensional Borel space
Z
,

(4)

\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
has a
2^{\omega}
-universal set for every zero-dimensional Borel space
Z
,

(5)

\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
has a
2^{\omega}
-universal set for some uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
,

(6)

\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
set for every uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
,

(7)

\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
for some zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
.

Proof.

The implication
(1)\rightarrow(3)
follows from Theorem
4.7
, and the implication
(2)\rightarrow(3)
is trivial. In order to prove that
(3)\rightarrow(2)
, assume that
(3)
holds for some uncountable zero-dimensional Borel space
Z
. Pick a zero-dimensional Borel space
W
, and assume without loss of generality that
W\subseteq Z
. Then

\mathbf{\Pi}(W)=\{A\cap W:A\in\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)\}=\{A\cap W:A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(Z)\}=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(W),

where the first equality holds because
\mathbf{\Pi}
is relativizable, and the last equality follows from Lemma
4.6
.

The implication
(2)\rightarrow(4)
follows from Proposition
4.11
, and the implication
(4)\rightarrow(5)
is trivial. To see that the implication
(5)\rightarrow(6)
holds, fix an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
such that
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
has a
2^{\omega}
-universal set. Notice that
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
is continuously closed because
\mathbf{\Pi}
is a topological pointclass. Furthermore, Lemmas
4.12
and
4.13
guarantee that
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
is non-selfdual. It follows from Lemma
3.5
that
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)=\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
, hence
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)=\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
for some
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Now let
W
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space. Since
Z
is uncountable, we can assume without loss of generality that
W\subseteq Z
. Then

\mathbf{\Pi}(W)=\{A\cap W:A\in\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)\}=\{A\cap W:A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)\}=\mathbf{\Gamma}(W),

where the first equality holds because
\mathbf{\Pi}
is relativizable, and the last equality follows from Lemma
3.12
. Since
W
is uncountable, it follows from Theorem
3.13
that
\mathbf{\Pi}(W)\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(W)
, as desired.

The implication
(6)\rightarrow(7)
is trivial. Therefore, in order to conclude the proof, it will be enough to show that
(7)\rightarrow(1)
. So fix an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
such that
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
.

Claim.

\mathbf{\Pi}(\omega^{\omega})\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
.

Proof of Claim.
By
[
Ke
, Theorem 7.8]
, we can assume without loss of generality that
Z
is a closed subspace of
\omega^{\omega}
. By
[
Ke
, Proposition 2.8]
, we can fix a retraction
\rho:\omega^{\omega}\longrightarrow Z
. By Lemma
3.5
, it will be enough to show that
\mathbf{\Pi}(\omega^{\omega})
is non-selfdual. Assume, in order to get a contradiction, that
\mathbf{\Pi}(\omega^{\omega})
is selfdual, and pick
A
such that
A\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
. Then
\rho^{-1}[Z\setminus A]=\omega^{\omega}\setminus\rho^{-1}[A]\in\mathbf{\Pi}(\omega^{\omega})
, hence

Z\setminus A=Z\cap(\rho^{-1}[Z\setminus A])\in\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)

because
\mathbf{\Pi}
is relativizable. This contradicts the fact that
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
is non-selfdual.
\blacksquare

Set
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Pi}(\omega^{\omega})
. First, using arguments similar to those in the proof of the above claim, one can easily show that
\mathbf{\Pi}(W)=\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
for every zero-dimensional Polish space
W
. Then, using the fact that
\mathbf{\Pi}
is relativizable and Lemma
3.12
, it is straightforward to check that
\mathbf{\Pi}
is induced by
\mathbf{\Gamma}
.
∎

4.3.
New Wadge classes from old

Many examples of Wadge classes are obtained by combining elements of known classes using a fixed set-theoretic operation (consider for example the difference hierarchy). In fact, combining sets that belong to a given sequence of non-selfdual Wadge classes according to a given Hausdorff operation always yields a non-selfdual Wadge class (this follows from Corollary
4.16
by ignoring the sets
U_{2k+1}
). However, the operation of separated unions (to be introduced in Definition
6.5
) will require in addition that certain sets are pairwise disjoint. This is why, in this section, the statements of our results look more complicated than one might expect.

We begin by showing that combining relativizable pointclasses according to a fixed Hausdorff operation preserves the property of having a universal set. Notice that Theorem
4.15
does not require any determinacy assumptions. The desired result on combining Wadge class will then follow easily, thanks to Theorem
4.14
.

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
, let
\mathbf{\Pi}_{n}
for
n\in\omega
be relativizable topological pointclasses, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. Assume that each
\mathbf{\Pi}_{n}
has a
2^{\omega}
-universal set. Set

(4.1)

\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)=\{\mathcal{H}_{D}(A_{0},U_{1},A_{2},U_{3}\ldots):A_{2k}\in\mathbf{\Pi}_{k}(Z)\text{ for every }k\in\omega\text{,}\\
U_{2k+1}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z)\text{ for every }k\in\omega\text{, and }U_{2i+1}\cap U_{2j+1}\text{ whenever }i\neq j\}

for every space
Z
. Then
\mathbf{\Pi}
is a relativizable topological pointclass and
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
has a
2^{\omega}
-universal set for every zero-dimensional space
Z
.

Proof.

Using the assumption that each
\mathbf{\Pi}_{n}
is a relativizable pointclass and Theorem
2.4
, it is straightforward to check that
\mathbf{\Pi}
is a relativizable pointclass. Pick a zero-dimensional space
Z
. Fix a homeomorphism
h:2^{\omega}\longrightarrow(2^{\omega})^{\omega}
. Set
(y)_{n}=\pi_{n}(h(y))
for
y\in 2^{\omega}
and
n\in\omega
, where
\pi_{n}:(2^{\omega})^{\omega}\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
denotes the projection on the
n
-th coordinate. By assumption, for every
k\in\omega
we can fix a
2^{\omega}
-universal set
S_{2k}
for
\mathbf{\Pi}_{k}(Z)
. Define

S^{\prime}_{2k}=\{(y,x)\in 2^{\omega}\times Z:((y)_{2k},x)\in S_{2k}\}

for
k\in\omega
, and observe that each
S^{\prime}_{2k}\in\mathbf{\Pi}_{k}(2^{\omega}\times Z)
.

By
[
Ke
, Theorem 22.3]
, we can fix a
2^{\omega}
-universal set
U
for
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z)
. Define

U_{2k+1}=\{(y,x)\in 2^{\omega}\times Z:((y)_{2k+1},x)\in U\}

for
k\in\omega
, and observe that each
U_{2k+1}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(2^{\omega}\times Z)
. By Theorem
2.4
, there exist
U^{\prime}_{2k+1}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(2^{\omega}\times Z)
for
k\in\omega
such that the following conditions are satisfied:

•

U^{\prime}_{2k+1}\subseteq U_{2k+1}
for each
k
,

•

U^{\prime}_{2j+1}\cap U^{\prime}_{2k+1}=\varnothing
whenever
j\neq k
,

•

\bigcup_{k\in\omega}U^{\prime}_{2k+1}=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}U_{2k+1}
.

Notice that, as
y\in 2^{\omega}
varies, the sequence of vertical sections

((U^{\prime}_{2k+1})_{y}:k\in\omega)

will yield precisely the sequences consisting of pairwise disjoint elements of
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z)
. Furthermore, changing the values of
(y)_{n}
for even
n
will not affect the above sequence.

Finally, define

T=\mathcal{H}_{D}(S^{\prime}_{0},U^{\prime}_{0},S^{\prime}_{1},U^{\prime}_{1},\ldots).

Using the properties that we just mentioned, it is straightforward to check that
T
is a
2^{\omega}
-universal set for
\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
.
∎

Corollary 4.16
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
, let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. If
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
and

(4.2)

\mathbf{\Gamma}=\{\mathcal{H}_{D}(A_{0},U_{1},A_{2},U_{3}\ldots):A_{2k}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\text{ for every }k\in\omega\text{,}\\
U_{2k+1}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z)\text{ for every }k\in\omega\text{, and }U_{2i+1}\cap U_{2j+1}\text{ whenever }i\neq j\}

then
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}(Z)
.

Proof.

Pick
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}(Z)\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
, where each
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Let
\mathbf{\Pi}_{n}
be the relativizable pointclass induced by
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}
for
n\in\omega
. It is clear that
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Pi}(Z)
, where
\mathbf{\Pi}
is defined as in the statement of Theorem
4.15
. Therefore, the desired conclusion follows from Theorems
4.14
and
4.15
.
∎

4.4.
Clarifying level and expansions

In this section, we will show how to view level and expansions through the lens of Hausdorff operations. While the only result from this section that will be needed in the rest of the article is Proposition
4.19
, the alternative descriptions given here are helpful in clarifying these two important concepts.

Definition 4.17
.

Let
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. Define

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}_{D}(Z)=\{\mathcal{H}_{D}(A_{0},A_{1},\ldots):A_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z)\text{ for }n\in\omega\}

for every space
Z
.

The following result (see
[
CMM2
, Lemma 13.9]
) shows that Definition
4.17
is the counterpart of expansions in the present context.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
, let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. Then
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{D}(Z)^{(\xi)}=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}_{D}(Z)
.

As a first application, we will give some concrete examples of expansions. This will be useful in Section
7.2
.

Proposition 4.19
.

Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, let
1\leq\eta<\omega_{1}
, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. Set
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{D}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))
. Then
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}=\mathsf{D}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z))
.

Proof.

Simply apply Lemma
4.18
for the appropriate choice of
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
.
∎

We conclude this section with a characterization of the level that is perhaps more intuitive than the official Definition
3.23
. The Borel version of Theorem
4.20
originally appeared as part of
[
LSR1
, Théorème 8]
.

Theorem 4.20
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
. Then

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\mathsf{sup}\{\xi<\omega_{1}:\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}_{D}(Z)\text{ for some }D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)\}.

Proof.

Set
I=\{\xi<\omega_{1}:\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}_{D}(Z)\text{ for some }D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)\}
. First assume that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\xi<\omega_{1}
. Let
\xi^{\prime}\in I
. By Theorem
3.25
, Theorem
4.7
and Lemma
4.18
, we must have
\xi\geq\xi^{\prime}
. Therefore, the inequality
\geq
holds. On the other hand, a similar argument shows that
\xi\in I
, hence the inequality
\leq
holds. The case
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\omega_{1}
is analogous, but slightly easier.
∎

Chapter 5
Wadge theory: the separation property

The purpose of this chapter is to introduce the separation property, and to give a complete proof that exactly one Wadge class in every non-selfdual pair has the separation property (see Theorem
5.8
). This theorem is essentially due to Steel and Van Wesep, and it will be important for our existence results, because of the role that it plays in the proof of Theorem
7.18
, which in turn will be needed to obtain one of the closure properties of Section
7.5
. It will also be applied directly in the proofs of Corollaries
9.15
and
9.17
. However, the separation property is not relevant to the uniqueness theorem.

5.1.
Definition and auxiliary results

Much like the socks in a pair, a non-selfdual Wadge class
\mathbf{\Gamma}
and its dual
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
do not appear to be distinguishable in any obvious way. Luckily, work of Steel and Van Wesep provides an elegant solution to this problem, at least when the ambient space is
\omega^{\omega}
. The key notion is the (first) separation property, already mentioned in the introduction and formally defined below.
1
1

1

The second separation property will only be needed in this section and the next, and it will not appear anywhere else in this article. According to
[
VW1
, page 92]
and
[
VW2
, page 77]
, both separation properties are due to Lusin.
More precisely, they showed that exactly one of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
has the separation property. We will show that their result holds for uncountable zero-dimensional Polish spaces (see Theorem
5.8
).

Definition 5.1

(Lusin)
.

Let
Z
be a set, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
.

•

We will say that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the
first separation property
(or simply the
separation property
) if for all
A,B\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
such that
A\cap B=\varnothing
there exists
C\in\Delta(\mathbf{\Gamma})
such that
A\subseteq C\subseteq Z\setminus B
.

•

We will say that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the
second separation property
if for all
A,B\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
there exist
A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\in\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
such that
A\setminus B\subseteq A^{\prime}
,
B\setminus A\subseteq B^{\prime}
, and
A^{\prime}\cap B^{\prime}=\varnothing
.

We begin by showing that both of the separation properties are preserved by relativization.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be a zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
.

(1)

If
\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the first separation property then
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
has the first separation property.

(2)

If
\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the second separation property then
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
has the second separation property.

Proof.

By
[
Ke
, Theorem 7.8]
, we can assume without loss of generality that
Z
is a closed subspace of
\omega^{\omega}
. Using
[
Ke
, Proposition 2.8]
, we can fix a retraction
\rho:\omega^{\omega}\longrightarrow Z
. In order to prove
(1)
, assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the separation property. Pick disjoint
A,B\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
, and observe that
\rho^{-1}[A],\rho^{-1}[B]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
by Lemma
3.11
. Since
\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the separation property, there exists
C\in\Delta(\mathbf{\Gamma})
such that
\rho^{-1}[A]\subseteq C\subseteq\omega^{\omega}\setminus\rho^{-1}[B]
. Notice that
C\cap Z\in\Delta(\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z))
by Lemma
3.12
. Since clearly
A\subseteq C\cap Z\subseteq Z\setminus B
, this concludes the proof of
(1)
. The proof of
(2)
is similar.
∎

We conclude this section by describing a method that is at the heart of the results of the next two sections.
2
2

2

According to
[
VW1
, page 99]
and
[
VW2
, page 81]
, this method was invented by Martin (see the proof of
[
VW3
, Theorem 2.5]
).

Lemma 5.3

(Martin)
.

Let
\Omega
be a non-empty discrete space, and let
\sigma_{i}
be a strategy for Player I on
\omega
for
i\in\Omega
. Set
f_{i}=f_{\sigma_{i}}
for
i\in\Omega
. Then there exists a function
\lambda:\omega\times\Omega^{\omega}\longrightarrow\omega^{\omega}
satisfying the following conditions, where we use the notation
z^{\ast}_{n}=\lambda(n,z)
:

(1)

f_{z(n)}(z^{\ast}_{n+1})=z^{\ast}_{n}
for every
z\in\Omega^{\omega}
and
n\in\omega
,

(2)

(i^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{n+1}=z^{\ast}_{n}
for every
z\in\Omega^{\omega}
,
i\in\Omega
and
n\in\omega
,

(3)

(i^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}=f_{i}(z^{\ast}_{0})
for every
z\in\Omega^{\omega}
and
i\in\Omega
,

(4)

\lambda
is continuous.

Proof.

First, we give a slightly informal description of the construction of
\lambda
, based on the following diagram. Assuming that
z\in\Omega^{\omega}
has been fixed, the
n
-th element of the first row (starting from the left) is simply the first element of
\omega
that Player I should play according to
\sigma_{z(n)}
. Then, one obtains the second row by letting
z^{\ast}_{n}(1)
be the response of Player I to
z^{\ast}_{n+1}(0)
according to
\sigma_{z(n)}
. By repeating this process, one obtains all the remaining rows.

\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{z^{\ast}_{2}(0)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{z(1)}}
\textstyle{z^{\ast}_{1}(0)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{z(0)}}
\textstyle{z^{\ast}_{0}(0)}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{z^{\ast}_{2}(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{z(1)}}
\textstyle{z^{\ast}_{1}(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{z(0)}}
\textstyle{z^{\ast}_{0}(1)}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{z^{\ast}_{2}(2)}
\textstyle{z^{\ast}_{1}(2)}
\textstyle{z^{\ast}_{0}(2)}

\ddots

\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}

More formally, the definition of
\lambda
is given by requiring that

z^{\ast}_{n}\upharpoonright(k+1)=\sigma_{z(n)}(z^{\ast}_{n+1}\upharpoonright k)

for every
z\in\Omega^{\omega}
and
n,k\in\omega
. Using this equality, one sees that

f_{z(n)}(z^{\ast}_{n+1})=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}\sigma_{z(n)}(z^{\ast}_{n+1}\upharpoonright k)=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}z^{\ast}_{n}\upharpoonright(k+1)=z^{\ast}_{n}

for every
z\in\Omega^{\omega}
and
n\in\omega
. Therefore
(1)
holds. Furthermore, given
z\in\Omega^{\omega}
,
i\in\Omega
and
n\in\omega
, it is straightforward to prove by induction that
(i^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{n+1}\upharpoonright k=z^{\ast}_{n}\upharpoonright k
for each
k
. Hence
(2)
holds. To prove that
(3)
holds, simply observe that

(i^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}=f_{i}((i^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{1})=f_{i}(z^{\ast}_{0})

for every
z\in\Omega^{\omega}
and
i\in\Omega
, where the first equality holds by
(1)
and the second equality holds by
(2)
. Given
(n,z)\in\omega\times\Omega^{\omega}
and
k\in\omega
, one sees that
\lambda(n,z^{\prime})\upharpoonright k=\lambda(n,z)\upharpoonright k
whenever
z^{\prime}\in\omega^{\omega}
and
z^{\prime}(i)=z(i)
for all
i\in\omega
such that
n\leq i<n+k
. This shows that
(4)
holds.
∎

5.2.
At most one class in every pair has the separation property

All the results in this section are essentially due to Van Wesep, except that we have given more general formulations, with the goal of obtaining Theorem
5.8
. Lemmas
5.4
and
5.5
are “extracted” from the proof of
[
VW1
, Theorem 4.2.1]
(see also
[
VW2
, Theorem 1]
and
[
VW3
, Theorem 5.2]
), while Theorem
5.6
is taken almost verbatim from
[
VW1
, Theorem 4.2.2]
(see also
[
VW2
, Theorem 2]
and
[
VW3
, Theorem 5.3]
), except that we have given a more systematic account of Martin’s method (see Lemma
5.3
).

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Then there exists
(A_{0},A_{1})\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z\times Z)\times\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z\times Z)
such that for every
(B_{0},B_{1})\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)\times\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
there exists
z\in Z
such that
(A_{0})_{z}=B_{0}
and
(A_{1})_{z}=B_{1}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.14
, we can fix a
2^{\omega}
-universal set
S
for
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
. Make the following definitions:

•

A^{\prime}_{0}=\{(x,y,z)\in 2^{\omega}\times 2^{\omega}\times Z:(x,z)\in S\}
,

•

A^{\prime}_{1}=\{(x,y,z)\in 2^{\omega}\times 2^{\omega}\times Z:(y,z)\in S\}
.

Using Lemma
3.11
, one sees that
A^{\prime}_{0},A^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega}\times 2^{\omega}\times Z)
. Furthermore, it is easy to realize that for every
(B_{0},B_{1})\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)\times\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
there exists
(x,y)\in 2^{\omega}\times 2^{\omega}
such that
(A^{\prime}_{0})_{(x,y)}=B_{0}
and
(A^{\prime}_{1})_{(x,y)}=B_{1}
.

By
[
Ke
, Corollary 6.5]
, we can fix a subspace
K
of
Z
such that
K\approx 2^{\omega}
. Let
h:2^{\omega}\times 2^{\omega}\longrightarrow K
be a homeomorphism. Define
j:2^{\omega}\times 2^{\omega}\times Z\longrightarrow Z\times Z
by setting
j(x,y,z)=(h(x,y),z)
for
(x,y,z)\in 2^{\omega}\times 2^{\omega}\times Z
, and observe that
j
is an embedding with
\mathsf{ran}(j)=K\times Z
. By Lemma
3.12
, there exist
A_{0},A_{1}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z\times Z)
such that
A_{0}\cap(K\times Z)=j[A^{\prime}_{0}]
and
A_{1}\cap(K\times Z)=j[A^{\prime}_{1}]
. It is straightforward to check that
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
are as desired.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. If
\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the second separation property then
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}(Z)
does not have the first separation property.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the second separation property. By Lemma
5.4
, we can fix
(A_{0},A_{1})\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z\times Z)\times\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z\times Z)
such that for every
(B_{0},B_{1})\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)\times\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
there exists
z\in Z
such that
(A_{0})_{z}=B_{0}
and
(A_{1})_{z}=B_{1}
. Since
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z\times Z)
has the second separation property by Lemma
5.2
, there exist
A^{\prime}_{0},A^{\prime}_{1}\in\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}(Z\times Z)
such that
A_{0}\setminus A_{1}\subseteq A^{\prime}_{0}
,
A_{1}\setminus A_{0}\subseteq A^{\prime}_{1}
, and
A^{\prime}_{0}\cap A^{\prime}_{1}=\varnothing
. Set
B_{0}=\{z\in Z:(z,z)\in A^{\prime}_{0}\}
and
B_{1}=\{z\in Z:(z,z)\in A^{\prime}_{1}\}
. Observe that
B_{0},B_{1}\in\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}(Z)
by Lemma
3.11
, and that
B_{0}\cap B_{1}=\varnothing
because
A^{\prime}_{0}\cap A^{\prime}_{1}=\varnothing
.

Assume, in order to get a contradiction, that
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}(Z)
has the first separation property. Then there exists
C\in\Delta(\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z))
such that
B_{0}\subseteq C\subseteq Z\setminus B_{1}
. Fix
z\in Z
such that
(A_{0})_{z}=Z\setminus C
and
(A_{1})_{z}=C
. It is straightforward to check that
z\in C
iff
z\notin C
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Theorem 5.6

(Van Wesep)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Then least one of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
has the second separation property.

Proof.

Since
\omega^{\omega}\times\omega^{\omega}\approx\omega^{\omega}
, using Lemma
5.4
we can fix
(A_{0},A_{1})\in\mathbf{\Gamma}\times\mathbf{\Gamma}
such that for every
(B_{0},B_{1})\in\mathbf{\Gamma}\times\mathbf{\Gamma}
there exists a continuous
f:\omega^{\omega}\longrightarrow\omega^{\omega}
such that
f^{-1}[A_{i}]=B_{i}
for each
i
. Define the game
\mathsf{G}_{0}
by declaring that Player II wins if the following conditions are satisfied:

•

x\in A_{0}\setminus A_{1}\rightarrow y\in A_{1}\setminus A_{0}
,

•

x\in A_{1}\setminus A_{0}\rightarrow y\in A_{0}\setminus A_{1}
,

•

y\notin A_{0}\cap A_{1}
,

where
x\in\omega^{\omega}
and
y\in\omega^{\omega}
denote the sequences played by Player I and Player II respectively. Similarly, define the game
\mathsf{G}_{1}
by declaring that Player II wins if the following conditions are satisfied:

•

x\in A_{0}\setminus A_{1}\rightarrow y\in A_{0}\setminus A_{1}
,

•

x\in A_{1}\setminus A_{0}\rightarrow y\in A_{1}\setminus A_{0}
,

•

y\in A_{0}\cup A_{1}
.

Claim 1.
If Player II has a winning strategy in
\mathsf{G}_{1}
then
\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the second separation property.

Proof of Claim 1.
Let
\tau
be a winning strategy for Player II in
\mathsf{G}_{0}
. Set
g=f_{\tau}
, and observe that
g
is continuous by Proposition
2.1
. Define
A_{i}^{\prime}=g^{-1}[\omega^{\omega}\setminus A_{1-i}]
for
i\in 2
, and observe that each
A_{i}^{\prime}\in\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
. Furthermore, since
\tau
is winning, one sees that
A_{0}\setminus A_{1}\subseteq A_{0}^{\prime}
,
A_{1}\setminus A_{0}\subseteq A_{1}^{\prime}
, and
A_{0}^{\prime}\cap A_{1}^{\prime}=\varnothing
. The property of
(A_{0},A_{1})
stated at the beginning of the proof then yields the desired result.
\blacksquare

Claim 2.
If Player II has a winning strategy in
\mathsf{G}_{0}
then
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
has the second separation property.

Proof of Claim 2.
This is similar to the proof of Claim 1.
\blacksquare

Given Claims 1 and 2, and the assumption that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds, it remains to show that Player I cannot have winning strategies for both
\mathsf{G}_{0}
and
\mathsf{G}_{1}
. Assume, in order to get a contradiction, that this is not the case. Fix a winning strategy
\sigma_{i}
for Player I in
\mathsf{G}_{i}
for
i\in 2
. Set
f_{i}=f_{\sigma_{i}}
for
i\in 2
, and observe that each
f_{i}
is continuous by Proposition
2.1
.

It is clear from the definition of
\mathsf{G}_{0}
that for every
y\in\omega^{\omega}
at least one of the following conditions will hold:

•

f_{0}(y)\in A_{0}\setminus A_{1}
and
y\notin A_{1}\setminus A_{0}
,

•

f_{0}(y)\in A_{1}\setminus A_{0}
and
y\notin A_{0}\setminus A_{1}
,

•

y\in A_{0}\cap A_{1}
.

Therefore, the following conditions will hold for every
y\in\omega^{\omega}
:

•

y\in A_{0}\setminus A_{1}\rightarrow f_{0}(y)\in A_{0}\setminus A_{1}
,

•

y\in A_{1}\setminus A_{0}\rightarrow f_{0}(y)\in A_{1}\setminus A_{0}
,

•

y\in\omega^{\omega}\setminus(A_{0}\cup A_{1})\rightarrow f_{0}(y)\in(A_{0}\setminus A_{1})\cup(A_{1}\setminus A_{0})
.

By a similar argument, the following conditions will hold for every
y\in\omega^{\omega}
:

•

y\in A_{0}\setminus A_{1}\rightarrow f_{1}(y)\in A_{1}\setminus A_{0}
,

•

y\in A_{1}\setminus A_{0}\rightarrow f_{1}(y)\in A_{0}\setminus A_{1}
,

•

y\in A_{0}\cap A_{1}\rightarrow f_{1}(y)\in(A_{0}\setminus A_{1})\cup(A_{1}\setminus A_{0})
.

Set
z^{\ast}_{n}=\lambda(n,z)
for
(n,z)\in\omega\times 2^{\omega}
, where
\lambda
is the function obtained by applying Lemma
5.3
with
\Omega=2
to
\sigma_{0}
and
\sigma_{1}
. Given
S\subseteq\omega^{\omega}
, set

S^{\ast}=\{z\in 2^{\omega}:z^{\ast}_{0}\in S\}.

Notice that
S^{\ast}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(2^{\omega})
whenever
S\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
, since the function
\lambda(0,-)
is continuous. In particular, all sets of the form
S^{\ast}
considered in the remainder of this proof will have the Baire property by Corollary
2.7
. Also observe that
(S\cup T)^{\ast}=S^{\ast}\cup T^{\ast}
for all
S,T\subseteq 2^{\omega}
.

Make the following definitions:

•

C=(A_{0}\setminus A_{1})\cup(A_{1}\setminus A_{0})
,

•

M=(A_{0}\cap A_{1})\cup(\omega^{\omega}\setminus(A_{0}\cup A_{1}))
.

Claim 3.

M^{\ast}
is meager.

Proof of Claim 3.
Assume that
M^{\ast}
is non-meager. By
[
Ke
, Proposition 8.26]
, we can fix
s\in 2^{<\omega}
such that
\{z\in 2^{\omega}:(s^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\in M\}
is comeager. Using Lemma
5.3
and the properties of the functions
f_{i}
listed above, it is easy to realize that
z^{\ast}_{0}\in C
implies
(i^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\in C
for each
i
. It follows that
(s^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\in M
implies
z^{\ast}_{0}\in M
, hence
M^{\ast}\supseteq\{z\in 2^{\omega}:(s^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\in M\}
is comeager. In particular, either
(A_{0}\cap A_{1})^{\ast}
or
(\omega^{\omega}\setminus(A_{0}\cup A_{1}))^{\ast}
is non-meager. We will assume that
(A_{0}\cap A_{1})^{\ast}
is non-meager, since the other case is similar. Notice that if
z^{\ast}_{0}\in A_{0}\cap A_{1}
then
(1^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\in C
. This shows that
\{z\in 2^{\omega}:(1^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\in C\}\supseteq(A_{0}\cap A_{1})^{\ast}
is non-meager, hence
C^{\ast}
is non-meager. This contradicts the fact that
M^{\ast}
is comeager.
\blacksquare

By Claim 3, either
(A_{0}\setminus A_{1})^{\ast}
or
(A_{1}\setminus A_{0})^{\ast}
is non-meager. Assume that
(A_{0}\setminus A_{1})^{\ast}
is non-meager, since the other case is symmetrical. By
[
Ke
, Proposition 8.26]
, we can fix
s\in 2^{<\omega}
such that
\{z\in 2^{\omega}:(s^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\in A_{0}\setminus A_{1}\}
is comeager. First assume that
s
contains an odd number of ones. Then
z^{\ast}_{0}\in A_{0}\setminus A_{1}
implies
(s^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\notin A_{0}\setminus A_{1}
. This means that
(A_{0}\setminus A_{1})^{\ast}\subseteq 2^{\omega}\setminus\{z\in 2^{\omega}:(s^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\in A_{0}\setminus A_{1}\}
is meager, which is a contradiction.

To conclude the proof, assume that
s
contains an even number of ones. Then
(A_{1}\setminus A_{0})^{\ast}\subseteq 2^{\omega}\setminus\{z\in 2^{\omega}:(s^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\in A_{0}\setminus A_{1}\}
is meager. It follows from Claim 3 that
(A_{0}\setminus A_{1})^{\ast}
is comeager. Since
\{z\in 2^{\omega}:(1^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\in A_{1}\setminus A_{0}\}\supseteq(A_{0}\setminus A_{1})^{\ast}
, this shows that
(A_{1}\setminus A_{0})^{\ast}
is non-meager, which is a contradiction.
∎

5.3.
At least one class in every pair has the separation property

The following is the main result of
[
St2
]
. Notice that, unlike in the previous section, the second separation property will be entirely bypassed.

Theorem 5.7

(Steel)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Then at least one of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
has the first separation property.

Proof.

Call
(E_{0},E_{1})
an
inseparable pair
if the following conditions hold:

•

Either
E_{0},E_{1}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
or
E_{0},E_{1}\in\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
,

•

E_{0}\cap E_{1}=\varnothing
,

•

There exists no
C\in\Delta(\mathbf{\Gamma})
such that
E_{0}\subseteq C\subseteq\omega^{\omega}\setminus E_{1}
.

Given
D_{0},D_{1},E_{0},E_{1}\subseteq\omega^{\omega}
, define the game
\mathsf{G}(D_{0},D_{1},E_{0},E_{1})
by declaring that Player I wins if

y\in D_{i}\rightarrow x\in E_{i}\text{ for every }i\in 2.

where
x\in\omega^{\omega}
and
y\in\omega^{\omega}
denote the sequences played by Player I and Player II respectively.

Claim.
Let
D_{0},D_{1}\subseteq\omega^{\omega}
, and let
(E_{0},E_{1})
be an inseparable pair. Assume that the following conditions hold:

•

Either
D_{0},D_{1}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
or
D_{0},D_{1}\in\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
,

•

D_{0}\cap D_{1}=\varnothing
.

Then Player I has a winning strategy in
\mathsf{G}(D_{0},D_{1},E_{0},E_{1})
.

Proof of the claim.
Assume, in order to get a contradiction, that Player I has no winning strategy. By the assumption that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds, we can fix a winning strategy
\tau
for Player II. Set
g=f_{\tau}
, and observe that
g
is continuous by Proposition
2.1
. Using the fact that
\tau
is winning, one sees that
g[E_{i}]\subseteq D_{1-i}
for each
i
and
g[\omega^{\omega}]\subseteq D_{0}\cup D_{1}
. It follows that
E_{0}\subseteq g^{-1}[D_{1}]\subseteq\omega^{\omega}\setminus E_{1}
and that
g^{-1}[D_{1}]=g^{-1}[\omega^{\omega}\setminus D_{0}]\in\Delta(\mathbf{\Gamma})
, which is a contradiction.
\blacksquare

Assume, in order to get a contradiction, that
(A_{0},A_{1})\in\mathbf{\Gamma}\times\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
(B_{0},B_{1})\in\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\times\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
are both inseparable pairs. By the claim, we can fix a winning strategy
\sigma
for Player I in
\mathsf{G}(A_{0},A_{1},B_{0},B_{1})
. Set
f=f_{\sigma}
, and observe that
f
is continuous by Proposition
2.1
. Define
C_{i}=f^{-1}[B_{i}]
for
i\in 2
. Notice that
f[A_{i}]\subseteq B_{i}
for each
i
because
\sigma
is winning, hence
A_{i}\subseteq C_{i}
for each
i
. Furthermore, it is clear that
C_{i}\in\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
for each
i
and
C_{0}\cap C_{1}=\varnothing
.

By applying the claim three more times, we can fix winning strategies
\sigma_{0}
,
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
for Player I in the games
\mathsf{G}(A_{1},A_{0},A_{0},A_{1})
,
\mathsf{G}(A_{0},\omega^{\omega}\setminus C_{0},A_{0},A_{1})
and
\mathsf{G}(\omega^{\omega}\setminus C_{1},A_{1},A_{0},A_{1})
respectively. Set
f_{i}=f_{\sigma_{i}}
for
i\in 3
, and observe that each
f_{i}
is continuous by Proposition
2.1
. Furthermore, it is easy to realize that the following inclusions hold:

•

f_{0}[A_{1}]\subseteq A_{0}
and
f_{0}[A_{0}]\subseteq A_{1}
,

•

f_{1}[A_{0}]\subseteq A_{0}
and
f_{1}[\omega^{\omega}\setminus C_{0}]\subseteq A_{1}
,

•

f_{2}[\omega^{\omega}\setminus C_{1}]\subseteq A_{0}
and
f_{2}[A_{1}]\subseteq A_{1}
.

Set
z^{\ast}_{n}=\lambda(n,z)
for
(n,z)\in\omega\times 3^{\omega}
, where
\lambda
is the function obtained by applying Lemma
5.3
with
\Omega=3
to
\sigma_{0}
,
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
. Given
S\subseteq 3^{\omega}
, set

S^{\ast}=\{z\in 3^{\omega}:z^{\ast}_{0}\in S\}.

Notice that
S^{\ast}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(3^{\omega})
whenever
S\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
, since the function
\lambda(0,-)
is continuous. In particular, all sets of the form
S^{\ast}
considered in the remainder of this proof will have the Baire property by Corollary
2.7
. Also observe that
(S\cup T)^{\ast}=S^{\ast}\cup T^{\ast}
for all
S,T\subseteq 2^{\omega}
.

First assume that
(A_{0}\cup A_{1})^{\ast}
is comeager. This means that we can fix
i\in 2
such that
A_{i}^{\ast}
is non-meager. By
[
Ke
, Proposition 8.26]
, we can fix
s\in 3^{<\omega}
such that
\{z\in 3^{\omega}:(s^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\in A_{i}\}
is comeager. Using Lemma
5.3
and the properties of the functions
f_{i}
listed above, one sees that if
s
contains an odd number of zeros, then
z^{\ast}_{0}\in A_{i}
implies
(s^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\in A_{1-i}
. On the other hand, if
s
contains an even number of zeros, then
z^{\ast}_{0}\in A_{i}
implies
(s^{\frown}0^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\in A_{1-i}
. In either case, it is easy to realize that
\{z\in 3^{\omega}:(s^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\in A_{1-i}\}
is non-meager, which is a contradiction.

Finally, assume that
(A_{0}\cup A_{1})^{\ast}
is non-comeager. By
[
Ke
, Proposition 8.26]
, we can fix
s\in 3^{<\omega}
such that
\{z\in 3^{\omega}:(s^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\notin A_{0}\cup A_{1}\}
is comeager. Since
(s^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\notin A_{0}\cup A_{1}
implies
z^{\ast}_{0}\notin A_{0}\cup A_{1}
, it follows that
\{z\in 3^{\omega}:z^{\ast}_{0}\notin A_{0}\cup A_{1}\}
is comeager. Since
(\omega^{\omega}\setminus C_{0})\cup(\omega^{\omega}\setminus C_{1})=\omega^{\omega}
, we can fix
i\in 2
such that
(\omega^{\omega}\setminus C_{i})^{\ast}
is non-meager. Since
z^{\ast}_{0}\notin C_{i}
implies
((i+1)^{\frown}z)^{\ast}_{0}\in A_{0}\cup A_{1}
, one sees that
(A_{0}\cup A_{1})^{\ast}
is non-meager. This contradiction concludes the proof.
∎

5.4.
The solution to the “pair of socks” problem

We are finally in a position to obtain the generalization promised in Section
5.1
.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space. If
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
then exactly one of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
has the separation property.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
, where
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. It follows immediately from Lemma
5.5
and Theorem
5.6
that at most one of
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
and
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}(Z)
has the separation property. On the other hand, by Lemma
5.2
and Theorem
5.7
, at least one of
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
and
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}(Z)
has the separation property.
∎

As a first application, we will show that expansions preserve the separation property. This result will be useful in the proof of Corollary
9.14
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. If
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
has the separation property then
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}
has the separation property.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
, where
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
, and assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
has the separation property. Pick disjoint
A,B\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)^{(\xi)}
. By Lemma
3.31
, we can fix a zero-dimensional Polish space
W
and a
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}
-measurable bijection
f:Z\longrightarrow W
such that
f[A],f[B]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
. Observe that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
must have the separation property, otherwise Lemma
5.2
and Theorem
5.8
would easily yield a contradiction. Therefore
\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
has the separation property by Lemma
5.2
. This means that we can find
C\in\Delta(\mathbf{\Gamma}(W))
such that
f[A]\subseteq C\subseteq W\setminus f[B]
. Notice that
f^{-1}[C]\in\Delta\left(\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)^{(\xi)}\right)
by Lemmas
3.29
and
3.30
. Since clearly
A\subseteq f^{-1}[C]\subseteq Z\setminus B
, this concludes the proof.
∎

Chapter 6
Wadge theory: type

The purpose of this chapter is to introduce the notions of type and separated unions, discuss their basic properties, and characterize the classes of small type. Loosely speaking, these are the classes that can be obtained by applying the operation of separated union to a countable collection of classes of lower complexity (see Theorem
6.11
). Much like the separation property, type and separated unions are not relevant to the uniqueness theorem, but they will be important for the existence results, because of the role that they play in obtaining the closure properties of Section
7.5
.

6.1.
Definition and basic properties

Given a partial order
(\mathbb{P},\preccurlyeq)
and
p\in\mathbb{P}
, recall the following definitions:

•

p
is
minimal
if there exists no
q\in\mathbb{P}
such that
q\prec p
,

•

p
is a
successor
if there exists
q\in\mathbb{P}
such that
q\prec p
and there exists no
r\in\mathbb{P}
such that
q\prec r\prec p
,

•

p
is a
countable limit
if
p
is not a successor and there exist
q_{n}\in\mathbb{P}
for
n\in\omega
such that
q_{0}\prec q_{1}\prec\cdots\prec p
and for every
r\prec p
there exists
n\in\omega
such that
r\prec q_{n}
.

The following notion is essentially due to Louveau (see
[
Lo1
, Definition 1.7]
and the remark that precedes it), although his ‘‘official’’ definition is of a (rather complicated) recursive nature, and it is limited to the Borel context. This is one of the fundamental notions needed to state our classification results.
1
1

1

It would have been more elegant to simply define
t(\mathbf{\Gamma})
as the cofinality of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
in
\mathsf{NSD}^{(\xi)}(Z)
. However, we preferred to remain consistent with the original definition.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
Z
be a space, and let
\xi\leq\omega_{1}
. Define

\mathsf{NSD}^{(\xi)}(Z)=\{\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}(Z):\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq\xi\}.

Given a topological pointclass
\mathbf{\Sigma}
, also set
\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}^{(\xi)}(Z)=\{\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z):\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq\xi\}
. Given
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}(Z)
such that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\xi
, view
\mathsf{NSD}^{(\xi)}(Z)
as a partial order under
\subseteq
, then define the
type
of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
as

\left.\begin{array}[]{lcl}&&t(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\text{if }\mathbf{\Gamma}\text{ is minimal in $\mathsf{NSD}^{(\xi)}(Z)$,}\\
1&\text{if }\mathbf{\Gamma}\text{ is a successor in $\mathsf{NSD}^{(\xi)}(Z)$,}\\
2&\text{if }\mathbf{\Gamma}\text{ is a countable limit in $\mathsf{NSD}^{(\xi)}(Z)$,}\\
3&\text{otherwise.}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

Notice that Theorem
3.21
will guarantee that the type is defined under suitable determinacy assumptions. The following proposition (whose trivial proof is left to the reader) collects some basic properties of the type.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
Z
be a space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}(Z)
.

•

t(\mathbf{\Gamma})=0
iff
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\{\varnothing\}
or
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\{Z\}
,

•

t(\mathbf{\Gamma})=t(\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
,

•

If
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=0
then

\left.\begin{array}[]{lcl}&&t(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{if }\mathbf{\Gamma}\text{ is a successor in $\mathsf{NSD}(Z)$,}\\
2&\text{if }\mathbf{\Gamma}\text{ is a countable limit in $\mathsf{NSD}(Z)$,}\\
3&\text{otherwise.}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

We conclude this section by showing that expansions leave the type unchanged (see Corollary
6.4
for the precise statement).

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. Then the function
\Phi_{\xi}:\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)\longrightarrow\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}^{(\xi)}(Z)
defined by setting
\Phi_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}
for
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
is an order-isomorphism with respect to
\subseteq
.

Proof.

Using Theorem
3.25
, one sees that
\Phi_{\xi}
is well-defined and surjective. The fact that
\Phi_{\xi}
is an order-isomorphism is then given by Corollary
3.26
.
∎

Corollary 6.4
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, let
\xi<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
. Then
t(\mathbf{\Gamma})=t(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)})
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.21
, there exists
\eta\leq\omega_{1}
such that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\eta
. First assume that
\eta<\omega_{1}
. By Theorem
3.25
, there exists
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
such that
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\eta)}=\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Notice that
\Phi_{\xi+\eta}\circ\Phi_{\eta}^{-1}:\mathsf{NSD}^{(\eta)}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)\longrightarrow\mathsf{NSD}^{(\xi+\eta)}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
is an order-isomorphism with respect to
\subseteq
by Theorem
6.3
. Also observe that

(\Phi_{\xi+\eta}\circ\Phi_{\eta}^{-1})(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\Phi_{\xi+\eta}(\mathbf{\Lambda})=\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\xi+\eta)}=(\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\eta)})^{(\xi)}=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)},

where the third equality holds by Theorem
3.27
. Since Corollary
3.28
guarantees that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)})=\xi+\eta
, the desired conclusion immediately follows.

Finally, assume that
\eta=\omega_{1}
. We will prove that
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}
, which clearly suffices. By Theorem
3.25
, there exists
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
such that
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\xi\cdot\omega)}=\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Then

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}=(\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\xi\cdot\omega)})^{(\xi)}=\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\xi+\xi\cdot\omega)}=\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\xi\cdot(1+\omega))}=\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\xi\cdot\omega)}=\mathbf{\Gamma},

where the second equality holds by Theorem
3.27
.
∎

6.2.
Separated unions

The following notion will ultimately allow us to explicitly describe the non-selfdual Wadge classes that are successors or countable limits (see Section
6.3
).
2
2

2

According to Wadge, this notion had already been identified by “the classical descriptive set theorists” (see
[
Wa2
, page 187]
).
For some insight on the relationship between separated unions and separated differences, see Lemma
7.16
.

Definition 6.5
.

Let
Z
be a space, let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. Define
\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
to be the collection of all sets of the form

\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(A_{n}\cap U_{n}),

where each
A_{n}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
and the
U_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1+\xi}^{0}(Z)
are pairwise disjoint. A set in this form is called a
separated union
of sets in
\mathbf{\Gamma}
.

As a first step, we will show that this operation produces new non-selfdual Wadge classes from old ones.

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, let
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. Set

\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}\left(\bigcup_{n\in\omega}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\right).

Then
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}(Z)
.
3
3

3

However, it is not necessarily true that
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
.

Proof.

Choose
D\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega)
such that

\mathcal{H}_{D}(A_{0},U_{1},A_{2},U_{3},\ldots)=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}(A_{2k}\cap U_{2k+1})

for all subsets
A_{0},U_{1},A_{2},U_{3},\ldots
of the ambient set. Also fix a function
\sigma:\omega\longrightarrow\omega
such that
\sigma^{-1}(n)
is infinite for every
n\in\omega
. To obtain the desired result, simply apply Corollary
4.16
to the classes
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\sigma(n)}
.
∎

Next, we will show that separated unions are connected to partitioned unions in a rather pleasing way.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
Z
be a zero-dimensional space, let
\xi<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Delta}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
be selfdual. Then

\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Delta})\cap\widecheck{\mathsf{SU}}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Delta})=\mathsf{PU}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Delta}).

Proof.

The inclusion
\supseteq
is trivial. In order to prove the inclusion
\subseteq
, pick
A\in\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Delta})
such that
Z\setminus A\in\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Delta})
. Pick
U_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z)
and
A_{n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}
for
n\in\omega
such that

A=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}(A_{2k}\cap U_{2k})\text{ and }Z\setminus A=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}(A_{2k+1}\cap U_{2k+1}).

Observe that
\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U_{n}=Z
. Therefore, by Theorem
2.4
, there exist
U^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
such that the following conditions hold:

•

U^{\prime}_{n}\subseteq U_{n}
for every
n\in\omega
,

•

U^{\prime}_{m}\cap U^{\prime}_{n}=\varnothing
whenever
m\neq n
,

•

\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U^{\prime}_{n}=Z
.

It is easy to realize that

A=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}(A_{2k}\cap U^{\prime}_{2k})\cup\bigcup_{k\in\omega}((Z\setminus A_{2k+1})\cap U^{\prime}_{2k+1}).

Since
\mathbf{\Delta}
is selfdual, this shows that
A\in\mathsf{PU}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Delta})
.
∎

Recall that, by Theorem
5.8
, exactly one of the Wadge classes in a non-selfdual pair has the separation property. The following result tells us which one, at least in certain cases.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
Z
be a zero-dimensional space, let
\xi<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Delta}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
be selfdual. Set
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Delta})
. Then
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
has the separation property.

Proof.

Pick
A_{0},A_{1}\in\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
such that
A_{0}\cap A_{1}=\varnothing
. Fix
B_{n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}
and
U_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
such that the following conditions hold:

•

U_{2k+i}\cap U_{2j+i}=\varnothing
whenever
k\neq j
and
i<2
,

•

A_{i}=Z\setminus\bigcup_{k\in\omega}(B_{2k+i}\cap U_{2k+i})
.

Using the fact that
A_{0}\cap A_{1}=\varnothing
, one sees that
\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U_{n}=Z
. So, by Theorem
2.4
, there exist
U^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
such that the following conditions hold:

•

U^{\prime}_{n}\subseteq U_{n}
for each
n
,

•

U^{\prime}_{m}\cap U^{\prime}_{n}=\varnothing
whenever
m\neq n
,

•

\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U^{\prime}_{n}=Z
.

Define

C=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}((Z\setminus B_{2k})\cap U^{\prime}_{2k})\cup\bigcup_{k\in\omega}(B_{2k+1}\cap U^{\prime}_{2k+1}).

Notice that
C\in\Delta(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\Delta(\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
because
\mathbf{\Delta}
is selfdual. Therefore, to conclude the proof, it remains to show that
A_{0}\subseteq C\subseteq Z\setminus A_{1}
. We will only show that
A_{0}\subseteq C
, since
A_{1}\subseteq Z\setminus C
can be verified using a similar argument. Pick
x\in A_{0}
. First assume that
x\in U^{\prime}_{2k}
for some
k
. Then
x\notin B_{2k}
, which clearly implies
x\in C
. Now assume that
x\in U^{\prime}_{2k+1}
for some
k
. Then
x\in B_{2k+1}
, otherwise the fact that
A_{0}\cap A_{1}=\varnothing
would be contradicted. It follows that
x\in C
in this case as well.
∎

6.3.
Characterizing small type

The aim of this section is to explicitly describe the non-selfdual Wadge classes of small type (that is, type
1
or
2
), at least in the case of countable level (see Theorem
6.11
). We will often consider
\mathbf{\Delta}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n})
, where the
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}
are intended to be the predecessors of a certain class
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. We remark that this is simply a convenient way to unify the cases in which
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is a successor (all
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}
are the same) and a countable limit (
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{0}\subsetneq\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1}\subsetneq\cdots
).

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
. Set
\mathbf{\Delta}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n})
, and assume that
\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta})\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta})
or
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\widecheck{\mathsf{SU}}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta})
,

(2)

\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
,
\mathbf{\Delta}\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
, and for every
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
such that
\mathbf{\Delta}\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}
either
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}
or
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

First we will prove that
(1)\rightarrow(2)
. Assume that
(1)
holds. Observe that
\mathbf{\Gamma},\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
by Theorem
6.6
plus the assumption that
\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta})\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
. Furthermore, it is clear that
\mathbf{\Delta}\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Now pick
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
such that
\mathbf{\Delta}\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}
. Assume, in order to get a contradiction, that
\mathbf{\Gamma}\nsubseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}
and
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\nsubseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}
. It follows from Lemma
3.3
that
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}\subseteq\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
, hence
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{PU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta})
by Lemma
6.7
. On the other hand, Proposition
3.17
shows that
\mathsf{PU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta})\subseteq\mathsf{PU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime})=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}
. In conclusion, we see that
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}=\mathsf{PU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta})
, which contradicts the fact that
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}
is non-selfdual.

Now assume that
(2)
holds. Using the implication
(1)\rightarrow(2)
, it is easy to see that both of the following conditions hold:

•

\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta})\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
or
\widecheck{\mathsf{SU}}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta})\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
,

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta})
or
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\widecheck{\mathsf{SU}}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta})
.

On the other hand, if both of the inclusions
\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta})\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\widecheck{\mathsf{SU}}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta})
were true, then the fact that
\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta})
is non-selfdual would be contradicted. Similarly, the inclusions
\widecheck{\mathsf{SU}}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta})\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta})
cannot both hold. At this point, it is clear that
(1)
holds.
∎

Before giving the promised characterization, we will need one more preliminary result, which illustrates how separated unions interact with expansions.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. If
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\bigcup_{n\in\omega}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n})\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
then

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}=\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}\left(\bigcup_{n\in\omega}\left(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}^{(\xi)}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n}^{(\xi)}\right)\right).

Proof.

Pick
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}(Z)\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
, where each
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Set

\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SU}_{0}\left(\bigcup_{n\in\omega}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n}\right).

Given a space
W
, we will use the notation
\mathbf{\Delta}(W)=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}(W)\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n}(W))
. Assume that
\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}(Z))\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
.

Claim 1.

\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
.

Proof.
Fix an embedding
j:\omega^{\omega}\longrightarrow Z
. Pick
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Using Theorem
2.4
and Lemma
3.12
, it is possible to find
\widetilde{A}\in\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}(Z))
in
Z
such that
j[A]=\widetilde{A}\cap j[\omega^{\omega}]
. Since
\widetilde{A}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
by assumption, it follows that
A=j^{-1}[\widetilde{A}]\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
.
\blacksquare

Claim 2.

\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)=\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}(W))
whenever
W
is an uncountable Polish space.

Proof.
Pick an uncountable Polish space
W
. Define
\Phi:\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})\longrightarrow\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(W)
by setting
\Phi(\mathbf{\Lambda})=\mathbf{\Lambda}(W)
for
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
, and observe that
\Phi
is an order-isomorphism with respect to
\subseteq
by Theorem
3.14
. Furthermore, Lemma
3.11
shows that
\widecheck{\Phi(\mathbf{\Lambda})}=\Phi(\widecheck{\mathbf{\Lambda}})
for every
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. By Claim 1, it is possible to apply Lemma
6.9
, which gives a characterization that can be expressed using only order-theoretic terms plus the operation of taking the dual class. It follows that either
\Phi(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}(W))
or
\Phi(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\widecheck{\mathsf{SU}}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}(W))
. However, using Lemma
5.2
, Theorem
5.8
and Lemma
6.8
, it is straighforward to check that the second case would lead to a contradiction.
\blacksquare

Now we have all the necessary tools to show that

\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)^{(\xi)}=\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}\left(\bigcup_{n\in\omega}\left(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}(Z)^{(\xi)}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n}(Z)^{(\xi)}\right)\right),

which will conclude the proof by Claim 2. The inclusion
\subseteq
follows easily from Claim 2 and the definition of expansion. In order to prove the other inclusion, pick

A=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}(A_{k}\cap U_{k}),

where each
A_{k}
belongs to some
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}(Z)^{(\xi)}
or
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n}(Z)^{(\xi)}
, and the
U_{k}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z)
are pairwise disjoint. By Lemma
3.31
, we can fix a zero-dimensional Polish space
W
and a
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}
-measurable bijection
f:Z\longrightarrow W
such that each
f[A_{k}]
belongs to some
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}(W)
or
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n}(W)
, and each
f[U_{k}]\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(W)
. Using Claim 2, Lemma
3.29
and Lemma
3.30
, one sees that

A=f^{-1}\left[\bigcup_{k\in\omega}(f[A_{k}]\cap f[U_{k}])\right]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}(Z)=\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)^{(\xi)},

as desired.
∎

Theorem 6.11
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
be such that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\xi<\omega_{1}
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

t(\mathbf{\Gamma})\in\{1,2\}
,

•

There exist
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\in\mathsf{NSD}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
such that each
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n})\geq\xi
and either
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Delta})
or
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\widecheck{\mathsf{SU}}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Delta})
, where
\mathbf{\Delta}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n})
.

Proof.

The case
\xi=0
can be easily obtained as a consequence of Lemma
6.9
. The general case will then follow, using the methods of Section
3.4
in conjunction with Corollary
6.4
and Lemma
6.10
.
∎

Chapter 7
Wadge theory: closure and preservation

This chapter is the Wadge-theoretic core of this monograph. In fact, the results of Section
7.5
show that suitable assumptions concerning the level, type and separation property will ensure closure under separated unions. These closure properties will be an essential ingredient for our existence results (see Theorems
9.13
and
10.3
). Along the way, we will show that the operation of separated differences preserves the property of having small type and the separation property. The results concerning good Wadge classes (which were originally obtained in
[
CMM1
]
) will ultimately ensure that Steel’s Theorem
8.9
can be applied to the Wadge classes that are relevant to our classification (see Theorem
9.7
and Lemma
9.8
).

7.1.
Closure properties: preliminaries

As will become increasingly clear, what we need from Wadge theory are closure properties. These properties are spelled out in the following definition, while the subsequent lemmas will be useful later on, towards obtaining the more sophisticated closure properties of Section
7.5
.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
Z
be a space, let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
.

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}
is
closed under intersections with
\mathbf{\Pi}_{1+\xi}
sets
if
A\cap B\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
whenever
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
B\in\mathbf{\Pi}_{1+\xi}(Z)
.

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}
is
closed under unions with
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1+\xi}
sets
if
A\cup B\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
whenever
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
B\in\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1+\xi}(Z)
.

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}
is
closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}
if
\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\mathbf{\Gamma}
.

The next four results show that these closure properties are well-behaved with respect to relativization and expansion.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
and
W
be uncountable zero-dimensional Borel spaces, let
\xi<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is closed under intersections with
\mathbf{\Pi}_{1+\xi}
sets (respectively under unions with
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1+\xi}
sets),

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
is closed under intersections with
\mathbf{\Pi}_{1+\xi}
sets (respectively under unions with
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1+\xi}
sets).

Proof.

We will only prove one of the implications, since the other cases are perfectly analogous. So assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is closed under intersections with
\mathbf{\Pi}_{1+\xi}
sets. Without loss of generality, assume that
W
is a subspace of
Z
. Pick
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
and
B\in\mathbf{\Pi}_{1+\xi}(W)
. By Lemma
3.12
, there exist
\widetilde{A}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
and
\widetilde{B}\in\mathbf{\Pi}_{1+\xi}(Z)
such that
\widetilde{A}\cap W=A
and
\widetilde{B}\cap W=B
. Notice that
\widetilde{A}\cap\widetilde{B}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
by assumption. Therefore
A\cap B=(\widetilde{A}\cap\widetilde{B})\cap W\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
by Lemma
3.12
.
∎

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. If
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
is closed under intersections with
\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1}
sets (respectively under unions with
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}
sets) then
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}
is closed under intersections with
\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1+\xi}
sets (respectively under unions with
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1+\xi}
sets).

Proof.

We will only prove one of the implications, since the other case is perfectly analogous. Pick
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
that is closed under intersections with
\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1}
sets, where
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Let
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)^{(\xi)}
and
B\in\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z)
. By Lemma
3.31
, it is possible to fix a zero-dimensional Polish space
W
and a
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}
-measurable bijection
f:Z\longrightarrow W
such that
f[A]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
and
f[B]\in\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1}(W)
. Observe that
f[A]\cap f[B]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
by Lemma
7.2
. Therefore

A\cap B=f^{-1}[f[A]\cap f[B]]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}(Z)=\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)^{(\xi)}

by Lemmas
3.29
and
3.30
, as desired.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
and
W
be uncountable zero-dimensional Borel spaces, let
\xi<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}
,

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}
.

Proof.

We will only prove one of the implications, since the other case is perfectly analogous. So assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}
. Without loss of generality, assume that
W
is a subspace of
Z
. Pick
A_{n}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
and pairwise disjoint
U_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1+\xi}(W)
for
n\in\omega
. Using Theorem
2.4
, it is possible to find pairwise disjoint
\widetilde{U}_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1+\xi}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
such that each
\widetilde{U}_{n}\cap W=U_{n}
. On the other hand, by Lemma
3.12
, there exist
\widetilde{A}_{n}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
such that each
\widetilde{A}_{n}\cap W=A_{n}
. To conclude the proof, observe that

\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(A_{n}\cap U_{n})=W\cap\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\widetilde{A}_{n}\cap\widetilde{U}_{n})\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(W),

by Lemma
3.12
, since
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}
.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\xi<\omega_{1}
. If
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{0}
then
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
be closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{0}
, where
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Pick
A_{n}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)^{(\xi)}
and pairwise disjoint
U_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
. By Lemma
3.31
, we can fix a zero-dimensional Polish space
W
and a
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}
-measurable bijection
f:Z\longrightarrow W
such that
f[A_{n}]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
and
f[U_{n}]\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(W)
for each
n
. Notice that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{0}
by Lemma
7.4
. Therefore

\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(A_{n}\cap U_{n})=f^{-1}\left[\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(f[A_{n}]\cap f[U_{n}])\right]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(\xi)}(Z)=\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)^{(\xi)}

by Lemmas
3.29
and
3.30
, as desired.
∎

We conclude this section with two elementary results in the same vein (the straightforward proof of Lemma
7.6
is left to the reader).

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
Z
be a space, let
\xi<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Delta}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
. Set
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Delta})
. Then
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}
.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
Z
be a space, let
1\leq\eta<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Delta},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
. Assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{0}
. Set
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})
. Then
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{0}
.

Proof.

Pick
A\in\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
. Fix
A_{n}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
and pairwise disjoint
U_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
such that
A=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(A_{n}\cap U_{n})
. Given
n\in\omega
, fix
A_{n,\mu,k}
,
V_{n,\mu,k}
and
A_{n}^{\ast}
for
\mu<\eta
and
k\in\omega
as in Definition
3.32
such that

A_{n}=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}((V_{n,\mu,k}:\mu<\eta,k\in\omega),(A_{n,\mu,k}:\mu<\eta,k\in\omega),A_{n}^{\ast}).

Set
V^{\prime}_{n,\mu,k}=V_{n,\mu,k}\cap U_{n}
for
(n,k)\in\omega\times\omega
and
\mu<\eta
. Notice that
V^{\prime}_{n,\mu,k}\cap V^{\prime}_{m,\mu,j}=\varnothing
whenever
(n,k)\neq(m,j)
. Also set
A^{\ast}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(A_{n}^{\ast}\cap U_{n})
, and observe that
A^{\ast}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
. It is straightforward to check that

A=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}((V^{\prime}_{n,\mu,k}:\mu<\eta,(n,k)\in\omega\times\omega),(A_{n,\mu,k}:\mu<\eta,(n,k)\in\omega\times\omega),A^{\ast}),

which concludes the proof.
∎

7.2.
Closure properties: good Wadge classes

The following key notion (see
[
CMM1
, Definition 12.1]
) is essentially due to van Engelen, although he did not give it a name.
1
1

1

One important difference is that van Engelen’s treatment of this notion is fundamentally tied to Louveau’s classification of the Borel Wadge classes from
[
Lo1
]
, hence it is limited to the Borel context.
Good Wadge classes will enable us to define a well-behaved notion of exact topological complexity (see Section
9.1
). Furthermore, they will ultimately allow us to apply Theorem
8.9
(since homogeneous spaces have good complexity by Theorem
9.7
, and good Wadge classes are reasonably closed by Lemma
9.8
).

Definition 7.8

(Carroy, Medini, Müller)
.

Let
Z
be a space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}
be a Wadge class in
Z
. We will say that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is
good
if the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

\mathbf{\Gamma}
is non-selfdual,

(2)

\Delta(\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(Z)))\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
,

(3)

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq 1
.

Using Proposition
4.19
and Theorem
3.25
, one can easily show that
\mathsf{D}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(Z))
and
\widecheck{\mathsf{D}}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(Z))
are examples of good Wadge classes whenever
Z
is an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space and
\omega\leq\eta<\omega_{1}
. The next proposition shows that, for all practical purposes, the requirement
(2)
can be substituted by a weaker requirement.

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
be such that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq 1
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

\Delta(\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(Z)))\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
,

•

\mathsf{D}_{n}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(Z))\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
for every
n\in\omega
.

Proof.

In order to prove the non-trivial implication, assume that
\mathsf{D}_{n}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(Z))\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
for every
n\in\omega
. By Theorem
3.25
, we can fix
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
such that
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(1)}=\mathbf{\Gamma}
. It follows from Proposition
4.19
and Corollary
3.26
that
\mathsf{D}_{n}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))\subseteq\mathbf{\Lambda}
for each
n
, hence
\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))\subseteq\mathbf{\Lambda}
or
\widecheck{\mathsf{D}}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))\subseteq\mathbf{\Lambda}
by Theorem
3.8
. To conclude the proof, apply Proposition
4.19
and Corollary
3.26
one more time.
∎

Next, we will show that good Wadge classes are well-behaved with respect to relativization.

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
and
W
be uncountable zero-dimensional Polish spaces, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is a good Wadge class,

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
is a good Wadge class.

Proof.

First observe that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
are non-selfdual by Theorem
3.13
. Furthermore, Theorem
3.20
guarantees that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z))\geq 1
iff
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}(W))\geq 1
. Therefore, by Proposition
7.9
, it remains to show that
\mathsf{D}_{n}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(Z))\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
for every
n\in\omega
iff
\mathsf{D}_{n}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(W))\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
for every
n\in\omega
. In order to see this, use the methods of
[
CMM2
, Section 9]
and
[
CMM2
, Lemma 21.5]
to obtain
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{n}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
for
n\in\omega
such that each
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{n}(Z)=\mathsf{D}_{n}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(Z))
and each
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{n}(W)=\mathsf{D}_{n}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(W))
. Then, the desired equivalence will follow from Theorem
3.14
.
∎

The remainder of this section is devoted to obtaining the fundamental closure properties of good Wadge classes (see Corollary
7.13
), as a particular case of a result of independent interest (see Theorem
7.12
). We remark that, for the uniqueness part of the classification, no further Wadge-theoretic analysis is required. However, in order to obtain the existence results of Sections
9.4
and
10.2
, we will need the more sophisticated closure properties discussed in Section
7.5
.

The following result first appeared as
[
CMM1
, Lemma 12.3]
, and it generalizes
[
AHN
, Lemma 3.6.a]
. However, we will give a new proof, which relies on separated differences and Theorem
3.35
instead of
[
AHN
]
. Corollary
7.13
first appeared as
[
CMM1
, Theorem 12.4]
.

Lemma 7.11

(Carroy, Medini, Müller)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
. Assume that
\mathsf{D}_{n}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
whenever
1\leq n<\omega
. Then
\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the following closure properties:

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under intersections with
\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1}
sets,

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under unions with
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}
sets.

Proof.

It will be enough to prove the first statement, since the second one will follow by considering the dual class. By Lemma
3.19
, we can assume without loss of generality that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=0
. Fix
\eta
,
\mathbf{\Delta}
,
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}
for
n\in\omega
as in Theorem
3.35
, so that
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})
. Pick
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
C\in\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1}(Z)
. Write

A=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}((U_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),(A_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),A^{\ast}),

where the
U_{\mu,n}
,
A_{\mu,n}
and
A^{\ast}
are as in Definition
3.32
.

First assume that
\mathbf{\Delta}=\{\varnothing\}\cup\{Z\}
, hence either
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}=\{\varnothing\}
or
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}=\{Z\}
. Using Lemma
3.34
and the fact that the difference hierarchy is strictly increasing (see
[
Ke
, Exercise 22.26]
), one sees that
\eta\geq\omega
. In particular, the following definitions make sense:

•

U^{\prime}_{0,0}=Z\setminus C
,

•

U^{\prime}_{0,n}=\varnothing
for
1\leq n<\omega
,

•

A^{\prime}_{0,n}=\varnothing
for
n\in\omega
,

•

U^{\prime}_{1+\mu,n}=U_{\mu,n}
for
\mu<\eta
and
n<\omega
,

•

A^{\prime}_{1+\mu,n}=A_{\mu,n}
for
\mu<\eta
and
n<\omega
.

It is easy to realize that

A\cap C=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}((U^{\prime}_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),(A^{\prime}_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),A^{\ast}),

which shows that
A\cap C\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
.

Now assume that
\mathbf{\Delta}\supsetneq\{\varnothing\}\cup\{Z\}
. In particular, since each
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n})\geq 1
, one can use Lemma
3.19
to obtain that
\mathbf{\Delta}
is closed under intersections with
\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1}
sets. By Theorem
2.4
, it is possible to find
U^{\prime}_{0,n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
such that the following conditions hold:

•

U^{\prime}_{0,0}\subseteq Z\setminus C
,

•

U^{\prime}_{0,1+n}\subseteq U_{0,n}
for
n\in\omega
,

•

U^{\prime}_{0,m}\cap U^{\prime}_{0,n}=\varnothing
whenever
m\neq n
,

•

\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U^{\prime}_{0,n}=(Z\setminus C)\cup\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U_{0,n}
.

Also make the following definitions:

•

A^{\prime}_{0,0}=\varnothing
,

•

A^{\prime}_{0,1+n}=A_{0,n}\cap C
for
n<\omega
,

•

U^{\prime}_{\mu,n}=U_{\mu,n}
for
1\leq\mu<\eta
and
n<\omega
,

•

A^{\prime}_{\mu,n}=A_{\mu,n}
for
1\leq\mu<\eta
and
n<\omega
.

It is easy to realize that

A\cap C=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}((U^{\prime}_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),(A^{\prime}_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),A^{\ast}),

which concludes the proof.
∎

Theorem 7.12
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, let
\xi<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
. Assume that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq\xi
and
\mathsf{D}_{n}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z))\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
whenever
1\leq n<\omega
. Then
\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the following closure properties:

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under intersections with
\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1+\xi}
sets,

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under unions with
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}
sets.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.25
, we can fix
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
such that
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\xi)}=\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Notice that

\mathsf{D}_{n}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))^{(\xi)}=\mathsf{D}_{n}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1+\xi}(Z))\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(\xi)}

for every
n\in\omega
, where the first equality holds by Proposition
4.19
. It follows from Corollary
3.26
that
\mathsf{D}_{n}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))\subseteq\mathbf{\Lambda}
for every
n\in\omega
. Therefore, Lemma
7.11
guarantees that
\mathbf{\Lambda}
is closed under intersections with
\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1}
sets and unions with
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}
sets. To conclude the proof, apply Lemma
7.3
.
∎

Corollary 7.13

(Carroy, Medini, Müller)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
be a good Wadge class in
Z
. Then
\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the following closure properties:

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under intersections with
\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{2}
sets,

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under unions with
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}
sets.

7.3.
Preservation of small type under separated differences

The aim of this section is to show that the operation
\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},-)
preserves the property of having small type for classes of level
0
(see Theorem
7.15
