for a precise statement). This is one of the ingredients needed to obtain the crucial closure properties of Section
7.5
. We begin with a technical lemma.

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
Z
be a zero-dimensional space, let
1\leq\eta<\omega_{1}
, let
\mathbf{\Delta}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
be selfdual, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}^{\ast}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
. Set
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}^{\ast})
for
n\in\omega
. Then

\mathsf{SU}_{0}\left(\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n})\right)=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}\left(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathsf{SU}_{0}\left(\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}^{\ast}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n}^{\ast})\right)\right).

Proof.

For notational convenience, set

\mathbf{\Delta}\!^{\ast}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}^{\ast}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n}^{\ast}).

Since
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\subseteq\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}\!^{\ast}))
for each
n
, it is clear that
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}^{\ast}\subseteq\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}\!^{\ast})
for each
n
. Similarly, using Lemma
3.33
and the assumption that
\mathbf{\Delta}
is selfdual, one sees that
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n}\subseteq\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}\!^{\ast}))
for each
n
. Since
\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}\!^{\ast}))
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{0}
by Lemmas
7.6
and
7.7
, the inclusion
\subseteq
follows.

In order to prove the inclusion
\supseteq
, pick
A\in\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}\!^{\ast}))
. Fix
A_{\mu,n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}
,
U_{\mu,n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
and
A^{\ast}\in\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}\!^{\ast})
as in Definition
3.32
such that

A=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}((U_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),(A_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),A^{\ast}).

Also fix
A_{k}^{\ast}\in\mathbf{\Delta}\!^{\ast}
and pairwise disjoint
V_{k}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
for
k\in\omega
such that

A^{\ast}=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}(A_{k}^{\ast}\cap V_{k}).

Set
V_{-1}=\bigcup\{U_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta\text{ and }n\in\omega\}
. By Theorem
2.4
, there exist
V^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
for
-1\leq k<\omega
satisfying the following conditions:

•

V^{\prime}_{k}\subseteq V_{k}
for
-1\leq k<\omega
,

•

V^{\prime}_{j}\cap V^{\prime}_{k}=\varnothing
whenever
j\neq k
,

•

\bigcup_{-1\leq k<\omega}V^{\prime}_{k}=\bigcup_{-1\leq k<\omega}V_{k}
.

Also choose an arbitrary
A_{-1}^{\ast}\in\mathbf{\Delta}\!^{\ast}
. It is straightforward to check that

A=\bigcup_{-1\leq k<\omega}(\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}((U_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),(A_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),A_{k}^{\ast})\cap V^{\prime}_{k}).

Finally, using Lemma
3.33
and the assumption that
\mathbf{\Delta}
is selfdual, one sees that the right-hand side belongs to
\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n}))
, as desired.
∎

Theorem 7.15
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, let
1\leq\eta<\omega_{1}
, let
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{n}\in\mathsf{NSD}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
be such that each
\ell(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{n})\geq 1
, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}\in\mathsf{NSD}(Z)
be such that
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}\subseteq\mathbf{\Delta}
, where
\mathbf{\Delta}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Lambda}}_{n})
. Set
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})
, and assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
. If
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})=0
and
t(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})\in\{1,2\}
then
t(\mathbf{\Gamma})\in\{1,2\}
.

Proof.

Assume that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})=0
and
t(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})\in\{1,2\}
. By Theorem
6.11
, there exist
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}^{\ast}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
such that

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}=\mathsf{SU}_{0}\left(\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\ast}_{n})\right)\text{ or }\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\ast}=\mathsf{SU}_{0}\left(\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\ast}_{n})\right).

Set
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}^{\ast})
for
n\in\omega
. Observe that Theorem
3.35
guarantees that each
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
. Furthermore, it guarantees that
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
and
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=0
. Since

\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SU}_{0}\left(\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n})\right)\text{ or }\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}=\mathsf{SU}_{0}\left(\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n})\right)

by Lemmas
3.33
and
7.14
, the desired conclusion follows from Theorem
6.11
.
∎

7.4.
Preservation of the separation property under separated differences

The aim of this section is to show that the operation
\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},-)
preserves the separation property (see Theorem
7.18
for a precise statement). This is one of the ingredients needed to obtain the crucial closure properties of Section
7.5
. We begin with a technical lemma.

Lemma 7.16
.

Let
Z
be a zero-dimensional space, let
1\leq\eta<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Delta}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
be selfdual with
Z\in\mathbf{\Delta}
. Then there exists a selfdual
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
such that

\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{\varnothing\})=\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}).

Proof.

If
\eta=1
, just set
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}=\mathbf{\Delta}
. Next, assume that
\eta=\eta^{\prime}+1
, where
1\leq\eta^{\prime}<\omega_{1}
. Set
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathbf{\Delta})
, and observe that
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}
is selfdual by Lemma
3.33
. To prove the inclusion
\subseteq
, pick
A\in\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{\varnothing\})
. Fix
A_{\mu,n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}
and
U_{\mu,n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
as in Definition
3.32
such that

A=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}((U_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),(A_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),\varnothing).

Set
A_{k}=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta^{\prime}}((U_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta^{\prime},n\in\omega),(A_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta^{\prime},n\in\omega),A_{\eta^{\prime},k})
and

V_{k}=U_{\eta^{\prime},k}\cup\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu<\eta^{\prime}\\
n\in\omega\end{subarray}}U_{\mu,n}

for
k\in\omega
. By Theorem
2.4
, it is possible to find
V^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
for
k\in\omega
satisfying the following conditions:

•

V^{\prime}_{k}\subseteq V_{k}
for
k\in\omega
,

•

V^{\prime}_{j}\cap V^{\prime}_{k}=\varnothing
whenever
j\neq k
,

•

\bigcup_{k\in\omega}V^{\prime}_{k}=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}V_{k}
.

It is easy to realize that
A=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(A_{k}\cap V^{\prime}_{k})
, which shows that
A\in\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime})
, as desired. In order to prove the inclusion
\supseteq
, first observe that
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{\varnothing\})
. It follows that

\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{\varnothing\}))=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{\varnothing\}),

where the equality holds by Lemma
7.7
, since
\{\varnothing\}
is obviously closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{0}
.

To conclude the proof, assume that
\eta
is a limit ordinal. Set

\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}=\bigcup_{1\leq\eta^{\prime}<\eta}(\mathsf{SD}_{\eta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{\varnothing\})\cup\widecheck{\mathsf{SD}}_{\eta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{\varnothing\})),

and observe that
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}
is selfdual. To prove the inclusion
\subseteq
, pick
A\in\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{\varnothing\})
. Fix
A_{\mu,n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}
and
U_{\mu,n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
as in Definition
3.32
such that

A=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}((U_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),(A_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),\varnothing).

Set
V_{\eta^{\prime}}=\bigcup\{U_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta^{\prime}\text{ and }n\in\omega\}
for
1\leq\eta^{\prime}<\eta
. By Theorem
2.4
, it is possible to find
V^{\prime}_{\eta^{\prime}}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
for
1\leq\eta^{\prime}<\eta
satisfying the following conditions:

•

V^{\prime}_{\eta^{\prime}}\subseteq V_{\eta^{\prime}}
for
\eta^{\prime}<\eta
,

•

V^{\prime}_{\eta^{\prime}}\cap V^{\prime}_{\eta^{\prime\prime}}=\varnothing
whenever
\eta^{\prime}\neq\eta^{\prime\prime}
,

•

\bigcup_{1\leq\eta^{\prime}<\eta}V^{\prime}_{\eta^{\prime}}=\bigcup_{1\leq\eta^{\prime}<\eta}V_{\eta^{\prime}}
.

Finally, set

A_{\eta^{\prime}}=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta^{\prime}}((U_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta^{\prime},n\in\omega),(A_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta^{\prime},n\in\omega),\varnothing)

for
1\leq\eta^{\prime}<\eta
. It is easy to realize that

A=\bigcup_{1\leq\eta^{\prime}<\eta}(A_{\eta^{\prime}}\cap V^{\prime}_{\eta^{\prime}}),

which shows that
A\in\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime})
, as desired. In order to prove the inclusion
\supseteq
, the argument given above will work, provided that
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{\varnothing\})
. To see this, use the fact that

\widecheck{\mathsf{SD}}_{\eta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{\varnothing\})\subseteq\mathsf{SD}_{\eta^{\prime}+1}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{\varnothing\})

for each
\eta^{\prime}
, which follows from Lemma
3.33
and the assumption that
Z\in\mathbf{\Delta}
.
∎

While Lemma
7.16
will be sufficient for most applications, in the proof of Theorem
7.20
we will need the following more precise version. However, their proofs are essentially the same.

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, let
1\leq\eta<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\in\mathsf{NSD}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
be such that each
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n})\geq 1
. Set
\mathbf{\Delta}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n})
, and assume that
\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{\varnothing\})\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
. Then there exist
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
such that

\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{\varnothing\})=\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}),

where
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\prime}_{n})
.

Proof.

If
\eta=1
, set
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{n}=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}
for
n\in\omega
. If
\eta=\eta^{\prime}+1
, where
1\leq\eta^{\prime}<\omega_{1}
, set
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{n}=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n})
for
n\in\omega
. If
\eta
is a limit ordinal, set
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\eta^{\prime}}=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{\varnothing\})
for every
\eta^{\prime}
such that
1\leq\eta^{\prime}<\eta
. To verify that
\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\{\varnothing\})=\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime})
, proceed as in the proof of Lemma
7.16
. To see that each
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
and each
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\eta^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
, apply Theorem
3.35
.
∎

Theorem 7.18
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, let
1\leq\eta<\omega_{1}
, let
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{n}\in\mathsf{NSD}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
be such that each
\ell(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{n})\geq 1
, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}\in\mathsf{NSD}(Z)
be such that
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}\subseteq\mathbf{\Delta}
, where
\mathbf{\Delta}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Lambda}}_{n})
. Set
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})
, and assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)
. If
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
has the separation property then
\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the separation property.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
has the separation property. Pick disjoint
A_{0},A_{1}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Given
i<2
, fix
U_{\mu,2k+i}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
and
A_{\mu,2k+i}\in\mathbf{\Delta}
for
\mu<\eta
and
k\in\omega
as in Definition
3.32
such that

A_{i}=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}((U_{\mu,2k+i}:\mu<\eta,k\in\omega),(A_{\mu,2k+i}:\mu<\eta,k\in\omega),A_{i}^{\ast}).

Set
U_{i}=\bigcup\{U_{\mu,2k+i}:\mu<\eta\text{ and }k\in\omega\}
for
i<2
.

First assume that
\mathsf{D}_{n}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
whenever
1\leq n<\omega
. Then, by Lemma
7.11
, we can assume without loss of generality that
A_{i}^{\ast}\cap U_{i}=\varnothing
for each
i
. It follows that
A_{0}^{\ast}\cap A_{1}^{\ast}=\varnothing
. Therefore, since
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
has the separation property, we can fix
B^{\ast}\in\Delta(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})
such that
A_{0}^{\ast}\subseteq B^{\ast}\subseteq Z\setminus A_{1}^{\ast}
. Given
\mu<\eta
, by Theorem
2.4
there exist
U^{\prime}_{\mu,n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z)
for
n\in\omega
satisfying the following conditions:

•

U^{\prime}_{\mu,n}\subseteq U_{\mu,n}
for each
n
,

•

U^{\prime}_{\mu,m}\cap U^{\prime}_{\mu,n}=\varnothing
whenever
m\neq n
,

•

\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U^{\prime}_{\mu,n}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U_{\mu,n}
.

Also set
B_{\mu,2k}=A_{\mu,2k}
and
B_{\mu,2k+1}=Z\setminus A_{\mu,2k+1}
for
\mu<\eta
and
k\in\omega
, then define

B=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}((U^{\prime}_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),(B_{\mu,n}:\mu<\eta,n\in\omega),B^{\ast}).

Since
B\in\Delta(\mathbf{\Gamma})
by Lemma
3.33
, it remains to verify that
A_{0}\subseteq B\subseteq Z\setminus A_{1}
. We will only show that
A_{0}\subseteq B
, as
A_{1}\subseteq Z\setminus B
can be verified using a similar argument. So pick
x\in A_{0}
. Given
\mu\leq\eta
, set

V_{\mu}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu^{\prime}<\mu\\
n\in\omega\end{subarray}}U_{\mu^{\prime},n}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu^{\prime}<\mu\\
n\in\omega\end{subarray}}U^{\prime}_{\mu^{\prime},n}.

Case 1.

x\in A_{0}^{\ast}\setminus V_{\eta}
.

In this case, it is clear that
x\in B^{\ast}\setminus V_{\eta}
, hence
x\in B
.

Case 2.

x\in A_{0}^{\ast}\cap V_{\eta}
.

Fix the minimal
\mu<\eta
such that
x\in\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U^{\prime}_{\mu,n}
, then fix
n\in\omega
such that
x\in U^{\prime}_{\mu,n}
. If we had
n=2k
for some
k\in\omega
, then we would have
x\in U^{\prime}_{\mu,2k}\subseteq U_{\mu,2k}
, contradicting the assumption that
A_{0}^{\ast}\cap U_{0}=\varnothing
. Therefore, we can fix
k\in\omega
such that
x\in U^{\prime}_{\mu,2k+1}
. Notice that
x\notin A_{\mu,2k+1}
, otherwise we would have
x\in A_{1}
by the minimality of
\mu
, contradicting the fact that
A_{0}\cap A_{1}=\varnothing
. At this point, it is clear that
x\in B
.

Case 3.

x\notin A_{0}^{\ast}
.

This means that we can fix
\mu<\eta
and
k\in\omega
such that

x\in A_{\mu,2k}\cap U_{\mu,2k}\setminus\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu^{\prime}<\mu\\
j\in\omega\end{subarray}}U_{\mu^{\prime},2j}.

First assume that
x\notin V_{\mu}
. Since

x\in U_{\mu,2k}\subseteq\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U_{\mu,n}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U^{\prime}_{\mu,n},

we can fix
n\in\omega
such that
x\in U^{\prime}_{\mu,n}
. If
n=2j
for some
j\in\omega
then
j=k
, because otherwise
U_{\mu,2j}\cap U_{\mu,2k}=\varnothing
. On the other hand, if
n=2j+1
for some
j\in\omega
then
x\notin A_{\mu,n}
, otherwise it would follow that
x\in A_{1}
. In either case, it is clear that
x\in B
, as desired. Now assume that
\mu^{\prime}<\mu
is minimal such that
x\in\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U^{\prime}_{\mu^{\prime},n}
. Fix
n\in\omega
such that
x\in U^{\prime}_{\mu^{\prime},n}
, and observe that
n=2j+1
for some
j\in\omega
. Furthermore, we must have
x\notin A_{\mu^{\prime},2j+1}
, otherwise it would follow that
x\in A_{1}
. Therefore
x\in B
in this case as well.

Finally, assume that
\mathsf{D}_{n}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))\nsubseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
, where
1\leq n<\omega
. Then
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}\subseteq\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(Z)
by Lemma
3.3
. Therefore, by Theorem
3.8
, one of the following cases must hold:

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}=\{\varnothing\}
or
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}=\{Z\}
,

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}=\mathsf{D}_{m}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))
or
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}=\widecheck{\mathsf{D}}_{m}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))
, where
1\leq m<\omega
.

However, the case
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}=\{\varnothing\}
contradicts the assumption that
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
has the separation property. Furthermore, since each
\widecheck{\mathsf{D}}_{m}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))
has the separation property by Lemmas
3.34
,
6.8
and
7.16
, the case
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}=\mathsf{D}_{m}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))
would lead to a contradiction by Theorem
5.8
. By Lemma
6.8
, in order to conclude the proof, it will be enough to show that
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\widecheck{\mathsf{SU}}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime})
for some selfdual
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
. In the case
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}=\{Z\}
, this clearly follows from Lemmas
3.33
and
7.16
. Now assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}=\widecheck{\mathsf{D}}_{m}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))
, where
1\leq m<\omega
. Observe that
t(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})=1
by Theorem
3.8
. Furthermore, it follows from Lemma
3.34
and Theorem
3.35
that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})=0
. By Theorems
6.11
and
7.15
, this means that there exists a selfdual
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
such that either
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime})
or
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\widecheck{\mathsf{SU}}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime})
. Assume, in order to get a contradiction, that
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime})
. Notice that
\mathsf{D}_{m}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(Z))
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{0}
by Lemmas
3.34
,
7.6
and
7.16
. It follows from Lemmas
3.33
and
7.7
that
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{0}
. Hence

\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\widecheck{\mathsf{SU}}_{0}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}))=\mathsf{SU}_{0}(\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}})=\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}},

which contradicts the fact that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is non-selfdual.
∎

7.5.
Closure properties: the main results

This section is the culmination (and the conclusion) of the purely Wadge-theoretic portion of this article.

Theorem 7.19
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, let
\xi<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
. Assume that the following conditions hold:

•

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\xi
,

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}
does not have the separation property.

Then
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}
.

Proof.

Using the methods of Section
3.4
and Lemma
7.5
, one sees that the case
\xi=0
implies the general case. So assume, in order to get a contradiction, that the desired result does not hold in this particular case. By Theorem
3.4
, we can assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is a
\subseteq
-minimal counterexample (in other words,
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=0
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}
does not have the separation property, but
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is not closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{0}
). Fix
\eta
,
\mathbf{\Delta}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
as given by Theorem
3.35
such that
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})
. Notice that
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
does not have the separation property, otherwise
\mathbf{\Gamma}
would have the separation property by Theorem
7.18
. Similarly, Lemma
7.7
shows that
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
is not closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{0}
. Furthermore, as
\mathbf{\Delta}
is selfdual and
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}\subseteq\mathbf{\Delta}\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
, one sees that
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}\subsetneq\mathbf{\Gamma}
. It follows that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})\geq 1
, otherwise the minimality of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
would be contradicted. In particular,
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{0}
(notice that
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}\neq\{Z\}
because
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
does not have the separation property), which is a contradiction.
∎

Theorem 7.20
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space, let
\xi<\omega_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
. Assume that the following conditions hold:

•

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\xi
,

•

t(\mathbf{\Gamma})=3
.

Then
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{\xi}
.

Proof.

Using the methods of Section
3.4
, Corollary
6.4
and Lemma
7.5
, one sees that the case
\xi=0
implies the general case. So assume, in order to get a contradiction, that the desired result does not hold in this particular case. By Theorem
3.4
, we can assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is a
\subseteq
-minimal counterexample (in other words,
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=0
and
t(\mathbf{\Gamma})=3
, but
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is not closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{0}
). Fix
\eta
,
\mathbf{\Delta}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
as given by Theorem
3.35
such that
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathsf{SD}_{\eta}(\mathbf{\Delta},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})
. Observe that
t(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})\neq 0
, otherwise we would have
t(\mathbf{\Gamma})\in\{1,2\}
by Lemma
3.33
, Theorem
6.11
and Lemma
7.17
. To conclude the proof, we will show that the cases
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})=0
and
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})\geq 1
both lead to a contradiction.

First assume that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})=0
. Notice that this implies that
t(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})=3
, otherwise we would have
t(\mathbf{\Gamma})\in\{1,2\}
by Theorem
7.15
. Similarly, Lemma
7.7
shows that
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
is not closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{0}
. Since
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}\subsetneq\mathbf{\Gamma}
, this contradicts the minimality of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Finally, assume that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})\geq 1
. In particular,
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{0}
(notice that
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast}\neq\{Z\}
because
t(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\ast})\neq 0
). By Lemma
7.7
, this contradicts the assumption that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is not closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{0}
.
∎

Chapter 8
Knaster-Reichbach covers and a theorem of Steel

The purpose of this chapter is to give a complete proof of Steel’s Theorem
8.9
, which is the fundamental tool for obtaining the uniqueness result. To this end, we will develop the machinery of Knaster-Reichbach covers and systems, which enables one to construct homeomorphisms between meager (or, dually, comeager) subspaces of suitable zero-dimensional Polish spaces by a topological version of the classical back-and-forth argument. The embeddings needed to carry out this argument will be provided by Harrington’s Lemma
8.8
, which cleverly employs the auxiliary notion of a reasonably closed Wadge class. It will be convenient to use Wadge-theoretic language, but the results of this chapter are stated in such a way that no determinacy assumptions will be necessary.

8.1.
Knaster-Reichbach covers

The following definition is
[
vE3
, Definition 3.2.1]
, and it is based on ideas from the classical article
[
KR
]
.

Definition 8.1

(van Engelen)
.

Let
Z
and
W
be spaces, let
E\subseteq Z
and
F\subseteq W
be closed nowhere dense, and let
h:E\longrightarrow F
be a homeomorphism. We will say that a triple
(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V},\psi)
is a
Knaster-Reichbach cover
(
\mathsf{KR}
-cover) for
(Z\setminus E,W\setminus F,h)
if the following conditions hold:

•

\mathcal{U}
is a partition of
Z\setminus E
consisting of clopen subsets of
Z
,

•

\mathcal{V}
is a partition of
W\setminus F
consisting of clopen subsets of
W
,

•

\psi:\mathcal{U}\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}
is a bijection,

•

If
f:Z\longrightarrow W
is a bijection such that
h\subseteq f
and
f[U]=\psi(U)
for every
U\in\mathcal{U}
, then
f
is continuous on
E
and
f^{-1}
is continuous on
F
.

Given such a
\mathsf{KR}
-cover and a bijection
f:Z\longrightarrow W
, we will say that
f

respects

\psi
if
f[U]=\psi(U)
for every
U\in\mathcal{U}
.

The following result is
[
vE3
, Lemma 3.2.2]
, and it will be a key ingredient at the inductive step in the proof of Theorem
8.9
. The proof given here is inspired by
[
vM1
, Theorem 3.1]
.

Lemma 8.2

(van Engelen)
.

Let
Z
and
W
be zero-dimensional spaces, let
E\subseteq Z
and
F\subseteq W
be closed nowhere dense, and let
h:E\longrightarrow F
be a homeomorphism. Then there exists a
\mathsf{KR}
-cover for
(Z\setminus E,W\setminus F,h)
.

Proof.

The case in which
E
and
F
are empty is trivial, so assume that
E
and
F
are non-empty. Assume without loss of generality that
Z\cap W=\varnothing
, and let
Z\oplus W
denote the topological sum of
Z
and
W
. Consider the equivalence relation
\equiv
on
Z\oplus W
obtained by identifying
x
with
h(x)
for every
x\in E
. Recall that
S\subseteq Z\oplus W
is
saturated
with respect to
\equiv
if
x,y\in Z\oplus W
and
x\in S
implies
y\in S
. We will denote by
Q
the corresponding quotient space. Furthermore, to avoid cumbersome notation, we will identify points of
Z\oplus W
with their equivalence class.

Claim 1.
The quotient topology on
Q
has a countable base consisting of clopen sets.

Proof of Claim 1.
Throughout the proof of this claim, we will assume to have fixed a compatible metric on
Z\oplus W
. Since
Z
is zero-dimensional and
E
is closed, it is possible to fix a countable base
\mathcal{B}
for
Z\setminus E
consisting of clopen subsets of
Z
. Similarly, fix a countable base
\mathcal{C}
for
W\setminus F
consisting of clopen subsets of
W
. Now fix a countable dense subset
D
of
E
. Given
z\in D
and
n\in\omega
, using the fact that disjoint closed sets in a zero-dimensional space can be separated by a clopen set, it is easy to obtain
U_{z,n},U^{\prime}_{z,n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(Z)
and
V_{z,n},V^{\prime}_{z,n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(W)
that satisfy the following requirements:

•

z\in U_{z,n}\subseteq\mathsf{B}(z,2^{-n})\cap Z
,

•

h(z)\in V_{z,n}\subseteq\mathsf{B}(h(z),2^{-n})\cap W
,

•

U^{\prime}_{z,n}\cap E=h^{-1}[V_{z,n}\cap F]
,

•

V^{\prime}_{z,n}\cap F=h[U_{z,n}\cap E]
.

It is straightforward to check that

O_{z,n}=(U_{z,n}\cap U^{\prime}_{z,n})\cup(V_{z,n}\cap V^{\prime}_{z,n})

is a clopen subset of
Z\oplus W
which is saturated with respect to
\equiv
. Using this fact, it is easy to realize that
\{O_{z,n}:z\in D,n\in\omega\}\cup\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}
corresponds in the obvious way to a base for
Q
consisting of clopen sets. Since this base is clearly countable, this concludes the proof.
\blacksquare

Using Claim 1, by a standard argument (see
[
En
, Theorem 6.2.12]
), we can assume without loss of generality that
Q\subseteq 2^{\omega}
. Set
K=\mathsf{cl}(Q)
, where we use
\mathsf{cl}
to denote closure in
2^{\omega}
. Throughout the remainder of this proof, we will use
\mathsf{d}
to denote a compatible metric on
K
.

Claim 2.
There exist non-empty
U_{n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(Z)
and non-empty
V_{n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(W)
for
n\in\omega
that satisfy the following conditions:

(1)

U_{m}\cap U_{n}=\varnothing
and
V_{m}\cap V_{n}=\varnothing
whenever
m\neq n
,

(2)

\bigcup_{n\in\omega}U_{n}=Z\setminus E
and
\bigcup_{n\in\omega}V_{n}=W\setminus F
,

(3)

\mathsf{d}(U_{n},E)\to 0
and
\mathsf{d}(V_{n},F)\to 0
,

(4)

\mathsf{diam}(U_{n})\to 0
and
\mathsf{diam}(V_{n})\to 0
.

Proof of Claim 2.
First assume that
K=Q
. Since
E
and
F
are non-empty, it is possible to find
U_{n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(Z)
and
V_{n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(W)
that satisfy conditions
(1)
and
(2)
. Using compactness, one sees that condition
(3)
will be automatically verified for any such choice. On the other hand, condition
(4)
can be obtained by further partitioning each
U_{n}
and
V_{n}
into (finitely many) clopen sets of sufficiently small diameter and arbitrarily re-enumerating. For the general case, apply the same argument with
\mathsf{cl}(Z)
in place of
Z
,
\mathsf{cl}(W)
in place of
W
, and
\mathsf{cl}(E)=\mathsf{cl}(F)
in place of
E=F
. Then consider the traces on
Z
and
W
respectively of the clopen sets obtained this way.
\blacksquare

Pick
a_{n}\in U_{n}
and
b_{n}\in V_{n}
for
n\in\omega
. Using conditions
(3)
and
(4)
, it is straightforward to verify that the sequences
(a_{n}:n\in\omega)
and
(b_{n}:n\in\omega)
satisfy the hypothesis of Lemma
8.3
with
Z=Q
and
S=E=F
. Therefore, there exists a bijection
\pi:\omega\longrightarrow\omega
such that
\mathsf{d}(a_{n},b_{\pi(n)})\to 0
. Set
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{n}:n\in\omega\}
and
\mathcal{V}=\{V_{n}:n\in\omega\}
. Define
\psi:\mathcal{U}\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}
by setting
\psi(U_{n})=V_{\pi(n)}
for
n\in\omega
. The following claim will clearly conclude the proof.

Claim 3.

(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V},\psi)
is a
\mathsf{KR}
-cover for
(Z\setminus E,W\setminus F,h)
.

Proof of Claim 3.
Let
f:Z\longrightarrow W
be a bijection that extends
h
and respects
\psi
. We need to show that
f
is continuous on
E
and
f^{-1}
is continuous on
F
. Since these proofs are similar, we will only give details for the first statement. So fix
x\in E
, and let
(x_{k}:k\in\omega)
be a sequence that converges to
x
in
Z
. Set
y=f(x)=h(x)
. We will show that the sequence
(f(x_{k}):k\in\omega)
converges to
y
in
W
.

Since
h
is continuous, we can assume without loss of generality that
x_{k}\notin E
for every
k\in\omega
. Define
n_{k}\in\omega
to be the unique index such that
x_{k}\in U_{n_{k}}
, and notice that
f(x_{k})\in V_{\pi(n_{k})}
because
f
respects
\psi
. It is clear that

\mathsf{d}(f(x_{k}),y)\leq\mathsf{d}(f(x_{k}),b_{\pi(n_{k})})+\mathsf{d}(b_{\pi(n_{k})},a_{n_{k}})+\mathsf{d}(a_{n_{k}},y).

Since
x_{k}
converges to
x
, it follows from condition
(4)
that
a_{n_{k}}
converges to
x=y
, hence
\mathsf{d}(a_{n_{k}},y)\to 0
. Similarly, one sees that
\mathsf{d}(f(x_{k}),b_{\pi(n_{k})})\to 0
. In conclusion, all three summands on the right-hand side of the above inequality tend to
0
, which establishes the claim.
\blacksquare

∎

The following lemma is inspired by a result of von Neumann
[
vN
, pages 11-12]
and by the simplified proofs given in
[
Hal
]
and
[
Yo
]
.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
Z
be a metric space with distance
\mathsf{d}
, let
(a_{n}:n\in\omega)
and
(b_{n}:n\in\omega)
be sequences in
Z
, and let
S\subseteq Z
be a non-empty set. Assume that the following conditions hold:

•

S\subseteq\mathsf{cl}(\{a_{n}:n\in\omega\})\cap\mathsf{cl}(\{b_{n}:n\in\omega\})
,

•

\mathsf{d}(a_{n},S)\to 0
and
\mathsf{d}(b_{n},S)\to 0
.

Then there exists a bijection
\pi:\omega\longrightarrow\omega
such that
\mathsf{d}(a_{n},b_{\pi(n)})\to 0
.

Proof.

We will recursively construct bijections
\pi_{k}
between finite subsets of
\omega
for
k\in\omega
. In the end, set
\pi=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}\pi_{k}
. Start by setting
\pi_{0}=\varnothing
. Now assume that
\pi_{k}
is given for some
k\in\omega
, then construct
n
and
m
as follows.

•

If
k
is even, let
n
be the minimal element of
\omega\setminus\mathsf{dom}(\pi_{k})
, then let
m
be the minimal element of
\omega\setminus\mathsf{ran}(\pi_{k})
such that
\mathsf{d}(b_{m},a_{n})<2^{-n}+\mathsf{d}(a_{n},S)
.

•

If
k
is odd, let
m
be the minimal element of
\omega\setminus\mathsf{ran}(\pi_{k})
, then let
n
be the minimal element of
\omega\setminus\mathsf{dom}(\pi_{k})
such that
\mathsf{d}(a_{n},b_{m})<2^{-m}+\mathsf{d}(b_{m},S)
.

To conclude the construction, set

\pi_{k+1}=\pi_{k}\cup\{(n,m)\}.

It is clear that
\pi:\omega\longrightarrow\omega
is a bijection. To see that
\mathsf{d}(a_{n},b_{\pi(n)})\to 0
, fix
\varepsilon>0
. Since
\pi
is a bijection, it is possible to fix
N\in\omega
such that
2^{-\pi(n)}<\varepsilon/2
and
\mathsf{d}(b_{\pi(n)},S)<\varepsilon/2
whenever
n\geq N
. Without loss of generality, also assume that
2^{-n}<\varepsilon/2
and
\mathsf{d}(a_{n},S)<\varepsilon/2
whenever
n\geq N
. It is straightforward to verify that
d(a_{n},b_{\pi(n)})<\varepsilon
whenever
n\geq N
.
∎

8.2.
Knaster-Reichbach systems

The purpose of this section is to show that a coherent sequence of
\mathsf{KR}
-covers yields a homeomorphism in a natural way. What we mean by “coherent” is made precise by the following definition, which first appeared in
[
Me3
, Section 6]
in the case
Z=W=2^{\omega}
.

Definition 8.4
.

Let
Z
and
W
be metric spaces. We will say that a sequence
((h_{n},\mathcal{K}_{n}):n\in\omega)
is a
Knaster-Reichbach system
(
\mathsf{KR}
-system
) for
(Z,W)
if the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

E_{n}
and
F_{n}
are closed nowhere dense in
Z
and
W
respectively for each
n
,

(2)

h_{n}:E_{n}\longrightarrow F_{n}
is a homeomorphism for each
n
,

(3)

h_{m}\subseteq h_{n}
whenever
m\leq n
,

(4)

\mathcal{K}_{n}=(\mathcal{U}_{n},\mathcal{V}_{n},\psi_{n})
is a
\mathsf{KR}
-cover for
(Z\setminus E_{n},W\setminus F_{n},h_{n})
for each
n
,

(5)

\mathsf{mesh}(\mathcal{U}_{n})\leq 2^{-n}
and
\mathsf{mesh}(\mathcal{V}_{n})\leq 2^{-n}
for each
n
,

(6)

\mathcal{U}_{m}
refines
\mathcal{U}_{n}
and
\mathcal{V}_{m}
refines
\mathcal{V}_{n}
whenever
m\geq n
,

(7)

If
U\in\mathcal{U}_{m}
and
U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
with
m\geq n
then (
U\subseteq U^{\prime}
iff
\psi_{m}(U)\subseteq\psi_{n}(U^{\prime})
).

The following results first appeared as
[
Me3
, Theorem 6.1 and Corollary 6.2]
in the case
Z=W=2^{\omega}
.

Theorem 8.5
.

Let
Z
and
W
be complete metric spaces. If
((h_{n},\mathcal{K}_{n}):n\in\omega)
is a
\mathsf{KR}
-system for
(Z,W)
then there exists a homeomorphism
h:Z\longrightarrow W
such that
h\supseteq\bigcup_{n\in\omega}h_{n}
.

Proof.

Assume that
((h_{n},\mathcal{K}_{n}):n\in\omega)
is a
\mathsf{KR}
-system for
(Z,W)
. Throughout this proof, we will use the notation of Definition
8.4
. Set
E=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}E_{n}
and
F=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}F_{n}
. Given
x\in Z\setminus E
and
n\in\omega
, denote by
U_{n}^{x}
the unique element of
\mathcal{U}_{n}
that contains
x
. Similarly, given
y\in W\setminus F
and
n\in\omega
, denote by
V_{n}^{y}
the unique element of
\mathcal{V}_{n}
that contains
y
.

Now pick
x\in Z
. If
x\in E_{n}
for some
n\in\omega
, define
h(x)=h_{n}(x)
. Notice that this does not depend on the choice of
n
by condition
(3)
. Now assume that
x\in Z\setminus E
. Using conditions
(5)
,
(6)
and
(7)
, one sees that
(\psi_{n}(U_{n}^{x}):n\in\omega)
is a decreasing sequence of closed subsets of
W
with vanishing diameter. Since
W
is complete, it follows that there exists
y\in W
such that
\bigcap_{n\in\omega}\psi_{n}(U_{n}^{x})=\{y\}
. Set
h(x)=y
. This completes the definition of
h
. Similarly, define
h^{\prime}:W\longrightarrow Z
by setting
h^{\prime}(y)=h_{n}^{-1}(y)
if
y\in F_{n}
for some
n\in\omega
, and
h^{\prime}(y)=x
if
y\in W\setminus F
, where
x\in Z
is such that
\bigcap_{n\in\omega}\psi_{n}^{-1}(V_{n}^{y})=\{x\}
. It is easy to check that
h^{\prime}\circ h=\mathsf{id}_{Z}
and
h\circ h^{\prime}=\mathsf{id}_{W}
, hence
h
is a bijection.

It is straightforward to verify that
h
respects
\psi_{n}
for each
n
. Therefore, by the definition of
\mathsf{KR}
-cover,
h
is continuous on
E
and
h^{-1}
is continuous on
F
. It remains to show that
h
is continuous on
Z\setminus E
and that
h^{-1}
is continuous on
W\setminus F
. Since these proofs are similar, we will only give the first one. Pick
x\in Z\setminus E
, and set
y=h(x)
. Pick a neighborhood
V
of
y
in
W
. By condition
(5)
, there exists
n\in\omega
such that
V_{n}^{y}\subseteq V
. It easy to realize that
h[U_{n}^{x}]=V_{n}^{y}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 8.6
.

Let
Z
and
W
be complete metric spaces, let
X\subseteq Z
, and let
Y\subseteq W
. Assume that
((h_{n},\mathcal{K}_{n}):n\in\omega)
is a
\mathsf{KR}
-system for
(Z,W)
, where each
h_{n}:E_{n}\longrightarrow F_{n}
, and the following additional conditions are satisfied:

(7)

X\subseteq\bigcup_{n\in\omega}E_{n}
,

(8)

Y\subseteq\bigcup_{n\in\omega}F_{n}
,

(9)

h_{n}[X\cap E_{n}]=Y\cap F_{n}
for each
n
.

Then there exists a homeomorphism
h:Z\longrightarrow W
such that
h\supseteq\bigcup_{n\in\omega}h_{n}
and
h[X]=Y
.

8.3.
Reasonably closed classes and a lemma of Harrington

The goal of this section is to discuss an ingenious result of Harrington, which is a key ingredient in the proof Theorem
8.9
. The original statement of Lemma
8.8
is
[
St1
, Lemma 3]
,
1
1

1

Steel credits this result to Harrington at the end of
[
St1
, Section 2]
.
and it requires certain determinacy assumptions. However, it is easy to realize that these assumptions are only used at the very beginning of the proof,
2
2

2

More specifically, they are used to deduce
\mathbf{\Gamma}=B\!\!\downarrow\,
from the assumption that
B\in\mathbf{\Gamma}\setminus\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
(see Lemma
3.3
). Instead, we will directly assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}=B\!\!\downarrow\,
.
and that the determinacy-free version of this result given below actually holds. In any case, we include a complete proof, taken from
[
AM
, Lemma 3.2]
.

We begin with some preliminaries concerning reasonably closed Wadge classes. The exposition is based on
[
vE3
, page 80]
. Given
i\in 2
, set

Q_{i}=\{x\in 2^{\omega}:x(n)=i\text{ for all but finitely many }n\in\omega\}.

Notice that every element of
2^{\omega}\setminus(Q_{0}\cup Q_{1})
is obtained by alternating finite blocks of zeros and finite blocks of ones. Define the function
\phi:2^{\omega}\setminus(Q_{0}\cup Q_{1})\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
by setting

\phi(x)(n)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\textrm{if the $n^{\text{th}}$ block of zeros of $x$ has even length},\\
1&\textrm{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

where we start counting with the
0^{\text{th}}
block of zeros. It is easy to check that
\phi
is continuous.

Definition 8.7
.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}
be a Wadge class in
2^{\omega}
. We will say that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is
reasonably closed
if
\phi^{-1}[A]\cup Q_{0}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
for every
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
.

Lemma 8.8

(Harrington)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}
be a reasonably closed Wadge class in
2^{\omega}
, and let
A,B\subseteq 2^{\omega}
. Assume that
A\leq B
in
2^{\omega}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}=B\!\!\downarrow\,
. Then there exists an injective continuous function
f:2^{\omega}\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
such that
A=f^{-1}[B]
.

Proof.

Let
A^{\ast}=\phi^{-1}[A]\cup Q_{0}
. Notice that
A^{\ast}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
because
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is reasonably closed. Therefore, by Proposition
2.1
, we can fix a monotone and eventually growing
\sigma:2^{<\omega}\longrightarrow 2^{<\omega}
such that
f_{\sigma}
witnesses
A^{\ast}\leq B
. We will recursively construct a monotone and eventually growing
\tau:2^{<\omega}\longrightarrow 2^{<\omega}
such that
f_{\tau}
witnesses
A\leq A^{\ast}
and
f_{\sigma}\circ f_{\tau}
is injective. By Proposition
2.1
, this will conclude the proof.

During the construction, we will make sure that the following conditions are satisfied for every
s\in 2^{<\omega}
:

(1)

\tau(s)
ends with a
1
,

(2)

There are exactly
|s|
blocks of zeros in
\tau(s)
,

(3)

s(n)
is the parity of the
n^{\textsf{th}}
block of zeros in
\tau(s)
for every
n<|s|
,

(4)

\sigma(\tau(s^{\frown}0))\neq\sigma(\tau(s^{\frown}1))
.

Notice that conditions
(1)
and
(2)
guarantee that
f_{\tau}[2^{\omega}]\subseteq 2^{\omega}\setminus(Q_{0}\cup Q_{1})
. Furthermore,
\phi(f_{\tau}(x))=x
for every
x\in 2^{\omega}
by conditions
(1)
and
(3)
. This shows that
f_{\tau}
will witness
A\leq A^{\ast}
. Finally, condition
(4)
will guarantee that
f_{\sigma}\circ f_{\tau}
is injective.

Begin the construction by setting
\tau(\varnothing)=(1)
. Now assume that
\tau(s)
has been constructed for a given
s\in 2^{<\omega}
. Given
i\in 2
and
k\leq\omega
, we will denote by
(i)^{k}
the sequence of length
k
with constant value
i
. Notice that
\tau(s)^{\frown}(0)^{\omega}\in A^{\ast}
and
\tau(s)^{\frown}(1)^{\omega}\notin A^{\ast}
. Therefore, since
f_{\sigma}
witnesses that
A^{\ast}\leq B
, we must have
f_{\sigma}\left(\tau(s)^{\frown}(0)^{\omega}\right)\in B
and
f_{\sigma}\left(\tau(s)^{\frown}(1)^{\omega}\right)\notin B
. This means that we can find
k\in\omega
such that

\sigma\left(\tau(s)^{\frown}(0)^{k}\right)\neq\sigma\left(\tau(s)^{\frown}(1)^{k}\right).

In particular, condition
(4)
will be satisfied for any choice of
\tau(s^{\frown}i)\supseteq\tau(s)^{\frown}(i)^{k}
for
i\in 2
. To conclude the construction, simply make choices that ensure that conditions
(1)
-
(3)
will be satisfied as well.
∎

8.4.
A theorem of Steel

The following result first appeared as
[
St1
, Theorem 2]
, in a slightly different form, and it will play a crucial role in the proof of the uniqueness theorem. Loosely speaking, it allows one to make the very considerable leap from “having the same complexity” to “being homeomorphic.” We remark that, thanks to the observations made in the previous section, we will be able to state this theorem in a way that does not require any determinacy assumptions.

Theorem 8.9

(Steel)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}
be a reasonably closed Wadge class in
2^{\omega}
. Assume that
X,Y\subseteq 2^{\omega}
satisfy the following requirements:

•

(X\cap\mathsf{N}_{s})\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
(Y\cap\mathsf{N}_{s})\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}
for every
s\in 2^{<\omega}
,

•

X
and
Y
are either both meager in
2^{\omega}
or both comeager in
2^{\omega}
.

Then there exists a homeomorphism
h:2^{\omega}\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
such that
h[X]=Y
.

Proof.

Fix a complete compatible metric on
2^{\omega}
. First, we will prove the theorem in the case in which
X
and
Y
are meager in
2^{\omega}
. So assume that there exist closed nowhere dense subsets
K_{n}
and
L_{n}
of
2^{\omega}
for
n\in\omega
such that
X\subseteq\bigcup_{n\in\omega}K_{n}
and
Y\subseteq\bigcup_{n\in\omega}L_{n}
, where
K_{0}=L_{0}=\varnothing
.

The plan is to recursively construct a
\mathsf{KR}
-system
((h_{n},\mathcal{K}_{n}):n\in\omega)
for
(2^{\omega},2^{\omega})
, where
h_{n}:E_{n}\longrightarrow F_{n}
and
\mathcal{K}_{n}=(\mathcal{U}_{n},\mathcal{V}_{n},\psi_{n})
. In addition to conditions
(1)
-
(6)
of Definition
8.4
, we will make sure that the following ones are satisfied for each
n
:

(7)

K_{n}\subseteq E_{n}
,

(8)

L_{n}\subseteq F_{n}
,

(9)

h_{n}[X\cap E_{n}]=Y\cap F_{n}
.

In the end, apply Corollary
8.6
to obtain the desired homeomorphism
h:2^{\omega}\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
.

To begin the construction, set
E_{0}=F_{0}=\varnothing
and
h_{0}=\varnothing
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{0}=\mathcal{V}_{0}
be a partition of
2^{\omega}
consisting of clopen sets of diameter at most
1
, then set
\mathcal{K}_{0}=(\mathcal{U}_{0},\mathcal{V}_{0},\psi_{0})
, where
\psi_{0}:\mathcal{U}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}_{0}
is the identity. Now assume that
E_{m}
,
F_{m}
,
h_{m}
and
\mathcal{K}_{m}=(\mathcal{U}_{m},\mathcal{V}_{m},\psi_{m})
have been constructed for
m\leq n
so that conditions
(1)
-
(9)
are satisfied. For every given
U\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
, apply Lemma
8.10
to
U
and
V=\psi_{n}(U)
, with
K=K_{n}
and
L=L_{n}
. This will yield closed nowhere dense subsets
E_{U}
and
F_{U}
of
U
and
V
respectively, and a homeomorphism
h_{U}:E_{U}\longrightarrow F_{U}
such that the following conditions are satisfied:

•

K_{n}\cap U\subseteq E_{U}
,

•

L_{n}\cap V\subseteq F_{U}
,

•

h_{U}[X\cap E_{U}]=Y\cap F_{U}
.

By Lemma
8.2
, for every
U\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
we can fix a
\mathsf{KR}
-cover
(\mathcal{U}_{U},\mathcal{V}_{U},\psi_{U})
for
(U,V,h_{U})
. By taking suitable refinements if necessary, can assume without loss of generality that
\mathsf{mesh}(\mathcal{U}_{U})\leq 2^{-(n+1)}
and
\mathsf{mesh}(\mathcal{V}_{U})\leq 2^{-(n+1)}
for each
U
. To complete the construction, make the following definitions:

•

E_{n+1}=E_{n}\cup\bigcup\{E_{U}:U\in\mathcal{U}_{n}\}
,

•

F_{n+1}=F_{n}\cup\bigcup\{F_{U}:U\in\mathcal{U}_{n}\}
,

•

h_{n+1}=h_{n}\cup\bigcup\{h_{U}:U\in\mathcal{U}_{n}\}
,

•

\mathcal{U}_{n+1}=\bigcup\{\mathcal{U}_{U}:U\in\mathcal{U}_{n}\}
,

•

\mathcal{V}_{n+1}=\bigcup\{\mathcal{V}_{U}:U\in\mathcal{U}_{n}\}
,

•

\psi_{n+1}=\bigcup\{\psi_{U}:U\in\mathcal{U}_{n}\}
,

•

\mathcal{K}_{n+1}=(\mathcal{U}_{n+1},\mathcal{V}_{n+1},\psi_{n+1})
.

It is straightforward to check that all the desired requirements will be verified.

Finally, assume that
X
and
Y
are both comeager in
2^{\omega}
. Using Lemma
3.7
, it is easy to see that
(2^{\omega}\setminus X)\cap\mathsf{N}_{s}\!\!\downarrow\,=\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
for every
s\in 2^{<\omega}
. Similarly, one sees that
(2^{\omega}\setminus Y)\cap\mathsf{N}_{s}\!\!\downarrow\,=\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
for every
s\in 2^{<\omega}
. Furthermore, it is clear that Lemma
8.10
applies to
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
as well as
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. In conclusion, the argument given above can be applied to
2^{\omega}\setminus X
and
2^{\omega}\setminus Y
, yielding the desired result.
∎

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}
be a Wadge class in
2^{\omega}
. Assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
or
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
is reasonably closed, and that
U,V,K,L,X,Y\subseteq 2^{\omega}
satisfy the following requirements:

•

U
and
V
are non-empty clopen subsets of
2^{\omega}
,

•

K
and
L
are closed nowhere dense subsets of
2^{\omega}
,

•

(X\cap\mathsf{N}_{s})\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
(Y\cap\mathsf{N}_{s})\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}
for every
s\in 2^{<\omega}
.

Then there exist closed nowhere dense subspaces
E
and
F
of
U
and
V
respectively, and a homeomorphism
h:E\longrightarrow F
satisfying the following conditions:

•

K\cap U\subseteq E
,

•

L\cap V\subseteq F
,

•

h[X\cap E]=Y\cap F
.

Proof.

Pick
s,t\in 2^{<\omega}
such that
\mathsf{N}_{s}\subseteq U
and
\mathsf{N}_{t}\subseteq V
. Since
K
and
L
are nowhere dense, we can also assume that
K\cap\mathsf{N}_{s}=\varnothing
and
L\cap\mathsf{N}_{t}=\varnothing
. Observe that the conclusion of Lemma
8.8
would still hold under the assumption that
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
is reasonably closed. Therefore, since

\mathbf{\Gamma}=X\!\!\downarrow\,=(X\cap\mathsf{N}_{s})\!\!\downarrow\,=Y\!\!\downarrow\,=(Y\cap\mathsf{N}_{t})\!\!\downarrow\,,

we can fix continuous injections
f:2^{\omega}\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
and
g:2^{\omega}\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
witnessing that
X\leq Y\cap\mathsf{N}_{t}
and
Y\leq X\cap\mathsf{N}_{s}
respectively. Notice that
f
and
g
are closed embeddings by compactness.

Since neither
\{\varnothing\}
nor
\{2^{\omega}\}
is reasonably closed, it is clear that both
X
and
Y
must be dense in
2^{\omega}
. By continuity, it follows that
f[2^{\omega}]\subseteq\mathsf{N}_{t}
and
g[2^{\omega}]\subseteq\mathsf{N}_{s}
. Make the following definitions:

•

E=(K\cap U)\cup g[L\cap V]
,

•

F=f[K\cap U]\cup(L\cap V)
,

•

h=(f\upharpoonright(K\cap U))\cup(g^{-1}\upharpoonright g[L\cap V])
.

Using the fact that
f
and
g
are closed embeddings, it is easy to check that
f[K\cap U]
and
g[L\cap V]
are closed nowhere dense in
2^{\omega}
. In order to see that
h:E\longrightarrow F
is a homeomorphism, simply observe that
K\cap U
and
g[L\cap V]
are separated by the clopen set
\mathsf{N}_{s}
, while
f[K\cap U]
and
L\cap V
are separated by the clopen set
\mathsf{N}_{t}
. The verification that all other requirements are satisfied is straightforward.
∎

Chapter 9
Zero-dimensional homogeneous spaces

This chapter contains the classification promised in the title. The notion of exact complexity of a zero-dimensional space will enable us to give natural-sounding statements of our results. A fundamental step along the way (which was originally achieved in
[
CMM1
]
) consists in showing that every zero-dimensional homogeneous space of sufficiently high complexity has exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
for some good Wadge class
\mathbf{\Gamma}
(see Theorem
9.7
). Since good Wadge classes are reasonably closed (see Lemma
9.8
), this will allow us to apply Steel’s Theorem
8.9
. The crucial assumption for the existence theorems is that of closure under
\mathsf{SU}_{1}
, as given by the results of Section
7.5
. Since our existence proofs naturally produce semifilters, we will be able to show that the zero-dimensional homogeneous spaces are topologically indistinguishable from semifilters (with the trivial exception of locally compact spaces).

9.1.
The complexity of a zero-dimensional space

In this section, we will discuss how Wadge classes can be used to describe the
absolute
topological complexity of a zero-dimensional space (as opposed to its
relative
complexity as a subset of some ambient space).
1
1

1

A substantial portion of this discussion could be generalized to arbitrary (that is, not necessarily zero-dimensional) spaces. However, as this would introduce several technical difficulties (and it will not be needed in the remainder of the paper), we will focus exclusively on zero-dimensional spaces.
In the spirit of Definition
3.9
, our canonical choice of zero-dimensional Polish space will be
\omega^{\omega}
.

Definition 9.1
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a topological pointclass, and let
X
be a zero-dimensional space. We will say that
X
is a
\mathbf{\Sigma}
space
(or
X
has
complexity

\mathbf{\Sigma}
) if there exists an embedding
j:X\longrightarrow\omega^{\omega}
such that
j[X]\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
.

Given
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}(\omega^{\omega})
, as we have already observed in Section
3.2
, the assignment obtained by setting
\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)=\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
for every space
Z
yields a topological pointclass. In this case, which is the most important case for our purposes, we will simply talk about
\mathbf{\Gamma}
spaces. Another example that is very relevant in the present context is obtained by setting
\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)=\Delta(\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(Z)))
for every space
Z
. In this case, we will simply talk about
\Delta(\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}))
spaces. By setting
\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)=\mathsf{Borel}(Z)
(respectively
\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)=\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}(Z)
or
\mathbf{\Sigma}(Z)=\mathbf{\Pi}^{1}_{n}(Z)
, where
1\leq n<\omega
) for every space
Z
, one obtains the usual notion of Borel (respectively
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}
or
\mathbf{\Pi}^{1}_{n}
) space.

Definition 9.2
.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}
be a good Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
, and let
X
be a zero-dimensional space. We will say that
X
has
exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
if there exists an embedding
j:X\longrightarrow\omega^{\omega}
such that
j[X]\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}
.

As an unpleasant example, consider
X=\omega^{\omega}
: it is easy to realize that there exist embeddings
i,j:X\longrightarrow\omega^{\omega}
such that
i[X]\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1}(\omega^{\omega})
and
j[X]\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{2}(\omega^{\omega})
. So, if we dropped “good” from Definition
9.2
, there would be spaces with more than one exact complexity, which is highly undesirable. In fact, the point of the following results is that the above notions are well-behaved if we restrict the attention to good Wadge classes. Proposition
9.3
is ultimately inspired by
[
vE3
, Lemma 4.2.16]
.

Proposition 9.3
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
be a good Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
, and let
X
be a zero-dimensional space. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

X
is a
\mathbf{\Gamma}
space,

(2)

j[X]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
for every zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
and embedding
j:X\longrightarrow Z
,

(3)

j[X]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
for some zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
and embedding
j:X\longrightarrow Z
.

Proof.

In order to prove the implication
(1)\rightarrow(2)
, fix an embedding
i:X\longrightarrow\omega^{\omega}
such that
i[X]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Now pick a zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
and an embedding
j:X\longrightarrow Z
. Set
h=j\circ i^{-1}
, and observe that
h:i[X]\longrightarrow j[X]
is a homeomorphism. Therefore, by
[
Ke
, Theorem 3.9]
, we can fix
G\in\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{2}(\omega^{\omega})
,
H\in\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{2}(Z)
and a homeomorphism
\widetilde{h}:G\longrightarrow H
such that
h\subseteq\widetilde{h}
. Since
i[X]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(G)
by Lemma
3.12
, it is clear that
j[X]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(H)
. A further application of Lemma
3.12
yields
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
such that
A\cap H=j[X]
. Since
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is closed under intersection with
\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{2}
sets by Lemma
7.10
and Corollary
7.13
, it follows that
j[X]=A\cap H\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
.

The implication
(2)\rightarrow(3)
is clear. In order to prove the implication
(3)\rightarrow(1)
, fix a zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
and an embedding
i:X\longrightarrow Z
such that
i[X]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
. Let
j:Z\longrightarrow\omega^{\omega}
be an embedding, and observe that
j[Z]\in\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{2}(\omega^{\omega})
by
[
Ke
, Theorem 3.11]
. Furthermore, it is clear that
j[i[X]]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(j[Z])
because
j:Z\longrightarrow j[Z]
is a homeomorphism. Therefore, by Lemma
3.12
, there exists
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
such that
A\cap j[Z]=j[i[X]]
. Since
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under intersection with
\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{2}
sets by Corollary
7.13
, it follows that
j[i[X]]=(j\circ i)[X]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
.
∎

Corollary 9.4
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
be a good Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
, and let
X
be a zero-dimensional space. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

X
has exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
,

(2)

X
is a
\mathbf{\Gamma}
space but not a
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
space,

(3)

j[X]\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
for every zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
and embedding
j:X\longrightarrow Z
,

(4)

j[X]\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
for some uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
and embedding
j:X\longrightarrow Z
.

Proof.

We will only prove the implication
(4)\rightarrow(1)
, in order to make clear where the uncountability assumption is used. The rest of the proof is left to the reader. So fix an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
and an embedding
i:X\longrightarrow Z
such that
i[X]\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
. Let
j:Z\longrightarrow\omega^{\omega}
be an embedding. By Lemma
3.3
, it will be enough to show that
(j\circ i)[X]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}\setminus\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
. The fact that
(j\circ i)[X]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
is clear by Proposition
9.3
. Now assume, in order to get a contradiction, that
(j\circ i)[X]\in\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
. Then
i[X]\in\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}(Z)
by Proposition
9.3
, which shows that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is selfdual. Since
Z
is uncountable, this contradicts Theorem
3.13
.
∎

We conclude this section by showing that complexity is hereditary with respect to sufficiently simple subspaces.

Proposition 9.5
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
be a good Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
, and let
X
be a zero-dimensional
\mathbf{\Gamma}
space. Then every subspace
Y
of
X
such that
Y\in\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{2}(X)
is a
\mathbf{\Gamma}
space.

Proof.

Fix an embedding
j:X\longrightarrow\omega^{\omega}
such that
j[X]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Pick
Y\in\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{2}(X)
. Let
\widetilde{Y}\in\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{2}(\omega^{\omega})
be such that
\widetilde{Y}\cap j[X]=j[Y]
. It follows from Corollary
7.13
that
j[Y]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

9.2.
Homogeneous spaces have good complexity

The main result of this section is that every zero-dimensional homogeneous space of sufficiently high complexity has exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
for some good Wadge class
\mathbf{\Gamma}
(see Theorem
9.7
). The key application of homogeneity appears in the proof of the following lemma, which will be useful in Sections
9.3
and
9.5
as well. Lemma
9.6
and Theorem
9.7
are essentially the same as
[
CMM1
, Lemma 14.2 and Theorem 14.4]
respectively (the difference is that the statements given here are level-by-level, and they use the notions of complexity introduced in Section
9.1
).

Lemma 9.6

(Carroy, Medini, Müller)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
be a good Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
, and let
X
be a zero-dimensional homogeneous space. If
X
has a non-empty open
\mathbf{\Gamma}
subspace then
X
is a
\mathbf{\Gamma}
space.

Proof.

Assume without loss of generality that
X
is a subspace of
\omega^{\omega}
. Let
A
be a non-empty open
\mathbf{\Gamma}
subspace of
X
. Define

\mathcal{U}=\{h[A]:h\text{ is a homeomorphism of }X\}.

Notice that
\mathcal{U}
is a cover of
X
because
X
is homogeneous and
A
is non-empty. Let
\{A_{n}:n\in\omega\}
be a countable subcover of
\mathcal{U}
, and observe that each
A_{n}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
by Proposition
9.3
. Fix
U_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(\omega^{\omega})
for
n\in\omega
such that each
U_{n}\cap X=A_{n}
. Set

V_{-1}=\omega^{\omega}\setminus\bigcup_{k\in\omega}U_{k},\text{ and }V_{n}=U_{n}\setminus\bigcup_{k<n}U_{k}

for
n\in\omega
. Notice that
V_{n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(\omega^{\omega})
whenever
-1\leq n<\omega
, and that
V_{m}\cap V_{n}=\varnothing
whenever
-1\leq m,n<\omega
and
m\neq n
. Furthermore, it is easy to check that

X=\bigcup_{-1\leq n<\omega}(A_{n}\cap V_{n}),

where
A_{-1}=\varnothing
. In conclusion, we have shown that
X\in\mathsf{PU}_{1}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
. Since
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq 1
, it follows that
X\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
.
∎

Theorem 9.7

(Carroy, Medini, Müller)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
X
be a zero-dimensional homogeneous
\mathbf{\Sigma}
space that is not a
\Delta(\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}))
space. Then there exists a good Wadge class
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
such that
X
has exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

Start by fixing an embedding
i:X\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
. Notice that
X
must be crowded, otherwise
X
would be discrete by homogeneity. Therefore, by taking the closure of
i[X]
in
2^{\omega}
and applying
[
Ke
, Theorem 7.4]
, we can assume without loss of generality that
i
is a dense embedding. Observe that
i[X]
must be codense in
2^{\omega}
, otherwise
X
would be locally compact by homogeneity. So we can fix a countable dense subset
D
of
2^{\omega}
such that
D\cap i[X]=\varnothing
. Since
2^{\omega}\setminus D\approx\omega^{\omega}
by
[
Ke
, Theorem 7.7]
, this means that there exists a dense embedding
j:X\longrightarrow\omega^{\omega}
. Furthermore, since
X
is a
\mathbf{\Sigma}
space and
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is a nice topological pointclass, we must have
j[X]\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
. For the sake of simplicity, from now on, we will actually assume that
X\in\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
and that
X
is dense in
\omega^{\omega}
.

By Theorem
3.4
, it is possible to fix a
\subseteq
-minimal
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
such that
X\cap U\in\mathbf{\Gamma}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
for some non-empty
U\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(\omega^{\omega})
. Fix a non-empty
U\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(\omega^{\omega})
such that
X\cap U\in\mathbf{\Gamma}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
. We will assume that
X\cap U\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
, since the case
X\cap U\in\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
is similar. Clearly, the following two claims will conclude the proof.

Claim 1.

\mathbf{\Gamma}
is a good Wadge class.

Proof.
By applying Lemma
9.6
to the good Wadge class
\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(\omega^{\omega}))
and its dual, one sees that
X\cap U\notin\Delta(\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(\omega^{\omega})))
, otherwise the assumption that
X
is not a
\Delta(\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}))
space would be contradicted by Proposition
9.3
. By Lemma
3.3
, it follows that
\Delta(\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(\omega^{\omega})))\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
. It remains to show that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq 1
. Assume, in order to get a contradiction, that
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=0
. Observe that this implies
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}(U))=0
by Theorem
3.20
. Also notice that
X
must be codense in
\omega^{\omega}
, otherwise
X
would be Polish by homogeneity. Since
X\cap U\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(U)
by Lemma
3.12
, it follows from Lemma
3.22
that there exists a non-empty
V\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(U)
and
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
such that
\mathbf{\Lambda}(U)\subsetneq\mathbf{\Gamma}(U)
and
X\cap V\in\mathbf{\Lambda}(U)
. Observe that
\mathbf{\Lambda}\subsetneq\mathbf{\Gamma}
by Theorem
3.14
. Therefore, in order to contradict the minimality of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
, it will be enough to show that
X\cap V\in\mathbf{\Lambda}
. By Lemma
3.12
, there exists
A\in\mathbf{\Lambda}
such that
A\cap U=X\cap V
. Notice that
\mathbf{\Lambda}\neq\{\omega^{\omega}\}
because
X
is codense in
\omega^{\omega}
. Therefore
X\cap V=A\cap U\in\mathbf{\Lambda}
by Lemma
3.7
.
\blacksquare

Claim 2.

X\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}
.

Proof.
By Claim 1, it is possible to apply Lemma
9.6
, which yields
X\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
by Proposition
9.3
. This means that
X\!\!\downarrow\,\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
. To conclude the proof, it will be enough to show that
X
is non-selfdual, since the desired result will then follow from the minimality of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Assume, in order to get a contradiction, that
X
is selfdual. Then, by Theorem
3.6
, there exist a non-empty
V\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(\omega^{\omega})
and a non-selfdual
A<X
in
\omega^{\omega}
such that
A\cap V=X\cap V
. By the same argument used at the end of the proof of Claim 1, this contradicts the minimality of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
.
\blacksquare

∎

9.3.
Uniqueness

In this section we will show that, up to homeomorphism, there exists at most one zero-dimensional homogeneous space of a given exact complexity and Baire category. In other words, for every good Wadge class
\mathbf{\Gamma}
in
\omega^{\omega}
, there exist at most two zero-dimensional homogeneous spaces of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
: one meager, and one Baire.
2
2

2

Recall that, by Proposition
2.21
, every homogeneous space is either meager or Baire.
This is the uniqueness part of the classification. Theorem
9.9
is essentially the same as
[
CMM1
, Theorem 15.2]
(the difference is that the statement given here is level-by-level, and it uses the notions of complexity introduced in Section
9.1
).

We decided to state this result in the following form (that is, in terms of embeddings into
2^{\omega}
) for two reasons. First, this will allow us to derive Corollary
9.10
, which will be useful in the proofs of Corollary
9.17
and Theorem
9.19
. Second, Theorem
9.9
shows that, given a zero-dimensional homogeneous space
X
of sufficiently high complexity, there is essentially a unique way to embed
X
into
2^{\omega}
densely, which seems to be of independent interest.

We begin with a simple technical result, which will allow us to apply Steel’s Theorem
8.9
. Lemma
9.8
first appeared as
[
CMM1
, Lemma 13.2]
.

Lemma 9.8

(Carroy, Medini, Müller)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. If
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(2^{\omega})
is a good Wadge class in
2^{\omega}
then
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is reasonably closed.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega})\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(2^{\omega})
be a good Wadge class, where
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Set
Z=2^{\omega}\setminus(Q_{0}\cup Q_{1})
, and let
\phi:Z\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
be the function defined in Section
8.3
. Pick
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega})
. Notice that
\phi^{-1}[A]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
by Lemma
3.11
. Therefore, by Lemma
3.12
, there exists
B\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega})
such that
B\cap Z=\phi^{-1}[A]
. But
\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega})
is good, hence
\phi^{-1}[A]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega})
by Corollary
7.13
. Finally, a further application of Corollary
7.13
shows that
\phi^{-1}[A]\cup Q_{0}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega})
, as desired.
∎

Theorem 9.9

(Carroy, Medini, Müller)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
be a good Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
, and let
X
and
Y
be zero-dimensional homogeneous spaces that satisfy the following conditions:

•

X
and
Y
both have exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
,

•

X
and
Y
are either both meager spaces or both Baire spaces.

Then
X
and
Y
are homeomorphic. More precisely, if
i:X\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
and
j:Y\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
are dense embeddings, then there exists a homeomorphism
h:2^{\omega}\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
such that
h[i[X]]=j[Y]
.

Proof.

Let
i:X\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
and
j:Y\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
be dense embeddings. Observe that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega})
is a good Wadge class by Lemma
7.10
, hence it is reasonably closed by Lemma
9.8
. Therefore, by Theorem
8.9
, it will be enough to prove the following three claims.

Claim 1.

(i[X]\cap\mathsf{N}_{s})\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega})
for every
s\in 2^{<\omega}
.

Proof of Claim 1.
Pick
s\in 2^{<\omega}
. Since
i[X]
is clearly a
\mathbf{\Gamma}
space, an application of Proposition
9.5
shows that
i[X]\cap\mathsf{N}_{s}
is a
\mathbf{\Gamma}
space. Furthermore, since
i[X]
is not a
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
space by Corollary
9.4
, it follows from Lemma
9.6
that
i[X]\cap\mathsf{N}_{s}
is a not a
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
space. A further application of Corollary
9.4
concludes the proof.
\blacksquare

Claim 2.

(j[Y]\cap\mathsf{N}_{s})\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega})
for every
s\in 2^{<\omega}
.

Proof of Claim 2.
Proceed as in the proof of Claim 1.
\blacksquare

Claim 3.

i[X]
and
j[Y]
are either both meager in
2^{\omega}
or both comeager in
2^{\omega}
.

Proof of Claim 3.
If
X
and
Y
are both meager spaces, then
i[X]
and
j[Y]
will both be meager subsets of
2^{\omega}
by density. If
X
and
Y
are both Baire spaces, then
i[X]
and
j[Y]
will both be comeager subsets of
2^{\omega}
by Corollary
2.8
.
\blacksquare

∎

Corollary 9.10
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
be a good Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
, and let
X
be a dense homogeneous subspace of
2^{\omega}
of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. If
U\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(2^{\omega})
is non-empty, then there exists a homeomorphism
h_{U}:2^{\omega}\longrightarrow U
such that
h_{U}[X]=X\cap U
. In particular, both
X
and
2^{\omega}\setminus X
are strongly homogeneous.

Proof.

Pick a non-empty
U\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(2^{\omega})
. Set
Y=X\cap U
, and observe that
Y
is a zero-dimensional homogeneous space. It is clear that if
X
is a meager space then
Y
is a meager space, and that if
X
is a Baire space then
Y
is Baire space. Notice that
Y
is a
\mathbf{\Gamma}
space by Proposition
9.5
. On the other hand, if
Y
were a
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
space, then
X
would be a
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
space by Lemma
9.6
, contradicting Corollary
9.4
. Therefore, by a further application of Corollary
9.4
, one sees that
Y
has exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. In conclusion, since
U\approx 2^{\omega}
, the desired homeomorphism is given by Theorem
9.9
. The strong homogeneity of
X
and
2^{\omega}\setminus X
then follows from Lemma
2.22
.
∎

9.4.
Existence: the positive results

In this section, we will show that certain assumptions on the level, type, and separation property of a good Wadge class
\mathbf{\Gamma}
permit the construction of a zero-dimensional homogeneous space of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Our method of proof will naturally yield semifilters (recall that these are homogeneous by Corollary
2.18
).

While the proof of Theorem
9.13
is essentially the same as the proof of
[
Me4
, Theorem 12.4]
(which in turn is essentially due to van Engelen), we have isolated more clearly the key closure property of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
needed for it to go through. We begin with two technical results.

Lemma 9.11
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
Z
be a zero-dimensional Polish space, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(Z)
be closed under intersections with
\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1}
sets and under unions with
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}
sets. Assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}=A\!\!\downarrow\,
and
B\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
is such that
A
is a closed subset of
B
. Then
\mathbf{\Gamma}=B\!\!\downarrow\,
.

Proof.

We will use
\mathsf{cl}
to denote closure in
Z
. Assume, in order to get a contradiction, that
A\nleq B
. Then
Z\setminus B\leq A
by Lemma
3.3
. By the closure properties of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
, it follows that

Z\setminus A=((Z\setminus B)\cap\mathsf{cl}(A))\cup(Z\setminus\mathsf{cl}(A))\in\mathbf{\Gamma},

which contradicts the fact that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is non-selfdual.
∎

Lemma 9.12
.

Let
Z
be a space, let
B\subseteq Z
, let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
be closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{1}
, and let
A_{n}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
for
n\in\omega
. If each
A_{n}
is a closed subset of
B
and
\bigcup_{n\in\omega}A_{n}=B
then
B\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

We will use
\mathsf{cl}
to denote closure in
Z
. Assume that each
A_{n}\in\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1}(B)
. Set

U_{n}=\mathsf{cl}(A_{n})\setminus\bigcup_{k<n}\mathsf{cl}(A_{k})

for
n\in\omega
, and observe that each
U_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(Z)
. It is straightforward to check that

B=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(A_{n}\cap U_{n}).

Since
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{1}
, it follows that
B\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
.
∎

Theorem 9.13
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
be a good Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
that is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{1}
. Then there exists a meager semifilter of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

We begin by making the following definitions:

•

\Omega=2^{<\omega}
,

•

Z=2^{\Omega}
,

•

K=\{\{x\upharpoonright n:n\in\omega\}:x\in 2^{\omega}\}
,

•

\widehat{S}=\{z\in Z:z\subseteq z^{\prime}\text{ for some }z^{\prime}\in S\}
whenever
S\subseteq K
,

•

\ddot{S}=\{z\in\widehat{S}:z\text{ is infinite}\}
whenever
S\subseteq K
,

•

Z_{e}=\{z\in Z:z\cap e=\varnothing\}
whenever
e\in\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)
.

Observe that both
K
and
\widehat{K}
are compact and crowded. Furthermore, it is easy to check that
\ddot{K}\in\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{2}(Z)
, and it is clear that each
Z_{e}\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(Z)
. We will use
\mathsf{cl}
to denote closure in
Z
. Fix
X\subseteq K
such that
X\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}(K)
, then set
X^{\prime}=X\cup(K\setminus\mathsf{cl}(X))
.

Claim 1.

X^{\prime}\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}(K)
.

Proof of Claim 1.
Notice that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(K)
is a good Wadge class by Lemma
7.10
. Therefore, using Lemma
7.11
, one sees that
X^{\prime}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(K)
. An application of Lemma
9.11
concludes the proof.
\blacksquare

By Claim 1, we can assume without loss of generality that
X
is dense in
K
. Notice that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)=X\!\!\downarrow\,
by Corollary
9.4
. Furthermore, Lemma
7.10
shows that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is a good Wadge class. Set
Y=\widehat{X}
. Throughout the remainder of this proof, we will use
\leq
exclusively to denote Wadge-reduction in
Z
.

Claim 2.

Y\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
.

Proof of Claim 2.
Consider the unique function
\varphi:\ddot{K}\longrightarrow K
such that
x\subseteq\varphi(x)
for every
x\in\ddot{K}
, and observe that
\varphi
is continuous. By Lemma
3.11
, it follows that
\ddot{X}=\varphi^{-1}[X]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(\ddot{K})
. Therefore, by Lemma
3.12
, there exists
\widetilde{X}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
such that
\ddot{X}=\widetilde{X}\cap\ddot{K}
. Finally, Corollary
7.13
ensures that

Y=(\widetilde{X}\cap\ddot{K})\cup(\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)\cap Y)\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z),

as desired.
\blacksquare

Define

\mathcal{R}=\{y\cup e:y\in Y\text{ and }e\in\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)\},

and observe that
\mathcal{R}
is a semiideal on
\Omega
. To conclude the proof, it will be enough to show that
\mathcal{R}
has exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
and that
\mathcal{R}
is meager. The first statement will follow from Claim 3 and Corollary
9.4
, while the second statement is the content of Claim 4.

Claim 3.

\mathcal{R}\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
.

Proof of Claim 3.
Define

\mathcal{R}_{e}=\mathcal{R}\cap\widehat{K}\cap Z_{e}

for
e\in\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)
, and observe that each
\mathcal{R}_{e}
is a closed subset of
\mathcal{R}
. It is easy to realize that
Y\cap\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)=\widehat{K}\cap\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)
by the density of
X
in
K
. Using this fact, one sees that each
\mathcal{R}_{e}=Y\cap Z_{e}
. Therefore, each
\mathcal{R}_{e}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
by Claim 2 and Lemma
3.7
.

Define
\psi_{e}:Z\longrightarrow Z
for
e\in\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)
by setting
\psi_{e}(x)=x\cup e
for
x\in Z
. It is clear that each
\psi_{e}
is continuous, hence closed by compactness. Since each
\psi_{e}\upharpoonright Z_{e}
is injective, it is an embedding by compactness. In particular, each
\psi_{e}[\mathcal{R}_{e}]\approx\mathcal{R}_{e}
. Furthermore, each
\psi_{e}\upharpoonright\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{R}\longrightarrow\mathcal{R}
is closed by Lemma
2.2
. Let
\{A_{n}:n\in\omega\}
be an enumeration of

\{\psi_{e}[\mathcal{R}_{e}]:e\in\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)\}.

Observe that each
A_{n}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
by Proposition
9.3
, and that each
A_{n}
is a closed subset of
\mathcal{R}
. Furthermore, it is clear that
\mathcal{R}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}A_{n}
. Since
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{1}
by Lemma
7.4
, it follows from Lemma
9.12
that
\mathcal{R}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
. To complete the proof, simply observe that
\mathcal{R}\cap K=X
, then apply Lemmas
7.11
and
9.11
.
\blacksquare

Claim 4.

\mathcal{R}
is meager.

Proof of Claim 4.
Notice that
\mathcal{R}
is either meager or comeager by Proposition
2.11
. Assume, in order to get a contradiction, that
\mathcal{R}
is comeager. Then, as in the proof of Corollary
2.12
, there exist
x,y\in\mathcal{R}
such that
x\cup y=\Omega
. It is easy to realize that this contradicts the fact that
\widehat{K}
has the finite union property.
\blacksquare

∎

Corollary 9.14
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
be a good Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
that satisfies at least one of the following conditions:

(1)

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq 2
,

(2)

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=1
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}
does not have the separation property,

(3)

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=1
and
t(\mathbf{\Gamma})=3
.

Then there exists a meager semifilter of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
9.13
, it will be enough to show that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{1}
. If condition
(1)
holds, this follows from the definition of level, since
\mathsf{SU}_{1}(\mathbf{\Gamma})\subseteq\mathsf{PU}_{2}(\mathbf{\Gamma})
. If condition
(2)
holds, apply Theorem
7.19
. Finally, if condition
(3)
holds, apply Theorem
7.20
.
∎

Corollary 9.15
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathsf{b}\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
be a good Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
that satisfies at least one of the following conditions:

(1)

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq 2
,

(2)

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=1
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the separation property,

(3)

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=1
and
t(\mathbf{\Gamma})=3
.

Then there exists a Baire semifilter of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

The key observation is that Corollary
9.14
applies to
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
. This is straightforward to check, except for condition
(2)
, which requires Theorem
5.8
. So there exists a meager semifilter
\mathcal{S}
of exact complexity
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
. Using Corollary
9.4
, it is easy to realize that
\mathcal{T}=c[2^{\omega}\setminus\mathcal{S}]
is a Baire semifilter of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
.
∎

9.5.
Existence: the negative results

Next, we will prove that the results of Section
9.4
are sharp. More precisely, as Theorem
9.16
and Corollary
9.17
show, there are no other zero-dimensional homogeneous spaces whose exact complexity is a good Wadge class. Theorem
9.16
is based on
[
vE3
, Lemma 4.3.7]
.

Theorem 9.16
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
be a good Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
that satisfies the following conditions:

•

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=1
,

•

t(\mathbf{\Gamma})\in\{1,2\}
,

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}
does not have the separation property.

Then every zero-dimensional homogeneous space of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is meager.

Proof.

Let
X
be a zero-dimensional homogeneous space of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Assume without loss of generality that
X
is a dense subspace of
2^{\omega}
. Notice that
X\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega})
by Corollary
9.4
. By Lemma
6.8
and Theorem
6.11
, it is possible to find
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\in\mathsf{NSD}(2^{\omega})
for
n\in\omega
such that each
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n})\geq 1
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega})=\mathsf{SU}_{1}(\mathbf{\Delta})
, where

\mathbf{\Delta}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{n}\cup\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{n}).

Fix
A_{n}\in\mathbf{\Delta}
and pairwise disjoint
U_{n}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(2^{\omega})
for
n\in\omega
such that

X=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}(A_{n}\cap U_{n}).

Let
C_{n,k}\in\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1}(2^{\omega})
for
n,k\in\omega
be such that each
U_{n}=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}C_{n,k}
. Observe that each
A_{n}\cap C_{n,k}\in\mathbf{\Delta}
by Lemma
3.19
.

To conclude the proof, it will be enough to show that each
A_{n}\cap C_{n,k}=X\cap C_{n,k}
is closed nowhere dense in
X
. Assume, in order to get a contradiction, that
X\cap V\subseteq X\cap C_{n,k}
for some non-empty
V\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{1}(2^{\omega})
and
n,k\in\omega
. Using Lemma
3.7
, one sees that
X\cap V=A_{n}\cap C_{n,k}\cap V\in\mathbf{\Delta}
. Notice that
\mathbf{\Delta}\subseteq\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}(2^{\omega})
because
\mathbf{\Delta}\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega})
and
\mathbf{\Delta}
is selfdual. It follows from Proposition
9.3
that
X\cap V
is a
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
space, hence
X
is a
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
space by Lemma
9.6
. This contradicts Corollary
9.4
.
∎

Corollary 9.17
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
be a good Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
that satisfies the following conditions:

•

\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=1
,

•

t(\mathbf{\Gamma})\in\{1,2\}
,

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the separation property.

Then every zero-dimensional homogeneous space of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is Baire.

Proof.

Let
X
be a zero-dimensional homogeneous space of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Assume without loss of generality that
X
is a dense subspace of
2^{\omega}
. Set
