Y=2^{\omega}\setminus X
, and observe that
Y
is homogeneous by Corollaries
2.16
and
9.10
. Furthermore, using Corollary
9.4
, one sees that
Y
has exact complexity
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
. Since
\widecheck{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
does not have the separation property by Theorem
5.8
, it follows from Theorem
9.16
that
Y
is a meager space, hence a meager subset of
2^{\omega}
by density. In conclusion, being a comeager subspace of
2^{\omega}
, the space
X
is Baire.
∎

9.6.
The classification of the zero-dimensional homogeneous spaces

As the following theorem shows, the previous three sections can be combined to yield a complete classification of the zero-dimensional homogeneous spaces of sufficiently high complexity under
\mathsf{AD}
. This classification is complete because, by Theorem
9.7
, a zero-dimensional homogeneous space that is not
\mathbf{\Delta}(\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}))
has exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
for some good Wadge class
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. The case
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathsf{Borel}
is due to van Engelen (see
[
vE3
, Theorem 4.4.4]
), although his results are expressed using the more complicated language of
[
Lo1
]
.

Theorem 9.18
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
be a good Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
.

•

If either
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq 2
or
(\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=1\text{ and }t(\mathbf{\Gamma})=3)
then, up to homeomorphism, there exist exactly two zero-dimensional homogeneous spaces of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. One of these spaces is Baire, and the other is meager.

•

If
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=1
,
t(\mathbf{\Gamma})\in\{1,2\}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}
does not have the separation property then, up to homeomorphism, there exists exactly one zero-dimensional homogeneous space of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. This space is meager.

•

If
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=1
,
t(\mathbf{\Gamma})\in\{1,2\}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}
has the separation property then, up to homeomorphism, there exists exactly one zero-dimensional homogeneous space of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. This space is Baire.

Proof.

The existence claims follow from Corollaries
9.14
and
9.15
, since every semifilter is homogeneous by Corollary
2.18
. The constraints on Baire category follow from Theorem
9.16
and Corollary
9.17
. Finally, the claims about uniqueness up to homeomorphism follow from Proposition
2.21
and Theorem
9.9
.
∎

9.7.
A purely topological characterization of semifilters

The following “self-strengthening” result is a natural byproduct of our classification. In particular, the equivalence
(1)\leftrightarrow(3)
can be viewed as a purely topological characterization of semifilters. In Section
10.3
, we will give an analogous characterization of filters. Theorem
9.19
simultaneously generalizes
[
vE3
, Corollary 4.4.6]
(which gives
(1)\leftrightarrow(2)
in the case
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathsf{Borel}
),
[
Me4
, Theorem 1.1]
(which gives
(1)\leftrightarrow(3)
in the case
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathsf{Borel}
), and
[
CMM1
, Theorem 1.1]
(which gives
(1)\leftrightarrow(2)
in the case
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathcal{P}
). Furthermore, it answers
[
Me4
, Question 13.4]
in the affirmative.

Theorem 9.19
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Assume that
X
is a zero-dimensional
\mathbf{\Sigma}
space that is not locally compact. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

X
is homogeneous,

(2)

X
is strongly homogeneous,

(3)

X
is homeomorphic to a semifilter.

Proof.

The implication
(2)\rightarrow(1)
is given by Corollary
2.16
, while the implication
(3)\rightarrow(1)
is given by Corollary
2.18
. Next, assume that
X
is a
\mathbf{\Delta}(\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}))
space. For the implication
(1)\rightarrow(2)
, see
[
vE1
, Corollary 8.4]
or
[
vE3
, Theorems 3.4.13 and 3.6.2]
. For the implication
(1)\rightarrow(3)
, see
[
Me4
, Theorem 8.4]
.

From now on, assume that
(1)
holds and that
X
is not a
\mathbf{\Delta}(\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}))
space. By Theorem
9.7
, there exists a good Wadge class
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
such that
X
has exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. It follows from Corollary
9.10
that
(2)
holds. Finally, we will show that
(3)
holds. If
X
is meager then, using Corollary
9.17
, one sees that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
must satisfy the requirement of Corollary
9.14
, which guarantees the existence of a meager semifilter of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. By a similar argument, if
X
is Baire then Corollary
9.15
and Theorem
9.16
yield a Baire semifilter of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. In either case, an application of Theorem
9.9
will conclude the proof.
∎

Chapter 10
Filters

In this chapter, we will build on the results of the previous one to classify filters up to homeomorphism (see Theorem
10.4
). As a byproduct, we will give natural topological properties (originally obtained by van Engelen in
[
vE5
]
for the Borel case) that describe exactly when a zero-dimensional space is homeomorphic to a filter (see Theorem
10.5
). In addition to closure under
\mathsf{SU}_{1}
and the properties of good Wadge classes, we will make use of the notion of closure under squares. We will conclude the chapter by discussing the relationship between filters and topological groups.

10.1.
Closure under squares

We begin by discussing a useful closure property, which first appeared in
[
vE5
]
in the case
Z=2^{\omega}
, under the name of “closure under products.”

Definition 10.1
.

Let
Z
be a space, let
2\leq n<\omega
, and let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega^{\omega})
. We will say that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is
closed under
n
-th powers
if
A^{n}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z^{n})
for every
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
. When
n=2
, we will simply say that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is
closed under squares
.

Proposition 10.2
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

There exists an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
such that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is closed under squares,

(2)

\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is closed under squares for every zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
,

(3)

There exists an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
such that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is closed under
n
-th powers whenever
2\leq n<\omega
,

(4)

\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is closed under
n
-th powers whenever
Z
is a zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
and
2\leq n<\omega
.

Proof.

To see that
(1)\rightarrow(2)
, fix an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
such that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is closed under squares. Let
W
be a zero-dimensional Polish space and pick
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
. Since
Z
is uncountable, we can assume without loss of generality that
W
is a subspace of
Z
. By Lemma
3.12
, there exists
\widetilde{A}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
such that
A=\widetilde{A}\cap W
. Since
A\times A=(\widetilde{A}\times\widetilde{A})\cap(W\times W)
and
\widetilde{A}\times\widetilde{A}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
, a further application of Lemma
3.12
shows that
A\times A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
, as desired.

The proof of the implication
(3)\rightarrow(4)
is similar to the one that we have just given, so we will omit it. The implications
(2)\rightarrow(1)
and
(4)\rightarrow(3)\rightarrow(1)
are trivial. To conclude the proof, we will show that
(1)\rightarrow(3)
. So fix an uncountable zero-dimensional Polish space
Z
such that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is closed under squares. Let
2\leq n<\omega
and
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
. Assume without loss of generality that
A\neq\varnothing
, and fix
x\in A
. Pick
k\in\omega
such that
2^{k}\geq n
. Notice that
A^{2^{k}}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z^{2^{k}})
because
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is closed under squares. Set

W=\left\{z\in Z^{2^{k}}:z(i)=x\text{ whenever }n\leq i<2^{k}\right\},

and observe that
A^{2^{k}}\cap W\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(W)
by Lemma
3.12
. Define
h:Z^{n}\longrightarrow W
by setting
h(z_{0},\ldots,z_{n-1})=(z_{0},\ldots,z_{n-1},x,\ldots,x)
, and observe that
h
is a homeomorphism. Therefore

A^{n}=h^{-1}\left[A^{2^{k}}\cap W\right]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z^{n})

by Lemma
3.11
, as desired.
∎

10.2.
The topological classification of filters

In this section, we will give the promised classification of filters up to homeomorphism (see Theorem
10.4
). Naturally, this classification will build on the results that we already have. The new existence result that we will need is Theorem
10.3
, whose proof is taken almost verbatim from the proof of
[
vE5
, Lemma 3.3]
. However, as in the statement of Theorem
9.13
, we have isolated more clearly the required closure properties. More importantly, in the proof of Theorem
10.4
, we will use the results of Section
7.5
.

Theorem 10.3
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
be a good Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
that is closed under squares and closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{1}
. Then there exists a filter of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

The setup of this proof is exactly the same as the setup of the proof of Theorem
9.13
. We repeat it here for convenience, but we will not be as thorough with some of the subsequent details. Recall the following definitions:

•

\Omega=2^{<\omega}
,

•

Z=2^{\Omega}
,

•

K=\{\{x\upharpoonright n:n\in\omega\}:x\in 2^{\omega}\}
,

•

\widehat{S}=\{z\in Z:z\subseteq z^{\prime}\text{ for some }z^{\prime}\in S\}
whenever
S\subseteq K
,

•

\ddot{S}=\{z\in\widehat{S}:z\text{ is infinite}\}
whenever
S\subseteq K
,

•

Z_{e}=\{z\in Z:z\cap e=\varnothing\}
whenever
e\in\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)
.

Fix
X\subseteq K
such that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(K)=X\!\!\downarrow\,
, and assume without loss of generality that
X
is dense in
K
. Notice that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)=X\!\!\downarrow\,
, and that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
is a good Wadge class. Set
Y=\widehat{X}
. For the remainder of this proof, we will use
\leq
exclusively to denote Wadge-reduction in
Z
.

Claim 1.

Y\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
.

Proof of Claim 1.
This is proved like Claim 2 in the proof of Theorem
9.13
.
\blacksquare

Given
1\leq n<\omega
,
s\in\Omega
and
\vec{s}\in\Omega^{n}
, we will use the following notation:

•

Define
\phi_{n}:(2^{\Omega})^{n}\longrightarrow 2^{\Omega}
by setting
\phi_{n}(z_{0},\ldots,z_{n-1})=z_{0}\cup\cdots\cup z_{n-1}
,

•

M_{n}=\{\vec{s}\in\Omega^{n}:|\vec{s}(i)|=|\vec{s}(j)|\text{ for all }i,j<n\text{ and }\vec{s}(i)\neq\vec{s}(j)\text{ if }i\neq j\}
,

•

F_{s}=\{z\in\widehat{K}:s\subseteq t\text{ for every }t\in z\}
,

•

F_{\vec{s}}=\prod_{i<n}F_{\vec{s}(i)}
.

Observe that each
\phi_{n}
is continuous, and that each
F_{s}
is compact. Set

Y^{\prime}=\bigcup_{1\leq n<\omega}\bigcup_{\vec{s}\in M_{n}}\phi_{n}[F_{\vec{s}}\cap Y^{n}],

then define

\mathcal{I}=\{y\cup e:y\in Y^{\prime}\text{ and }e\in\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)\}.

Claim 2.

\mathcal{I}
is an ideal on
\Omega
.

Proof of Claim 2.
It is trivial to check that
\varnothing\in\mathcal{I}
, while
\Omega\notin\mathcal{I}
because
K
has the finite union property. Next, we will show that
\mathcal{I}
is downward-closed. Define
\theta:\ddot{K}\longrightarrow 2^{\omega}
by setting
\theta(z)=\bigcup z
for
z\in\ddot{K}
. Pick

z=y_{0}\cup\cdots\cup y_{n-1}\cup e,

where
1\leq n<\omega
,
(y_{0},\ldots,y_{n-1})\in F_{\vec{s}}\cap Y^{n}
for some
\vec{s}\in M_{n}
, and
e\in\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)
. Let
z^{\prime}\subseteq z
, and assume without loss of generality that
z^{\prime}\notin\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)
. Write

z^{\prime}=y^{\prime}_{0}\cup\cdots\cup y^{\prime}_{m-1}\cup e^{\prime},

where
1\leq m\leq n
, each
y^{\prime}_{i}\in\ddot{X}
,
\theta(y^{\prime}_{i})\neq\theta(y^{\prime}_{j})
whenever
i\neq j
, and
e^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)
. In particular, it is possible to find
k\in\omega
big enough so that
\theta(y_{i})\upharpoonright k\neq\theta(y_{j})\upharpoonright k
whenever
i\neq j
. Define
\vec{t}:m\longrightarrow\Omega
by setting
\vec{t}(i)=\theta(y_{i})\upharpoonright k
for
i<m
, and observe that
\vec{t}\in M_{m}
. Notice that setting
y^{\prime\prime}_{i}=y^{\prime}_{i}\setminus 2^{<k}
for
i<m
yields
(y^{\prime\prime}_{0},\ldots,y^{\prime\prime}_{m-1})\in F_{\vec{t}}\cap Y^{n}
. Also set
e^{\prime\prime}=e^{\prime}\cup\bigcup_{i<m}(y^{\prime}_{i}\cap 2^{<k})
. It is clear that

z^{\prime}=y^{\prime\prime}_{0}\cup\cdots\cup y^{\prime\prime}_{m-1}\cup e^{\prime\prime},

which shows that
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}
, as desired. Using a similar argument, one sees that
\mathcal{I}
is closed under finite unions.
\blacksquare

By Claim 2, to conclude the proof, it will be enough to show that
\mathcal{I}
has exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. This will follow from Claim 5 and Corollary
9.4
. The following two claims will be used in the proof of Claim 5.

Claim 3.

\phi_{n}[F_{\vec{s}}\cap Y^{n}]
is closed in
\mathcal{I}
whenever
1\leq n<\omega
and
\vec{s}\in M_{n}
.

Proof of Claim 3.
Let
1\leq n<\omega
and
\vec{s}\in M_{n}
. It will be enough to show that

\phi_{n}[F_{\vec{s}}\cap Y^{n}]=\phi_{n}[F_{\vec{s}}]\cap\mathcal{I}.

The inclusion
\subseteq
is clear by the definitions of
Y^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{I}
. In order to prove the other inclusion, pick
\vec{x}\in F_{\vec{s}}
such that
\bigcup_{i<n}\vec{x}(i)\in\mathcal{I}
. We will show that each
\vec{x}(i)\in Y
. So pick
i<n
. Since
Y\cap\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)=\widehat{K}\cap\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)
by the density of
X
in
K
, we can assume without loss of generality that
\vec{x}(i)
is infinite. Let
1\leq m<\omega
,
\vec{t}\in M_{m}
,
\vec{y}\in F_{\vec{t}}\cap Y^{n}
and
e\in\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)
be such that

\bigcup_{j<n}\vec{x}(j)=\bigcup_{j<m}\vec{y}(j)\cup e.

Pick
j<m
such that
\vec{x}(i)\cap\vec{y}(j)
is infinite. Since
\vec{x}(i),\vec{y}(j)\in\widehat{K}
, this means that there exists
z\in K
such that
\vec{x}(i),\vec{y}(j)\subseteq z
. However, the fact that
\vec{y}(j)\in Y
implies that
z\in X
. Hence
\vec{x}(i)\in Y
as well.
\blacksquare

Claim 4.

\phi_{n}[F_{\vec{s}}\cap Y^{n}]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
whenever
1\leq n<\omega
and
\vec{s}\in M_{n}
.

Proof of Claim 4.
Begin by observing that
\phi_{n}\upharpoonright F_{\vec{s}}
is injective whenever
1\leq n<\omega
and
\vec{s}\in M_{n}
. Therefore, each
\phi_{n}\upharpoonright F_{\vec{s}}
is an embedding by compactness. Notice that
Y^{n}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z^{n})
whenever
1\leq n<\omega
by Claim 1 and Proposition
10.2
, since
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under squares. Furthermore, each
\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z^{n})
is a good Wadge class by Lemma
7.10
. It follows that each
F_{\vec{s}}\cap Y^{n}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z^{n})
by Lemma
7.11
, hence each
\phi_{n}[F_{\vec{s}}\cap Y^{n}]\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
by Proposition
9.3
.

Claim 5.

\mathcal{I}\!\!\downarrow\,=\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
.

Proof of Claim 5.
Define
\psi_{e}:Z\longrightarrow Z
for
e\in\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)
by setting
\psi_{e}(x)=x\cup e
for
x\in Z
. It is clear that each
\psi_{e}
is continuous, hence closed by compactness. Since each
\psi_{e}\upharpoonright Z_{e}
is injective, it is an embedding by compactness. Furthermore, each
\psi_{e}\upharpoonright\mathcal{I}:\mathcal{I}\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}
is closed by Lemma
2.2
. Let
\{A_{k}:k\in\omega\}
be an enumeration of

\{\psi_{e}[\phi_{n}[F_{\vec{s}}\cap Y^{n}]\cap Z_{e}]:1\leq n<\omega,\vec{s}\in M_{n}\text{ and }e\in\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega)\}.

Using Claim 4, Lemma
3.7
and Proposition
9.3
, one sees that each
A_{k}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
. Furthermore, each
A_{k}\in\mathbf{\Pi}^{0}_{1}(\mathcal{I})
by Claim 3. Since
\mathcal{I}=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}A_{k}
, it follows from Lemmas
7.4
and
9.12
that
\mathcal{I}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(Z)
. To complete the proof, simply observe that
\mathcal{I}\cap K=X
, then apply Lemmas
7.11
and
9.11
.
\blacksquare

∎

Now we have all the tools needed to completely classify filters up to homeomorphism. For the classification of filters that are
\Delta(\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}))
spaces, see
[
vE5
, Lemmas 2.9 and 3.3]
. On the other hand, by Theorem
9.7
, the following result takes care of every filter that is not a
\Delta(\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}))
space. We also remark that the filter of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
mentioned below is unique up to homeomorphism by Corollary
2.12
and Theorem
9.9
.

Theorem 10.4
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega})
be a good Wadge class in
\omega^{\omega}
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

There exists a filter of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
,

(2)

\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under squares and either
\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})\geq 2
,
(\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=1\text{ and }t(\mathbf{\Gamma})=3)
, or
(\ell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=1\text{ and }\mathbf{\Gamma}\text{ does not have the separation property})
.

Proof.

In order to prove the implication
(1)\rightarrow(2)
, let
\mathcal{F}
be a filter of exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Notice that
\mathcal{F}
is meager by Corollary
2.12
. Therefore, by Theorem
9.18
, it will be enough to show that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under squares. By Proposition
10.2
, it will be enough to show that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega})
is closed under squares. So pick
A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega})
, and observe that
A\leq\mathcal{F}
in
2^{\omega}
by Corollary
9.4
. This trivially implies that
A\times A\leq\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{F}
in
2^{\omega}\times 2^{\omega}
. Since
\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{F}\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega}\times 2^{\omega})
by Theorem
2.10
and Proposition
9.3
, it follows that
A\times A\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(2^{\omega}\times 2^{\omega})
, as desired. Now assume that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
satisfies condition
(2)
. As in the proof of Corollary
9.14
, one sees that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under
\mathsf{SU}_{1}
. Therefore, condition
(1)
holds by Theorem
10.3
.
∎

10.3.
A purely topological characterization of filters

As we discussed in Sections
2.3
and
2.4
, the combinatorial structure of filters imposes strong constraints on their topology. But is it possible to go the other way? In other words, are there natural conditions on a space
X
which will guarantee that
X
is homeomorphic to a filter? Van Engelen showed that, in the Borel context, this question has a very elegant positive answer (see
[
vE5
, Theorem 3.4]
). The following result shows that, under suitable determinacy assumptions, his characterization extends beyond the Borel realm.

Theorem 10.5
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
X
be a zero-dimensional
\mathbf{\Sigma}
space that is not locally compact. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

X
is homeomorphic to a filter,

(2)

X
is homogeneous, meager, and homeomorphic to
X^{2}
.

Proof.

The implication
(1)\rightarrow(2)
follows from the discussion in Section
2.3
, Theorem
2.10
, and Corollary
2.12
. In order to prove the implication
(2)\rightarrow(1)
, assume that condition
(2)
holds. If
X
is a
\Delta(\mathsf{D}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}))
space, then the desired result follows from
[
vE5
, Lemma 3.3]
, so assume that this is not the case. By Theorem
9.7
, there exists a good Wadge class
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{NSD}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\omega^{\omega})
such that
X
has exact complexity
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. By Theorems
9.18
and
10.4
, it will be enough to show that
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is closed under squares. To see this, proceed as in the proof of Theorem
10.4
.
∎

10.4.
Filters versus topological groups

As Theorem
9.19
shows, the lower-right portion of the diagram from Section
2.4
“collapses” under
\mathsf{AD}
. Therefore, it seems natural to ask whether the rest of the diagram could collapse as well. More precisely, one might ask: does
\mathsf{AD}
imply that every zero-dimensional topological group is homemorphic to a filter?

As usual, the trivial case of locally compact spaces provides several counterexamples (see the spaces mentioned by Proposition
2.14
). And even after excluding these spaces, the question remains too naive, as
\omega^{\omega}
is a zero-dimensional topological group that is not homeomorphic to a filter (by Corollary
2.12
). However, adding the requirement that the topological group is meager yields an open question. In fact, even the Borel version of this problem is still open. It was first formulated at the very end of
[
vE6
]
, where van Engelen solved the problem for spaces of low complexity (see
[
vE6
, Theorem 3.4]
). The precise statements are as follows.
1
1

1

We added the assumption “not locally compact” in the statement of Theorem
10.6
because our definition of filter is slightly more restrictive than that of van Engelen. However, this does not result in any loss of generality (see
[
MZ1
, Proposition 2]
).

Theorem 10.6

(van Engelen)
.

Let
X
be a zero-dimensional
\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{3}
space that is not locally compact. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

X
is a meager topological group,

•

X
is homeomorphic to a filter.

Conjecture 10.7

(van Engelen)
.

Theorem
10.6
holds for all zero-dimensional Borel spaces that are not locally compact.

While we were unable to make any progress on the above conjecture, we suspect that if it were true then the following stronger conjecture would be true as well.

Conjecture 10.8
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Then Theorem
10.6
holds for all zero-dimensional
\mathbf{\Sigma}
spaces that are not locally compact.

We remark that a proof of Conjecture
10.8
would yield a complete classification of the zero-dimensional topological groups under
\mathsf{AD}
. In fact, by Proposition
2.21
and the following well-known result, restricting the attention to meager topological groups does not result in any loss of generality. Therefore, the desired classification would follow from Theorem
10.4
and the remarks that precede it.

Proposition 10.9
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a nice topological pointclass, and assume that
\mathsf{Det}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(\omega^{\omega}))
holds. Let
X
be a zero-dimensional
\mathbf{\Sigma}
space that is not locally compact. If
X
is a Baire topological group then
X\approx\omega^{\omega}
.

Proof.

Assume that
X
is a Baire topological group. Since
X
is homogeneous and not locally compact, it is clear that no non-empty open subspace of
X
is compact. Therefore, by
[
Ke
, Theorem 7.7]
, it will be enough to show that
X
is Polish.

By
[
AT
, Theorem 3.6.10, Proposition 3.6.20 and Proposition 4.3.8]
, it is possible to find a Polish topological group
Z
such that
X
is a dense topological subgroup of
Z
. Assume, in order to get a contradiction, that
X\subsetneq Z
. Pick
z\in Z\setminus X
, and observe that the cosets
X
and
zX
are disjoint. On the other hand, Corollary
2.8
shows that both
X
and
zX
are comeager in
Z
, contradicting the fact that
Z
is Polish.
∎

However, Conjecture
10.8
would be false if we altogether dropped the determinacy assumption. In fact, working in
\mathsf{ZFC}
, it is a simple exercise to construct an uncountable zero-dimensional topological group
X
in which
2^{\omega}
does not embed. It is easy to realize that the space
\mathbb{Q}\times X
will be a meager topological group that is not homeomorphic to a filter.

We conclude this section by showing that, although the diagram from Section
2.4
might partially collapse, under no circumstance will a complete collapse happen. We will combine ideas from
[
Me4
, Proposition 5.4]
and
[
vE3
, Theorem 3.6.5]
.

Proposition 10.10
.

There exists a semifilter that is not a topological group.

Proof.

Fix infinite sets
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
such that
\Omega_{1}\cup\Omega_{2}=\omega
and
\Omega_{1}\cap\Omega_{2}=\varnothing
. Define

\mathcal{T}=\{x_{1}\cup x_{2}:x_{1}\subseteq\Omega_{1}\text{, }x_{2}\subseteq\Omega_{2}\text{, and }(x_{1}\notin\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega_{1})\text{ or }x_{2}\in\mathsf{Cof}(\Omega_{2}))\},

and observe that
\mathcal{T}
is a semifilter. Furthermore, it is clear that
\mathcal{T}
is the union of
X=\{x\subseteq\omega:x\cap\Omega_{1}\notin\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega_{1})\}
and
Y=\{x_{1}\cup x_{2}:x_{1}\in\mathsf{Fin}(\Omega_{1})\text{ and }x_{2}\in\mathsf{Cof}(\Omega_{2})\}
. Since
X
is Polish and
Y
is countable, it follows that
\mathcal{T}
is a Borel space (in fact, it is a
\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{3}
space).

Now assume, in order to get a contradiction, that
\mathcal{T}
is a topological group. Observe that
\mathcal{T}
is a Baire space because
X
is dense in
\mathcal{T}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{T}
is Polish by Proposition
10.9
. Since
\mathsf{Cof}(\Omega_{2})
is a countable closed subspace of
\mathcal{T}
that is crowded, this is contradiction.
∎

We remark that the semifilter
\mathcal{T}
described in the above proof is homeomorphic to the notable space
\mathbf{T}
introduced by van Douwen (see
[
vEvM
]
and
[
Me4
, Proposition 5.4]
). Since
\mathbb{Q}\times\mathbf{T}
is not a topological group by
[
vE4
, Corollary 4.3]
, and multiplication by
\mathbb{Q}
preserves the property of being homeomorphic to a semifilter by
[
Me4
, Lemma 8.2]
, this shows that that the example given by Proposition
10.10
could be made meager as well.
